PREFACE 

South America is our nearest neighbor among the continents. During recent years the increasing flow of visitors between our country and the countries of that continent, the constant necessity for the exchange of a variety of products, and the rapid development of air lines, in addition to the numerous steamship lines in existence, have all helped to bring us more and more closely in touch with the South American people and their problems.

We are dependent on South America for many things. 

Our fields are fertilized with Chilean nitrates. Most of the coffee and much of the chocolate and cocoa that we use come from South American countries. Some of the rubber for making our automobile tires, hospital supplies, and rainy-day apparel is shipped from South American ports. Products of the mines, farms, and ranches, as well as drugs and chemicals and other valuable products, are also exported to the United States in great quantities.

The people of South America, in turn, need our help in developing their resources, in establishing new industries, in building roads and railroads, and in financing great projects. They need our money, our skilled workmen, and our machinery and other manufactured products. Some of our corporations are spending millions of dollars in developing the mineral wealth of South America, and some of our great meat-packing com-

vi 

SOUTH AMERICA 

panies have immense plants there. These developments, together with the adoption of modern methods of work, have caused the rapid growth of cities and the expansion of industries, and have aided greatly in the opening up of large markets for our manufactures. Our South 

American commerce is a most valuable asset. 

The children in our schools, who are our business and professional men and women of the near future, should become more interested in and more intimately acquainted with the people, the countries, and the resources of South America. To help in this direction the old edition of this book has been largely rewritten. 

Old pictures have been replaced by new ones, facts and figures brought up to date, important matters of the present day stressed, and problems for each chapter, and other new features, added. 

Testing themselves on the work they have done gives pupils a firm foundation for future study. To this end, drills and tests are given at the close of each chapter. Pupils derive more benefit from any subject if it is seen in relation to other subjects. Hence exercises in history, arithmetic, and English all relating to the subject matter of the text are included. The notebooks containing lists of names important enough to be remembered allow pupils to exercise their judgment and are helpful in connection with drills, tests, and games. 

Because of the interesting facts to be learned from a study of the statistics in the Appendix of this book, the tables in the Appendix have been referred to throughout the text, and exercises on them have been included at the end.

NELLIE B. ALLEN

SOUTH AMERICA CHAPTER I  

The two continents in the Western Hemisphere are 

North America and South America. We may call them the twin continents, for in many ways they are as much alike as twin children often are. On the other hand, there are great differences between them, as is often the case with twin children.

You have studied your own continent of North America and know many things about it. Can you shut your eyes and see it as the map shows it? If you cannot see it clearly with your eyes closed, look at a map. Then shut your eyes again and see if your picture is clearer. 

Did you notice how wide North America is at the north and how it tapers to a very narrow strip of land where it joins South America? Now look at the map of South America opposite page 1. You see that the continent is wide at the north and tapers to a point at the south like North America. 

Do you remember the long Rocky Mountain System which stretches from north to south through the western part of North America, and the beautiful high peaks there with their snow-covered tops? The 1

SOUTH AMERICA 

picture on page 3 shows some of the high mountains in South America. They look much like those in North 

America, yet they are thousands of miles away from them. They belong to the Andes System, which stretches southward through the western part of South America just as the Rocky Mountain System extends through western North America. In the Andes are some of the grandest snow-capped ranges and highest peaks in the world. Even in the torrid zone some of the mountains are so high that their tops are covered with snow the year round. What color did the map-maker use to show the Andes Mountains on the regional map of 

South America?

The Rocky Mountains of North America and the 

Andes of South America are really one great system which reaches from Alaska in the north to the southern tip of South America. The lowest part of the system is in the Isthmus of Panama, where the mountains are so low that they may well be called hills. In building the Panama Canal across the Isthmus the engineers had to dig through these hills to connect the waters of the Atlantic and Pacific oceans.

In this great western highland of North and South America there are many volcanoes, and the Pacific coast of the two continents is one of the earthquake regions of the world. Many cities in western South 

America have been destroyed by earthquake shocks, and rumblings and shakings of the earth are very common there. Most of the people live in low, one-story houses. These are built in such a way that they sway and rock but seldom fall unless the shock is a severe one. 

In South America, as in North America, there are

mountains along the eastern coast of the continent. 

They are lower than those in the west, but they are beautiful, with their rounded tops, wooded slopes, and green valleys. Look at the regional map of South 

America and see what the eastern ranges are called. FIG. 1. These high mountains are in Peru. The building that you see is the highest station on any standard-gauge railroad in the world. It is 

15,610 feet above sea level. The railroad runs from the coast up into the mountains. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

How can you tell from the map that these mountains are lower and less rugged than the Andes Mountains? 

The Appalachian Highlands, in eastern North America, are a great storehouse of coal and iron. The Brazilian Highlands, in eastern South America, are also a treasure house. They are stored with gold and with diamonds and other gems more beautiful, perhaps, but less valuable than the minerals found in the Appalachians,

SOUTH AMERICA 

In North America you remember that between the highlands on the east and west are wide plains stretching through Canada and the United States. These plains are the most important region in that continent. 

You remember too the great fields of corn, wheat, and cotton there, and the thousands of cattle, sheep, and hogs that are found on the farms and ranches. 

How is it in South America? Look at the regional map again and notice the great stretches of plains between the mountains. By what color are they shown?

In later chapters you will read about these plains and the people who live on them. The level lands stretch for hundreds of miles north and south and east and west, and on them, as you can see by looking at Fig. 2, there is plenty of room for large cattle ranches, great flocks of sheep, and big farms where corn, wheat, and flax are raised. In other parts of the wide plains are wonderful tropical forests with their strange and interesting animals and plants. 

Perhaps in your study of North America you read about the ruins of ancient cities which have been found in Mexico and Central America. In western South

America also are ancient ruins left by people who lived on the Andean plateau long before white men even knew that there was such a continent as South America. 

In these ruins there are flights of rocky steps, giant columns, huge stones fitted so closely together that a knife cannot be wedged in between them, and stone terraces inclosing fields high on the mountain sides. 

All these tell us of ancient cities, important industries, and civilized peoples who lived and worked on the high 

Andean plateau many long years ago.

You have been reading about some of the ways in which the twin continents of North America and South America are alike. Now let us find out some of the ways in which they differ.

The most important differences between the two continents are due to the fact that the wider part of FIG. 2. This is part of a herd of cattle belonging to an Argentine ranchowner. There are many large cattle and sheep ranches on the wide plains of South America. Meat, hides and skins, and other animal products are important exports from the countries on the plains North America lies in the temperate zone, whereas the part of South America having the greatest width is in the torrid zone. The southern countries of North America lie in tropical regions, whereas the narrow southern end of South America stretches into the cold belt. 

Nature has been kinder to North America than to its southern neighbor in placing the greater part of its area in the zone best fitted for the growth of civilization and

SOUTH AMERICA 

the development of commerce and industry, and by giving it deeper inlets and thus more and better harbors through which to carry on trade. 

Because most of South America lies south of the equator, the seasons there are the opposite of ours. FIG. 3. Centuries before European nations knew anything about the two 

American continents, a powerful tribe of Indians lived on the high plateau of South America. Notice the size of the stones in this wall which some of them built, and the careful way in which the stones have been fitted together. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

You know that in winter the sun is far to the south of us and shines low in our southern sky at noonday, giving us but little heat. But at that time of the year it is high in the sky and giving summer heat to the people living south of the equator. It would seem strange to us to celebrate Christmas in the summer time.

In June and July in the Northern Hemisphere the sun is as far north as it ever comes. It then shines high

in our sky and gives us our warm summer days. But at this time it is far to the north of the people in southern South America and shines low in their northern sky at noon. Its slanting rays give them little heat, and thus they have winter with its cold winds, ice, and snow while we are having summer. What seasons are the boys and girls in Argentina and Chile having now? 

Look at the map and see just where the equator crosses South America. Through what countries does it pass? Here on the wide plains and lowlands there is no autumn, winter, or spring, but only hot summer weather all the year. Strange to say, however, there are some places in this part of the torrid zone where there is no real summer at all, where water freezes every night in the year, and where the snow never melts. See if you can tell by looking at the regional map what parts of Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, and Bolivia have summer all the year round, and what parts you would expect to have cold weather most of the year. 

The central plains of both North America and South America are drained by large rivers. The Mississippi flows southward through the wide plains of the United States. Its tributaries, which drain the highlands hundreds of miles to the east and west, bring to the great river enormous quantities of soil which it carries in its waters to its mouth. Here the stream flows more slowly and so drops much of the soil that it is carrying. Thus it has built up a large delta plain which year by year reaches farther and farther out into the Gulf of Mexico. 

In South America it is the mighty Amazon which drains the wide central plains, flowing to the east instead of to the south. In its muddy waters it, too,

SOUTH AMERICA 

carries immense quantities of soil which its many branches wash from mountain slopes and hillsides. 

But, unlike the Mississippi, it is building no delta plain at its mouth. This is because the swift ocean currents sweeping along the shore carry the soil for miles before dropping it in the bed of the sea. The great Amazon empties such an enormous quantity of water into the ocean that for miles from the land in the vicinity of its mouth the waters are fresh.

Both the Mississippi and the Amazon lie in the heart of a great continent, one in the temperate zone and the other in the torrid zone. In the basin of the 

Mississippi there are many large cities, fertile farms where wheat, corn, cotton, and sugar cane grow, and pastures where many cattle feed. For the most part the valley of the Amazon is a great tropical jungle covered with rich forests of valuable hardwoods, adorned with flowering vines, and carpeted with undergrowth so dense that you would have to chop your way through with an ax. It has few cities and is inhabited chiefly by Indians. There are large areas here where no white man has ever been. These hot, damp, wooded plains are called selvas, a name which comes from a Latin word meaning "forest." Look at the jungle forest shown in Fig. 4. The explorer who took this photograph from his plane says: "Following an unknown river through the tropical forest is great sport, but one must keep an eye on the channel. A mile or two to the right or to the left and the river disappears. All that remains there is the unbroken sea of forest." 

A network of railroads covers our Central Plains and fine, hard roads extend in every direction, but in

FIG. 4. As you look at this airplane view of the jungle forest in the Amazon Valley, try to picture the blue sky, the muddy, winding river, and the different greens of the trees. (Hamilton Rice Expedition)

SOUTH AMERICA 

the valley of the Amazon there are no roads or railroads. The long, navigable rivers are the only highways. Small steamers take supplies up to the scattered settlements on or near the rivers and carry down to ocean ports the products of inland regions. Many of the inhabitants of the Amazon Valley go about on the rivers in dugouts made from the trunks of trees. 

Most of the railroads of South America connect cities along the coast or extend from them a short distance inland. Only a few have been built in the mountain regions. Most of these are for the purpose of carrying provisions up to scattered mining centers and for transporting the minerals obtained there down to the seaports on the coast. The Indians who live in the mountains travel on foot or on mule-back and use mules and llamas to carry loads.

Airways have become increasingly important in South America in recent years. They have made it possible to travel easily and quickly to parts of the continent such as the Amazon Valley, which formerly was reached only by a long, slow journey by river boat. They have also made it possible to soar over high mountains where there are few railroads, and to reach places with neither railroads, navigable rivers, nor good highways.

You remember from your study of the countries of southern North America that the population consists largely of Indians and of mestizos, or people of mixed white and Indian blood. There are also many Indians and mestizos in South America, and many Negroes live in the warmer parts of the continent, just as is the case in the warm lands of North America. The people of the white race in South America, most of whom are of

Spanish descent and speak the Spanish language, live chiefly in the temperate part of the continent. They are far outnumbered by Indians and Negroes. You can see a group of Indians in Fig. 5. 

As you know, it was in 1492 that Christopher Columbus sailed westward across the Atlantic Ocean to find FIG. 5. Can you see the light hat of the trader in the center of this group of Indians? He is trading clothes and knives for the bows, arrows, and blowguns of the Indians. What things in the picture tell you whether these 

Indians live in the tropical jungle of South America or on the cold highlands? (Hamilton Rice Expedition) a new route to India. Instead of reaching India, however, he found a new world in the Western Hemisphere. 

You know the story of how Isabella, queen of Spain, pledged her jewels, it is said, to furnish Columbus with money to buy his ships and supplies and to hire his sailors. The Spanish were much interested in exploring lands that were then little known, and sent many expeditions to the newly discovered Americas.

SOUTH AMERICA 

One of the most famous Spaniards of this period was 

Francisco Pizarro. He explored much of western South

America, and in 1532 conquered the great Indian empire of the Incas which flourished at that time on the high Andean plateaus. Other Spaniards explored other parts of South America and made settlements there. 

Later one colony after another freed itself from Spain and became an independent country. 

Portugal, as well as Spain, was a land of ships and sailors, and explorers from that country settled Brazil. 

Many white people now living in Brazil are descendants of the early Portuguese and speak their language. 

For years many of the industries and much of the commerce of South America have been carried on by people from other European countries, chiefly Germany, Great Britain, and Italy. In recent years more people from the United States than ever before have been traveling in South America, becoming acquainted with the people, investing money in important industries, and carrying on business of different kinds. 

Meetings called Pan-American congresses, where delegates from the countries in both North and South America come together, have been held in different cities. Topics of common interest have been discussed, treaties made, and plans formed which are helping to unite more closely the countries in the Western Hemisphere. Because of these and other reasons we now carry on much trade with the countries of South America. Later you will read about many of the products which we buy from them. Perhaps you can name a few now, for you probably use some every day. What product comes from the plantation shown in Fig. 6?

FIG. 6. The long rows of coffee trees on this plantation stretch as far as you can see. The house of the owner of the plantation is in the group of buildings near the center of the picture. Most of the smaller buildings are the laborers' homes. (Courtesy of Brazilian-American Coffee Promotion Committee)

SOUTH AMERICA 

Many of the animals of South America are different from those in our continent. As is usual in tropical lands, the birds, butterflies, and snakes of the warm portion of the continent are larger and more brightly colored than those in temperate lands. There are turtles so large and heavy that you could not lift one FIG. 7. Although the tapir is one of the largest animals to be found in the forests of South America, it is shy and gentle. It searches for its food at night and sleeps during the daytime. (American Museum of Natural History) from the ground. You might enjoy their eggs for dinner as much as the Indians do, however. There are brilliantly colored parrots and chattering monkeys. The ant bear, an animal three or four feet long, feeds on ants.

It never lacks for food; for there are ants of all sizes and kinds, large and small, black and brown, those that bite and sting, and those that are harmless. Some white ants build mounds fifteen to twenty feet in height,skyscrapers of the ant world. Another variety travels in armies of many thousands. They clean the leaves and fruit from every bush and tree and plant in their route, and all the larger animals make haste to get out of their way.

The tapir, which looks somewhat like a large hog, is one of the largest South American animals. The body and head of the armadillo are covered with bony plates which protect it when attacked. The jaguar

is a relative of the tiger, smaller but dangerous. The sloth likes to hang all day upside down on the limb of a big tree. If it moves at all, it is so slowly that one might think it had fallen asleep between steps. 

The snakes of South 

America are so numerous and so poisonous that many people and thousands of animals are killed by them every year. In Brazil, just outside the city of São 

Paulo, there is an establishment where hundreds of the most dangerous varieties of snakes are kept. The attendants there extract their poison and make it into medicines which are sent all over the continent. 

If a person is able to obtain the proper medicine soon enough after he has been bitten by a poisonous snake, his life can be saved. 

Very different from the animals of the low, hot plains are those of the cooler highlands. Here live the famous cousins, the llama, so useful to the indians; the alpaca, valuable for its fine, soft wool; and the vicuña, with an even softer, silkier coat. 

FIG. 8. It seems as if it might be easier for these sloths to stand upright than to hang upside down. Both mother and baby are probably as comfortable hanging from the branch of a tree, however, as we are sitting in a chair. (American 

Museum of Natural History) 

South America is the continent nearest our own.

Every year our relations with the people there grow

SOUTH AMERICA 

closer and our dependence on them and their dependence on us greater. We need them as customers for the products of our mills and factories and foundries. 

Because of differences in climate, soil, surface, and vegetation, our South American neighbors can send us many things which we cannot produce for ourselves. 

We depend on them for most of our coffee and for some of the cocoa, sugar, rubber, and other products which we use. In the Andes there are minerals which we need, and in Argentina, Uruguay, and Brazil, where many cattle feed on the grassy plains, animal products of which we use large quantities are prepared. 

The United States has today a great opportunity in this southern land. The people there are ready to buy what we have to sell and to send us large quantities of their products. It is well for us to learn all that we can about the continent which in the future may be more closely bound to us in commerce than any other great division of the world.

CHAPTER II BRINGING WORLD NEIGHBORS NEARER 

Many people think of South America as lying south of North America. But does it? Look at a map of the

Western Hemisphere in your geography, and you will see that South America is much farther east than North America is. If you follow north a line drawn through the westernmost point of South America, you will find that it runs through the middle of Lake Erie. No part of South America lies as far west as Detroit, Michigan.

Easternmost South America lies far out in the Atlantic. 

Indeed, if the eastern coast of our continent extended as far east as the easternmost point of South America, Colonel Lindbergh's famous airplane flight from New

York to Paris would have been less than half as long as it really was.

Look again at the map, and you will see that this eastward-lying position of South America makes an ocean trip from our Atlantic and Gulf ports around the southern tip of South America to the west-coast ports of that continent very long indeed. Find Ecuador and Peru on the regional map in this book and then trace on a map of the world the route of a ship from New York to these countries, sailing down the eastern coast of South America, through the Strait of Magellan, and then up the west coast. This journey is about ten thousand miles long. Because of its great length little 19

SOUTH AMERICA 

trade was carried on for many years between the people of western South America and those of Europe and eastern United States.

The people of eastern United States long wished to bring their west-coast neighbors of South America nearer and also to shorten the voyage from their Atlantic and Gulf ports to Asia and the islands of the Pacific. 

We therefore bought from Panama, one of the Central American countries, a strip of land ten miles wide and fifty miles long, extending across the Isthmus of Panama at its narrowest point. Through the middle of this strip of land we built the Panama Canal. This brings the west-coast countries of South America thousands of miles nearer by water to New York and other Atlantic seaports. You can see from a world map that, by using the Canal, ships from New York sail almost directly south to the west coast of South America. Such a journey to Ecuador or Peru is only about three thousand miles long instead of ten thousand miles.

The Canal was begun in 1904, and the first ships passed through it in 1914. It is about fifty miles long, and vessels average from six to ten hours to pass through. Entering at sea level, they are lifted by means of locks to the higher water levels near the central part of the Zone, a distance of eighty-five feet. They are lowered to sea level at the other end of the Canal by means of another set of locks. In Fig. 9 you see a ship being towed through one of the locks. 

The Panama Canal has developed industries, changed trade routes, increased commerce, and lessened the price of products. It has had a greater effect on the commerce of the world than has any other engineering

FIG. 9. This ship is being towed through a lock in the Panama Canal. The two electric engines attached to the bow of the ship pull it ahead, and the two attached to the stern help to keep it in the center of the lock or to stop it if necessary. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

SOUTH AMERICA 

project except, perhaps, the Suez Canal. Ships passing through the Panama Canal have to pay a toll charge for the privilege; but, even so, it is cheaper to use the 

Canal than to incur the far greater expenses and run the risks of the dangers of the longer voyage.

When we bought the Canal Zone the streets of the towns there were deep with mud or dust and contained much garbage and filth. There were no sewers. Most of the water supply was caught in barrels and cisterns, which were filled at every tropical downpour. Hundreds of people died every year of malaria and yellow fever. Before work on the Canal could even be started, something had to be done to protect from disease and death the thousands of workmen who would be needed there.

The United States government put the work of cleaning up the Canal Zone into the hands of Colonel 

William C. Gorgas. Not long before the United States began work in Panama, the discovery had been made that certain varieties of mosquitoes are the carriers of disease germs, especially those of yellow fever and malaria, the scourge of tropical countries. So Colonel 

Gorgas and his helpers began to make war on the mosquitoes of Panama and to get rid of their breeding places. They screened buildings and covered stagnant waters with oil. The oil formed a scum which smothered the newly hatched mosquitoes. Pure water was brought from the mountains, and the people were taught safer and healthier ways of living and working. Colonel 

Gorgas became world famous because of the work that he did in the Canal Zone. Without it thousands of our workmen would have died, and we might have been

BRINGING WORLD NEIGHBORS NEARER 23 

as unsuccessful in building the Canal as the French people were when they started it in 1879. 

Before the time of Colonel Gorgas's work in the Canal Zone, many South American cities had been unhealthful and dangerous places in which to live. The people 

FIG. 10. Heavy tropical storms often wash much soil from the hillsides into the Canal. Here you see a dredge at work removing such soil. Can you explain how a dredge is operated? (Courtesy of Grace Line) there learned what had been done in Panama, and how the amount of sickness had been reduced, the death rate lowered, and the health of the people improved. Aided in some cases by Colonel Gorgas himself, people in 

South America started the cleaning up of their cities.

Today many places which were once hotbeds of disease are clean and healthful, with a death rate as low as, or lower than, that of the cities in our own country. 

Today thousands of white men are living and working in tropical regions of the world. They are building railroads and bridges, starting new industries, and

SOUTH AMERICA 

directing the work of the natives in getting products which every year are becoming more and more necessary in the United States and other countries of the temperate zone. The white man's conquest of the tropics began with the work of Colonel Gorgas in the Canal Zone. Perhaps some day you will go to the Canal Zone, visit the cities there, sail through the Canal, and see some of the places in South America which, since our work in the Canal Zone, have been made clean and healthful.

CHAPTER III BRAZIL, THE LARGEST COUNTRY IN SOUTH AMERICA 

Brazil is a huge country in size; only three countries in the world are larger. Find out from the Appendix what these countries are. If, on a map of North America, one of Brazil made on the same scale was laid with its most northern point on the arctic circle, its southern border would lie in the Gulf of Mexico. The distance from Recife westward to the boundary of Peru is a little more than the distance from New York to San Francisco. From this widest portion the country narrows toward the southern boundary. A larger part of Brazil than of any other country in the world lies in the equatorial belt. The country occupies nearly half of South America, its boundaries touch all the countries of that continent except two, and in it live about as many people as live in all the other countries of South America combined. Find out from the map what two South American countries do not touch Brazil.

The people of the United States are greatly interested in Brazil. We are the best customer of that country, and our Brazilian trade in some years amounts to about one third of a billion dollars. Think what great quantities of goods must be exchanged between the two countries to amount to this immense sum! Brazil sells us more coffee than it sends to all other countries put 27

SOUTH AMERICA 

together. In return for coffee and other products, we send to Brazil automobiles, gasoline, kerosene, flour, machinery, engines, motion-picture films, electric cars, and many other things. Automobile firms in the United 

States have branches in Brazil. Meat-packing, electriclighting, and vegetable-oil-producing companies have invested money or established plants there. Other 

United States concerns are obtaining concessions of land, water power, and mineral resources in this rich 

South American country.

There are many likenesses between Brazil and the United States. Both occupy the central part of similarly shaped continents; both are drained by mighty rivers; and both have in the east narrow chains of low mountains running parallel to the coast, while the main highland lies to the west.

This largest of all the South American republics is made up of states as our country is. There are twenty states, one territory, and a federal district in which the capital, Rio de Janeiro, is situated, just as our capital city, Washington, is in the District of Columbia. Some of the Brazilian states are very large. Amazonas, the state which includes much of the basin of the Amazon River, has an area equal to nearly a fourth of the entire country. It is so thinly peopled, however, that the population averages less than one person to each square mile. Matto Grosso, perhaps the least known of all the Brazilian states, is more than twice as large as Texas; São Paulo, the coffee state, is the richest and the most powerful in the republic; and the state of Rio de Janeiro is the most densely populated, with about eighty-five people to each square mile. Rhode Island,

FIG. 11. The greater part of the highlands in eastern Brazil is thinly populated. Many of the natives of this region live in huts like the one in the picture. They grow corn and other crops in their gardens. What should you say about the rainfall here?

SOUTH AMERICA 

our most densely populated state, averages about seven times as many, although it is only about one twentieth as large as the state of Rio de Janeiro. 

The United States is so large that a foreigner coming here could see only a little of the country unless he made a very long visit. So in a short visit to Brazil we can see only a little of that great country. Though nearly all of it is in the torrid zone, we shall find that its various parts differ as much as Florida differs from Oklahoma. Some parts receive a great deal of rain and some very little. Some regions are low and hot, and others are high enough to be cool and pleasant. We shall see large areas that are unfit for agriculture as well as some of the finest farms in the world. In some states there are large ranches with thousands of cattle; in other states there are no ranches or domestic animals. 

In some inland regions only uncivilized Indians and Negroes live, whereas on the coast there are large cities where white people live and carry on business. 

The heavy rainfall, the warm climate, the fertile soil, and the varied surface make possible the production of a wide variety of crops. There are fruit farms, sugar, tobacco, and cotton plantations, and millions of cacao and coffee trees. Thousands of acres are planted to wheat and corn. In Brazil and other South American countries we shall find many fields of manioc, for it is one of the chief foods of the natives in the warm regions. 

The thick roots look somewhat like sweet potatoes.

The people boil them for food or grind them into a coarse flour that they use in making cakes which serve in the place of bread. It is from a variety of manioc that the tapioca which we use in puddings is made.

BRAZIL 

31 No country in the world has finer pastures for cattle than Brazil, and the cattle industry has become of great importance, as you will learn later. Brazil also has mineral wealth, much of which is not yet developed. Its coal deposits are too small to be of much use in manufactur- FIG. 12. This woman is baking a cake made from manioc flour. She has built a fire under the stone in front of her and is using the stone as a stove. 

The cake is about half an inch thick and more than a foot in diameter. 

Should you like some of it for your supper? (Hamilton Rice Expedition) ing, and for this reason its large, rich beds of iron ore have been but little worked. Great amounts of manganese, a metal used in the making of steel, are mined. 

This is the most valuable mineral export of the country. 

Gold and also diamonds and other precious stones have been mined in Brazil for many years, but their production is not so large or so important as it was formerly. 

The tropical forest which covers most of the Amazon Valley contains dozens of useful products, many of

SOUTH AMERICA 

which are as yet little known and used. A famous writer calls this great forest region nature's apothecary shop. Besides the valuable lumber, there are plants, trees, and vines from which can be obtained gums, oils. dyes, and materials for drugs and medicines and for tanning hides. Except for the rubber product, little of the forest wealth has as yet been touched. Large areas here have never been explored, and some rivers of considerable size have never been sailed on by any white man. No roads or railroads have been built through the great forest, and the rivers are the only highways. No country in the world contains so many long rivers as Brazil. Besides the rivers of the Amazon system, there are hundreds of others which flow into the Atlantic or which enter the Paraná River and so reach the ocean farther south.

The banks of many of the streams are heavily wooded. In the more tropical parts of the country the trees are so thick, the jungles so dense, and the tangle of vines so matted that it would be impossible for you to pass through them without cutting your way at every step. On page 9 you saw a picture of this jungle forest. The variety of animals which live in the Amazon 

Valley would furnish a menagerie with many specimens, and the plants would be no less interesting. 

Beautiful flowers grow in abundance in the swamps and jungles.

Brazil has more advantages to aid in its development than the United States had in its early history. When our first colonists blazed their trails through the forests and built their little log cabins in the clearings, they knew nothing of railroads, telegraphs, airplanes, and

Notice on the regional map how large a part of Brazil is occupied by the Amazon Lowlands. We shall visit FIG. 13. In remote parts of Brazil pack animals are used for carrying burdens. These oxen are carrying firewood from a forest to a town in the state of Matto Grosso. (By Ewing Galloway, N.Y.) this region first of all. We shall not expect to see hills or mountains in these lowlands; neither shall we see many industries being carried on, for most of the leading industries of Brazil are carried on in the higher, cooler parts of the country. 

We will start for Brazil on a steamer from New York.

The ocean voyage to Belém, the nearest large port of Brazil, will take us about two weeks. As we sail southward the air grows warmer. When we are nearly at

SOUTH AMERICA 

the end of our journey, there is great excitement on board, for we are soon to cross the equator. According to old legends Father Neptune, ancient god of the sea, is supposed to board the ship at this time. The passengers who have never before "crossed the line" are preparing and being prepared to meet him. Soon he appears in appropriate costume, and those who have never had the honor of meeting him are initiated as his true sons and daughters amid the laughter and cheers of the more fortunate passengers who have been through such an initiation before.

When we are about a hundred miles from the Brazilian coast, the ocean loses its beautiful blue color and becomes a muddy yellow. This change is caused by the water which the mighty Amazon pours into the ocean. 

The soil which makes the ocean water so muddy comes from the hills and mountains hundreds of miles to the west. What a long journey some of it has taken since it was washed by the rain and the streams from the mountains of Peru, Ecuador, Bolivia, Colombia, and Venezuela!

The Amazon pours its yellow waters into the ocean through several mouths. The distance between the most northerly and the most southerly of these mouths is more than one hundred and fifty miles. What place is about this distance from your home? In the space between the outer mouths are many islands. Most of these islands are only a few feet above sea level and are covered with dense forests. The weather is always hot here; the temperature is seldom below 80° F. The land is flooded part of the year, and some areas are covered with water each day by the tides. Only a few

FIG. 14. Travelers on the Pará River often see sunsets as beautiful as this one. The Pará River is the southernmost mouth of the Amazon. (A. H. Fisher from Ewing Galloway, N.Y.)

SOUTH AMERICA 

natives live here in the heat and dampness. Their little huts are raised on posts above the water, and their food consists chiefly of fish and fruits. On the highest part of the largest island, Marajó, the people raise some cattle, which help to supply the city of Belém with meat. FIG. 15. The men on horseback are cowboys on the island of Marajó, which lies between two of the mouths of the Amazon. It is more than twice as large as the state of Massachusetts. In the distance you can see a few of the herd of cattle that the cowboys are tending The most southerly mouth of the Amazon is the Pará River. Find it on the map opposite page 32. It has a wide, deep channel, and we shall follow this route in starting on our trip into the interior of Brazil. The banks of this delta stream are densely wooded. When we think that it is possible to travel for days on the Amazon itself and journey for weeks up dozens of its branches and be all the time in the midst of dense tropical forests, we begin to realize something of the vast extent of the selvas.

BRAZIL 

37 Large ocean steamers from the United States and European countries sail for a thousand miles or more up the Amazon, and we could, if we wished, make our trip up the river without changing boats. Before going farther, however, we wish to see more of Belém, our first South American city. Since it lies only a few feet above sea level and almost on the equator, we know that it will be very hot. If we go slowly and rest indoors in the middle of the day, as the Brazilians do, we shall get along very comfortably.

In spite of the heat Belém is an attractive city. The trees and shrubs and plants are larger and have bigger and brighter blossoms than do those in our gardens at home, and they make the whole city look as if it were decorated for some festival. The houses are surrounded by spreading shade trees, and many of the streets are lined with magnolia trees, which in blossoming time are covered with large white blossoms. Nothing looks dry or dusty, for there is plenty of rain, too much it seems to us, for a heavy shower interferes with our sightseeing nearly every afternoon during our stay in the city. After the rain, however, a cooler breeze usually springs up which lasts well into the night. This makes the evenings delightful, and crowds of people throng the brilliantly lighted plazas, as parks and squares in South America are called. 

During our visit in Belém we can stop in good hotels; ride in comfortable electric cars or in automobiles made in the United States; get news of the world through daily papers, the telephone, and the radio; enjoy motion pictures; and learn about the history, plants, animals, and people of South America in interesting museums.

SOUTH AMERICA 

While we are in South America we want to find out about the kinds of work which the people do, for many of their products come to our country. Here in Belém there are sawmills where mahogany, ebony, and other logs from the tropical forests are made into boards and N87 60T FIG. 16. These boats, called lighters, are at the docks of the Amazon port of Manáos. They are loaded with Brazil nuts which are to be carried to the large ships out in the river. In the next chapter you will read more about the important city of Manáos other forms of lumber. In another part of the city are tanneries where cattle hides are made into soft leather by the use of the bark of the mangrove trees which grow in the swamps. In some mills here nuts and seeds which grew on vines, shrubs, and trees in the forest are crushed and pressed for the oil they contain. Some of the oil is used in soap factories in Belém, and some is shipped to other countries. Large quantities of the nuts and seeds are exported also. Next to the rubber

BRAZIL 

39 industry, the gathering and shipping of oil-producing nuts and seeds and the making of oil are the most important industries of the Amazon Valley. Have you ever eaten the big three-cornered Brazil nuts? They are one of the most important products of the region. 

Thousands of natives spend much of their time gathering them, and many others work in the oil mills and on the wharves where the oil and nuts are loaded on vessels for export. In the harbor of Belém are many small boats which have brought the nuts to the city from farther up the river.

Brazil nuts grow in a hard, round shell about as large as a grapefruit. They are packed in so closely that if you once took them out you could never fit them in again. In many Brazilian cities the trees which produce these nuts are planted along the streets and in plazas and gardens. They make fine shade trees because they are tall and have wide-spreading branches. 

The farmers around Belém raise tobacco, which is used in the cigarette factories in the city, and native fruits are preserved in the canneries there. Thousands of tons of rubber have been shipped from Belém, and it was this product which caused the city to grow into a large, important port. The rubber industry in South 

America is not nearly so important as it used to be; but rubber is such a valuable product, and the history of the industry is so interesting, that we wish to know more about it than we can learn here in Belém. So we will take a trip up the Amazon River, see the trees which yield the milky liquid from which rubber is made, and watch the Indians tapping the trees and making big black balls of rubber.

CHAPTER IV  

A sail up the Amazon is an interesting journey. The great river is wider, deeper, carries more water to the ocean, has more large tributaries, and by means of them drains more land than does any other river in the world. One writer calls the Amazon the "king of rivers," and the land which it drains the "queen of basins." At times the river is between fifty and sixty miles wide and looks more like a sea than a river. For this reason the Brazilians often call it the Rio Mar, which means "river-sea." During the wet season, from December to early June, there is a daily downpour of rain in its valley, and the main stream and its tributaries rise from twenty to fifty feet above their usual level. Then the country for hundreds of miles around is covered with water. The people living in the flooded areas go to the higher lands, and all travel is by boat. Can you imagine sailing on such an inland sea in the deep shade of tall trees growing in the water? How different it would be from a sail on the sunny, open stretches of waters which we are used to, and how queer it would seem to glide along under the thick, green foliage!

The forest here is very different from any that you have ever seen. A forest in the United States is made up of one or two kinds of trees or possibly of half a 42

dozen varieties. We speak of our hemlock woods, our maple groves, or our pine forests. But here on the banks of the Amazon there are twenty, thirty, forty, and even fifty or more kinds of trees, each trying, it would seem, to make a forest of its own. Many of the FIG. 17. This boat came down the Amazon when the river was flooded. 

The captain missed the channel, and the boat stuck on what he thought was a sandbar. When the waters began to lower, however, he found that his boat was on the shore. There it had to stay until the river rose again the next year. (Hamilton Rice Expedition) trees in these jungle forests are palms. Their foliage is usually lighter in color than that of some other trees which grow there.

Then, too, no forest in the United States is so dark and has such deep shadows as this hot, damp Brazilian jungle. The trees are so thick, the vines so many, and the leaves so big that in large areas no sunshine can get through to lighten the gloom or to dry the ground. In the middle of the day the bright tropical

SOUTH AMERICA 

sun blazes overhead, but in the depth of the forest there is only a dim, shadowy light.

The tall trunks of the great trees rise straight and clean, without a branch, for fifty or even a hundred feet. Some are covered with smooth, silvery-gray bark, and some are entirely hidden by vines with big leaves and with stems as large as a man's arm. The clinging vines, climbing upward toward the sun and the air above, slowly but surely kill the big trees which support them.

This wonderful forest is always green and always brilliant with flowers. Can you imagine trees the size of large maples covered with big red blossoms, and vines as high as a tall building gay with bright yellow flowers? It would be worth a long, hard, hot trip to see such a sight, would it not?

The lumber that might be obtained from the forest would be enough to last the world for years, and, as you know, the bark, sap, seeds, and fruit furnish useful oils, gums, and dyes. Some plants and trees contain fibers as fine as silk and as strong as linen. 

For a long time the natives have used these fibers for making clothing, hammocks, and baskets, and in the future the people of other countries may make use of some of them.

Our trip up the Amazon takes us through two of the states of Brazil - Pará and Amazonas. Pará, stretching inland from the coast, is the smaller of the two. 

Amazonas is almost a country in itself; indeed, it is much larger than any country in Europe except Russia. It is nearly three times the size of our largest state, Texas, yet in all its great area there are fewer

FIG. 18. Many of the people in the Amazon Basin live in little villages like the one in the picture, with the jungle forest behind their homes and the river in front. The picture shows you how necessary boats are to the natives of the region. (Courtesy of Joseph T. Singewald, Jr.)

SOUTH AMERICA 

people than in the two largest cities of that state. 

Most of the people of Amazonas live in Manáos, the capital, and in a few other towns and villages along the rivers. There are some Indians and Negroes, but the majority of the people are half-breed descendants of these races and of the Portuguese explorers and settlers. The few full-blooded Indians live far away from any white settlements.

The people of the colored races here are engaged chiefly in the rubber industry, but some gather Brazil nuts to be sent down to Belém on the steamer which stops once in a while at the village wharf. The natives who live on the smaller streams which are not on the route of the steamer load the nuts into their dugout canoes and carry them downstream to the nearest village at which the steamer calls.

Now that we know a little about the region which we are to visit and its people, we will begin our journey up the Amazon. Ocean vessels which need a depth of twenty feet of water can sail upstream as far as Iquitos in Peru. This city is twenty-three hundred miles from the mouth of the Amazon. Find Iquitos on the map and see how near it is to the Pacific Ocean. 

From time to time we see large islands in the river which we mistake for the shore. We have heard of people who did not know that islands were the tops of hills and mountains rising out of the water, but who thought that they were floating bodies and wondered how sailors could find their way to them so easily. Here in the great river-sea are islands that really float. When the Amazon is flooded, it often washes away pieces of its banks and uproots trees

which it carries along in its waters. Gradually more and more material becomes tangled in the mass, causing it to grow larger and larger the farther it floats. 

Now and then we pass huts of the rubber-gatherers, which are on the edge of the river and are separated

Here the small steamers which go up the side streams to collect rubber stop to get fuel. One thing which we are sure to find at every hut is a boat, for the river is the only means of travel. If roads were made in the dense jungles, they would have to be constantly cared for, or in a

SOUTH AMERICA 

few months they would be entirely hidden by the new growth of trees, shrubs, and vines. 

During the wet season in the Amazon Basin it rains every day. The morning dawns fair and pleasant, but soon clouds begin to form and the rain comes pouring down. The trees drip, and steam rises from the hot, moist land. Every day the river rises higher, and finally the shores disappear and the trees seem to be growing out of the water. After several weeks the showers cease. Then the water lowers, and later the soaked land appears.

As you know, the Amazon has many large branches. 

Soon after entering the main river we passed the mouth of the Xingú. The Xingú has several tributaries, any one of which is a stream larger than some of the important rivers of Europe. Find this river on the map opposite page 32. 

Some hundred miles farther up the Amazon the Tapájoz enters it. This branch is nearly a thousand miles long, and at its mouth it is from eight to ten miles wide. 

Be sure that you know just where the Tapájoz River is, for later you will read some interesting things about it. 

Still farther up the Amazon we come to the mouth of its most important tributary, the Madeira River, one of the longest waterways of South America. The name means "river of wood," and was given to the stream because of the large number of uprooted trees which are thrown up on the banks or float on the water. The Madeira is navigable for seven hundred miles. Its course is then interrupted by rapids and falls, but above these it is navigable for large boats for several hundred miles farther.

On the map opposite page 32 you will find beyond the falls a region called Acre. This territory was formerly claimed by both Bolivia and Brazil. Neither nation was willing to give it up to the other on account of its valuable forests, which contain millions of FIG. 20. This raft of valuable hardwood logs is on its way to the Amazon port of Manáos. It has been floated down the Rio Negro for more than a thousand miles. At Manáos the logs will be loaded on a large ship bound for Europe and the United States. (Courtesy of Joseph T. Singewald, Jr.) rubber trees. Both finally agreed to a treaty, however, by which this territory was given to Brazil on condition that that country should pay Bolivia a large sum of money and should build a railroad around the falls of the Madeira River. 

Perhaps you wonder why the people of Bolivia wished so much to have a railroad around the falls through this unpeopled tropical jungle. The regional map will help you to understand this. Notice how much of Bolivia lies east of the Andes Mountains.

There are good pastures here and fertile farm lands. 

Deep forests with millions of rubber trees and other trees of great value cover large areas, and beds of rich minerals are hidden in the earth. As yet no roads or railroads lead westward over the mountains to ports on the Pacific Ocean. So the only way to get the products of eastern Bolivia out of the country is to ship them down the Madeira, around the falls by rail, and thence down the Madeira and Amazon rivers to Belém.

The largest branch which enters the Amazon from the north is the Rio Negro, named from the dark color of its water. This river is probably as long as our own great Pacific-coast river, the Columbia. On the regional map find the Rio Negro. In what country does it rise?

So near together are the headwaters of the Orinoco River and the Rio Negro that during the rainy season they meet. Just think what a long river trip a person can take when these streams are flooded! On the map see if you can find your way by river from the mouth of the Orinoco to the mouth of the Amazon. 

It takes about five days to sail up the Amazon to the mouth of the Rio Negro, yet even there we are not halfway to the source of the great river in the Andes Mountains. The Amazon rises in Peru, where it is called the Marañon River. Find this name on the regional map and trace the course of the great river all the way from its source in the Andes Mountains to the Atlantic Ocean. Notice about how far down in its course the Rio Negro enters it.

tropical forest that will surprise you, something that you would not expect to see in such a region. Electric cars run out into the suburbs, where are the truck farms which supply the city markets with fresh fruits and vegetables. CHAPTER V  

Rubber is one of the world's most useful products. 

During recent years it has become more and more valuable as many new uses for it have been found. 

Without rubber what would automobile owners use for tires; fire companies for hose; manufacturing companies for machine tubing and belting; hospitals for sheeting, gloves, hot-water bags, and dozens of other supplies; and electric companies for the material which prevents electric power from doing damage and going to waste? What would you do without erasers, balls, rubbers, raincoats, and many other rubber articles which you use? 

White people first heard of rubber through Christopher Columbus. When he returned to Spain, he told of seeing some natives on the shores of one of the islands in the West Indies playing with a ball which, when thrown to the ground, bounded high in the air. 

In South America he saw some Indians smearing their moccasins and other garments with the same material to make them waterproof. The story is told that 

Columbus gave the king of Spain a suit of this rubber clothing. The king was proud of it until one very hot day he wore it out in the blazing sunshine, when his wonderful suit became a sticky mass. In those days, of course, nothing was known about the process of vul- 54

canizing, which now prevents rubber goods from being affected by changes in temperature. 

It was many years later, in 1770, that an Englishman named Priestley discovered that this milky juice of a South American tree, when hardened, would erase FIG. 22. The difference between high and low water at Manáos is more than forty feet, and floating docks rise and fall with the river. Rubber and other products are transferred from boats to the warehouses on shore by means of an aërial cable. To what countries do you think the rubber may be shipped from the warehouses? (Courtesy of Joseph T. Singewald, Jr.) pencil marks. He made some little cubes of the substance, about one-half inch square, and sold them for seventy-five cents each. Because of the work they did, he called the little cubes "rubbers," and the name has clung to the material ever since. Raincoats were once called mackintoshes because an Englishman. named Mackintosh discovered how to prepare and spread rubber on cloth to make it waterproof.

The most interesting and important experiments with rubber were made by Charles Goodyear, a Con-

SOUTH AMERICA 

necticut merchant. He spent nearly all the money he possessed trying to find ways of making rubber more useful. He had learned that sulphur mixed with rubber improved the quality of the rubber, but the process was still far from being a success. One day Mr. Goodyear happened to drop some of the mixture on a hot stove. Instead of melting, as he expected it to do, it hardened. He kept on with his experiments until he found just the right amount of heat needed to make rubber more elastic, durable, and immune to temperature changes. This process is called vulcanizing. Of course many improvements have been made since then, but it was the vulcanizing process discovered by Charles Goodyear which gave our great rubber industry of today its start.

The United States produces no rubber, but it uses more than any other nation. Our millions of automobiles have rubber tires, and between twenty-five and thirty thousand different articles are made wholly or partly of rubber. The United States leads the world in the manufacture of rubber belting, hose, tires and tubes, boots and shoes, heels and soles, and many other articles made of this useful product. 

Of the hundreds of products which we have to import, rubber ranks among the first four in value. The other three are coffee, cane sugar, and raw silk. In ordinary years we buy about a billion pounds of rubber and rubberlike products. This is about half of all that is produced in the entire world. 

The milky juice, called latex, from which rubber is made comes from a tropical tree. The latex is found between the outer and the inner bark of the tree.

A similar juice is found in several hundred kinds of trees, plants, and vines that grow in the torrid zone. 

It is found also in plants common to the temperate zone, such as the milkweed and goldenrod. The best latex for rubber-making, however, comes from the FIG. 23. This is a trader's hut near the bank of one of the branches of the 

Amazon. Why is it raised on posts? Why is it thatched with palm leaves instead of with shingles? What do you think the trader will give the natives in return for the products that they bring to him? (Courtesy of C. F. Marbut)

Hevea, a tree native to the Amazon Valley. For more than a century the latex of Hevea trees has been collected by the Indian and mestizo natives there. Some of them are hired by rubber companies, and some work independently, selling to traders and agents the product they prepare. The work of collecting the latex lasts only part of the year. During other months some of the laborers gather Brazil nuts, vanilla beans, sarsaparilla roots, and other forest products. The

SOUTH AMERICA 

huts of most of the rubber-gatherers are near the rivers, and some of them are built on posts several feet high. They are small and are roofed with a thick layer of palm leaves. The floor is usually made of earth, and the beds are hammocks made of fibers found in the forest.

Before the sun is up the laborers start on their rounds through the forest. Each one has many trees to visit and much to do in preparing the latex after his return. He carries some little cups and a tool somewhat like a hatchet. He follows a narrow path that he has chopped through the thick undergrowth from tree to tree. He makes his path in a circle so that when he has finished his rounds he will be near home. 

Arriving at the first rubber tree, the laborer cuts some little gashes in its bark, being careful not to cut so deep as to injure the tree. Beneath each gash he fastens a little cup so that the latex will drop into it. Then he goes on to other trees, where he repeats the process. After visiting the last tree on his route he returns to his hut.

Later in the day he makes a second round to collect the latex which has dripped into the cups. If he is working alone, he brings the latex back to his hut. 

If several laborers are working for a company, they carry the latex to some central place. Here two posts are fixed in the ground six feet or farther apart, and a pole supported by the posts is so arranged that it may be turned over and over. Under the pole the men build a fire of palm nuts, which burn with a thick smoke. Over the fire they place a metal cone, with a hole in the small end, through which the smoke passes.

FIG. 24. These men are preparing the latex which they have collected. Inside the hut one of them is smoking a ball of rubber over the fire. The palm nuts which you see in the gourd are used for fuel

SOUTH AMERICA 

These preparations made, the workers pour some latex on the pole just above the cone, turning the pole round and round as they pour. The heat and the smoke harden the latex and darken it. More latex is added from time to time, until a large ball, or biscuit, weighing many pounds is formed. 

The big black biscuits of rubber are carried to the nearest river landing. If the latex has been prepared near some small stream which steamers do not enter, the balls are carried in canoes down to a station on a larger stream. Here they are loaded on steamers which take them down to Manáos or Belém. The biscuits are then cut up and transferred to ocean steamers. 

The laborer who works alone prepares his rubber a little differently (see Fig. 24). He uses a small pole, one end of which is paddle-shaped. He dips this end into his pail of latex and turns it back and forth over the smoke of his little fire. The balls which he makes are small, usually weighing from four to eight pounds. 

Most of the rubber stations are near the banks of the Amazon and its branches, and the rubber-gatherers go only a few miles into the forests. In the unexplored parts of Brazil, Peru, Ecuador, and Bolivia there are probably millions of rubber trees undiscovered and untapped, but at present the expense of getting the rubber to the streams is too great for the business to be profitable in these regions.

Until about 1900 all the rubber used in the world was made from the latex collected from wild forest trees. Some was obtained in Central America and 

Africa, but most of it came from the rubber trees in the Amazon Valley. Today only a very small part

of the world's total rubber production comes from the Amazon Valley. The remainder is produced on the other side of the world.

The reason for this great change is an interesting part of the story of rubber. In 1876 an Englishman FIG. 25. After the balls of rubber are prepared as described on pages 5860, they are taken to river trading stations for shipment down the river 

(Brown Brothers Photo) 

named Sir Henry Wickham was sent by his government to study the forests of South America. While there he became interested in rubber trees and rubber production. He wondered if these trees would grow in the tropical lands governed by England. He found some of the best varieties of Hevea trees growing near the Tapájoz River, and from them he collected about seventy thousand seeds, which he packed carefully and

SOUTH AMERICA 

sent to England. Here they were planted and given special attention. About three thousand lived and sent up little green shoots. When the plants were a few months old, they were sent to the tropical island of Ceylon, south of Asia. A few of the plants lived and bore seeds, and it was these seeds which started the great rubber industry of southeastern Asia. 

A large part of the Malay Peninsula, which extends southward from the continent of Asia, belongs to the British. The plantations on this peninsula now produce about half of all the rubber used in the world. Not far away are the three large islands of Sumatra, Java, and Borneo, which belong to the group called the East Indies. These islands and Ceylon produce nearly all the rest of the world's rubber.

If you could see these well-kept plantations of Asia and compare them with the jungle forests of the Amazon Valley, you would hardly be able to realize that the same product comes from them both. The thin sheets of rubber which are prepared from the latex gathered on the plantations in the Far East look very different from the big round biscuits made in South America. In Asia the latex is poured on heated tables, where it hardens and is pressed into sheets. 

Besides being the source of the seeds from which the big plantations in the Far East were started, the Tapájoz region is interesting now for another reason. 

It is here that Henry Ford, the famous automobile manufacturer, has acquired an area nearly as large as the two states of Rhode Island and Connecticut, with a frontage of seventy-five miles on the Tapájoz River. 

In this wilderness workmen have cleared away some of

the jungle forest and have built houses for the officials of his rubber company, camps for the native workers, and a store, hospital, school, machine shop, power plant, garage, and a railroad which will be extended as the plantation grows. Electric lights, electric refrig- FIG. 26. This is part of a rubber plantation owned by Americans in Liberia, Africa. Most of the laborers employed on the plantation are Negroes. (Courtesy of Firestone Tire and Rubber Company) eration, electric washing machines, modern plumbing, and other up-to-date equipment have been installed. Wharves along the river and roads through the forest have been built. Vegetables for the employees are raised on a large farm, and cattle are kept to furnish milk and meat. Laborers are cutting down trees to clear the jungle and making use of the fine hard wood for buildings in the settlement and for export. Several million rubber trees are now growing on

SOUTH AMERICA 

the thousands of acres which have been cleared, and more trees are being constantly planted. In future years great harvests of latex may be expected in Fordlandia, as the place is called. 

Other United States manufacturers also believe that we should produce some of the rubber we use. Leading tire manufacturers have large plantations in Sumatra and Java, in the Philippine Islands, and in Liberia, Africa. Trees have been planted in Florida to find out if rubber can be produced successfully in our own country. 

That electrical wizard, the late Thomas Edison, was long interested in the production of rubber. His idea was to produce it from crops that can be sown and harvested yearly. So he began to experiment with common plants, especially goldenrod and milkweed.

It will be interesting to watch the experiments which are taking place. If they are successful, they will cause as great a change in the rubber industry as did the seeds which Sir Henry Wickham sent from Brazil to England years ago.

CHAPTER VI  

Chocolate and cocoa are made from the fruit of the cacao tree. We are especially interested in this product, for we use more cacao beans and manufacture more chocolate and cocoa than does any other country. 

Chocolate was used by the people of South America and Mexico long before Columbus discovered America. 

Europeans knew nothing about chocolate until Columbus and other explorers carried some back to Spain and showed their friends how the Indians of South 

America prepared it. They ground the beans to a powder and mixed the powder with water. Then they beat this mixture in a sort of churn until it was light and foamy and much thicker than the chocolate that we drink. Latl was their word for water, and choco seems to have been suggested by the noise made by the dasher as it beat the frothy mixture in the churn. 

So we get the word chocolatl, or, as we call it, chocolate. 

The cacao tree grows well throughout the torrid zone. It needs considerable rain, a rich soil, and a warm temperature averaging from seventy to ninety degrees the year round. In all the tropical countries of 

South America there are regions suitable for the growth of cacao trees. Ecuador used to produce more cacao beans than any other country in the world, but its production today is much less than that of Brazil. 67

SOUTH AMERICA 

The world's largest plantations and the regions which rank highest in the production of cacao beans, however, lie across the Atlantic Ocean from Brazil. There, FIG. 27. Cacao pods are from six to ten inches in length. As you can see, they are fastened to the trunk or the larger branches of the trees by very short stems. Each fruit usually contains thirty or more beans in the Gold Coast in 

West Africa and on the tropical islands off the coast, more of these useful beans are produced than anywhere else. 

Brazil ranks next to the Gold Coast in the production of cacao beans. The greater part of its crop is produced in the state of Bahia, one of the larger and more important states of Brazil.

The cacao trees are grown on plantations which stretch in a broad belt for many miles along its coastal plain. On the wharves and in the warehouses of São Salvador, the capital of this state, you would see many thousand bags filled with cacao beans ready to be loaded on the steamers tied up at the docks. Find this city on the map opposite page 32.

São Salvador, which was formerly called Bahia, is

Brazil's oldest city and one of its chief seaports. It has a large, deep harbor guarded by strong forts. A tall lighthouse rises near the entrance, and modern wharves with many warehouses behind them line the water front. The city is built on steep cliffs near the water's 

FIG. 28. São Salvador is a busy seaport. Cacao beans and tobacco are its most important exports. Why is this port well located for trade with the countries of western Europe? (Photograph by Lionel Green) edge. The older part lies on the lower level near the water. Here are narrow, winding streets and old business houses and warehouses, as well as some modern docks and wharves. The higher part, on the cliffs, is pleasanter. Here are beautiful homes and gardens, pleasant parks, fine streets, modern shops, schools, theaters, and churches. Elevators carry the people up and down between the two levels.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Manufacturing is increasing in São Salvador, as it is in other parts of Brazil, and there are many mills and factories in the city. Bahia is one of the most thickly settled states in the country and has a larger proportion of Negroes than any other state. In São 

Salvador we should see many Negroes working on the docks and wharves and in the manufacturing establishments. Bahia is famous for the tobacco raised on its many farms, as well as for its cacao and much of the tobacco crop is used in São Salvador for making cigars and cigarettes. There are also cotton and jute mills and sugar refineries in the city. Near the water front are many wholesale establishments which ship away the manufactures of the city and the products of the surrounding region. 

Let us go inland from São Salvador and visit some of the plantations from which come the cacao beans that are sent to this seaport. The trees on the cacao plantations look different from any we have ever seen. 

Fig. 27 shows you that the big dark-colored cacao pods grow close to the trunks and branches of the trees. Workmen cut off the pods with sharp knives. 

In order to reach the higher pods, they use knives fastened to poles. It must be tiresome work to bend back and look up into the trees while handling one of the long poles. See the piles of pods under the trees. What quantities there are! They are left there a few days, and then laborers cut the pods open and scrape out the beans. These, with the pulp which clings to them, are put into boxes three or four feet square, where they are allowed to sweat, or ferment, for a time. The

boxes have holes in the bottom through which the drippings pass out. During this fermentation process workmen stir the beans from time to time. The stirring and the heat of the sun help to improve the flavor of the beans and to remove the pulp which covers FIG. 29. The workman is raking the cacao beans in the tray so that all will get their share of the hot sunshine. (Courtesy of Walter Baker & Co., Inc.) them. Finally, men carry the beans to large drying floors and spread them out to a depth of several inches. These floors are often made of cement. In some small towns the paved sidewalks and even the streets are used as drying floors. Some large producers of cacao dry their beans indoors by means of hot air, but most of them are dried in the sunshine. 

The beans are left on the drying floors for some days. From time to time they are raked back and

SOUTH AMERICA 

forth until they are thoroughly dried. In some places barefooted men and boys walk over the beans again and again, stirring them well with their feet. Their bare feet touch only the skin which covers the beans. 

This is removed later in the factories, and so the process is not so unsanitary as it sounds. Gradually machinery is taking the place of these crude methods of work, but on the majority of plantations the older methods are still used.

We import each year from European countries several thousand tons of prepared cocoa and chocolate. 

Besides all this manufactured product, United States firms buy yearly from tropical lands about a quarter of a million tons of cacao beans, which are manufactured here in some sixty large factories. The beans come from Brazil, Venezuela, Colombia, and Ecuador in

South America, from plantations in Central America and the West Indies, and from Africa and distant Ceylon. Different flavors and shades of color are given to chocolate by mixing different varieties of cacao beans.

When the beans arrive at a factory, they are thoroughly cleaned. Then they are roasted for three or four hours in huge ovens, each one of which is large enough to hold a ton. When the beans are sufficiently brown, they are removed from the ovens to machines where the skin is broken and fanned away. Then they are crushed and ground between two big steel cylinders which revolve rapidly against each other. In a day one of these monster mills grinds more than half a ton of beans into a thick, dark liquid that looks somewhat like cold molasses. This, when cooled in cakes, is the unsweetened chocolate which is used for

cooking. In large factories where sweet chocolate is made the cacao beans and sugar are put into one end of a long machine, and from the other end come the perfectly formed cakes such as you buy to eat. Such a machine can make five tons of these cakes in a day. 

Nearly half of the cacao bean is made up of fat called cocoa butter.

This large amount of fat makes chocolate too rich a drink for some people, and so some of the fat is removed, leaving a fine, dry powder. This is our common cocoa.

FIG. 30. This machine keeps the liquid choco late, which will be made into chocolate bars, at an even temperature. Any variation in this temperature, however small, would injure the flavor of the chocolate. (Courtesy of Walter 

Baker & Co., Inc.) 

It is interesting to watch the machines in a factory measure the powdered cocoa and pour it into cans, put on the covers, roll the cans into their labels, and even nail the covers on the wooden boxes in which the cans are packed. 

Some of the oil removed in making cocoa is put into sweet chocolate to absorb the sugar, and much is used in making cold cream, face lotions, and medicines.
