CHAPTER VII COFFEE, THE MOST IMPORTANT PRODUCT OF BRAZIL 

We are now going to visit São Paulo, the richest of all the Brazilian states. It is as large as the states of 

New York and Pennsylvania combined, and in it live about one sixth of all the people of Brazil. Its beautiful capital of the same name has more than a million people, and its chief seaport, Santos, ships away more coffee than does any other city in the world. 

If we come by water to Santos, we shall ride up to 

São Paulo, fifty miles away, on a famous railroad.

The word "up" is used correctly here; for São Paulo, in the highlands, is half a mile higher than Santos on the low, hot coastal plain. After riding a few miles along the plain the land rises sharply. Here our engine is changed for one that grips a strong cable as it pulls us up the slope. We skim on slender bridges over deep, narrow gorges; we wind along cliffs over which leap sparkling waterfalls; we catch glimpses of green mountains and swift streams; and in pleasant valleys banana trees wave their immense leaves and coffee trees grow in long rows. At the top of the incline we change engines again and run along a few miles farther to São Paulo.

Fig. 31 shows you a small part of São Paulo. It is a beautiful city, with fine buildings, splendid schools, 76

FIG. 31. This is a scene in the business section of São Paulo. The tall building at the left is said to be the largest building in South America. (Photograph by Lionel Green)

SOUTH AMERICA 

smooth roads and tree-lined boulevards, and parks lovely with ponds, gardens, trees, and velvety grass. 

All these things have been made possible chiefly by one product coffee. Brazil is the world's great coffee country. Coffee is its most important product and its most valuable export. Indeed, the coffee shipped from Brazil's ports is worth between two and three times as much as all the other exports put together. 

In the state of São Paulo alone there are more than a billion coffee trees, and in other states, principally Minas Geraes, Rio de Janeiro, and Espirito Santo, where the soil and climate are suitable, there are millions more. 

Several coffee estates have from three to five million trees each. Wouldn't you like to visit such wonderful plantations?

Several railroads lead out from the city of São Paulo into the neighboring country. The soil, of a deep reddish color, and the hillsides green with long rows of coffee trees make the scene a lovely one. We can ride for hours on the red roads and see on either side nothing but trim, glossy-green coffee trees. Some are full-grown and pruned back to a height of about ten or twelve feet; some are younger and slenderer; and still others, recently planted, are only a foot or two high. A well-kept farm in any country is always a fine sight, but a coffee estate is especially beautiful. 

Between the younger trees on many of the plantations there are rows of corn. Next to coffee, corn is the leading crop of Brazil, and we see it growing in many places. There is a great difference, however, between these two important crops. Almost all the corn is used in Brazil, and so corn is not an important

COFFEE, THE MOST IMPORTANT PRODUCT 79 

export. Most of the coffee, on the other hand, is shipped to other lands.

Three or four times a year the coffee trees are covered with small white flowers which fill the air with their fragrance. Soon the blossoms fade and drop, and the berries begin to form. Try to picture a tree with shiny green leaves, hung thick with clusters of berries which look like dark, short-stemmed cherries.

Imagine sloping hillsides, broad valleys, and level fields of brick-red soil covered as far as you can see with thousands of such trees. Then perhaps you can realize something of the beauty of a coffee plantation when the berries are ripe. On page 13 you saw a picture of a coffee plantation. Look at it again and imagine all these colors in it.

High on the slopes of a beautiful valley, with a fine view spread out before it, is the house of the owner of the plantation. You can see a picture of it on page 80. 

Some of the owners of large coffee plantations are extremely wealthy; their children are educated abroad, their homes are palaces, and their lives are filled with every luxury.

Besides the owner's mansion there are long rows of cottages for the laborers, and barns for the horses, mules, and oxen used on the estate. There are blacksmith shops, machine shops, sheds for the farm machinery, a bakery, drug store, and sawmill, besides the buildings where the coffee is prepared for market, and the warehouses where it is stored. 

Most of the workmen are Italians. Many live here the year round, and thousands more come from Italy each year. In recent years many Japanese have come

SOUTH AMERICA 

here to find work. Used to working on the farms in 

Japan, they make good laborers on the coffee plantations. 

A worker contracts to care for a certain number of trees- two thousand to three thousand, or even more, depending on how many people there are in his family FIG. 32. This is the house of a Brazilian coffee-grower. Is it not a beautiful place? to help. The pay depends on the number of trees included in the contract. A good deal of hoeing and weeding is necessary, for weeds and grass grow rapidly in this warm region with its abundant rainfall. In the picking season, especially, everybody in the family is busy. At that time extra laborers are hired also, just as they are in our wheat belt at harvest time. 

The laborers pick the fruit from the trees or shake it onto large sheets of cloth spread on the ground. The

COFFEE, THE MOST IMPORTANT PRODUCT 81 

berries are put into large baskets and carried to waiting carts or cars which take them to the buildings where they are prepared for market.

In the center of each berry, buried in a soft pulp, are two beans lying with their flat sides together, FIG. 33. Harvest time on the coffee plantations in the state of São Paulc begins in May and lasts until October. During this period thousands of men, women, and children work on the plantations picking the berries from the trees. (Courtesy of the Brazilian-American Coffee Promotion Committee) somewhat as the two halves of a peanut lie. The fruit is first put into machines which mash the pulp and wash it away. The beans are then spread out in large paved yards to dry in the hot sunshine. This drying process usually takes about three weeks. Workmen with long rakes turn the beans over from time to time so that all may be thoroughly dried. If a shower comes up in the daytime, the beans are hastily cov-

SOUTH AMERICA 

ered. At night they are raked up in heaps and covered, to protect them from the dew or rain.

perfect beans and allow the good ones to drop through holes in the table into bags tied underneath. When the bags are full they are taken to still other workers, who sew them up in readiness for being shipped away. 

Publishers' Photo Service 

FIG. 35. Some of the coffee that is shipped away from Santos is loaded by man power, as in the picture, and some is loaded by machinery Even now the coffee is not ready to use, and you would not recognize in these greenish-gray berries the brown coffee beans with which you are familiar. They must be roasted to turn them dark and give them a good flavor. This is not done on the plantations, as the beans keep better and make a better drink if they are not roasted too long before they are used.

The wealth of the state of São Paulo has come largely from the enormous amount of coffee produced

SOUTH AMERICA 

there. Thousands of bags of coffee beans are brought in from the estates around the city of São Paulo to be sent down to Santos by railroad. In Santos nearly everyone is engaged in work connected with coffee. 

There are coffee brokers, coffee testers, and coffee tasters. In the big warehouses along the water front, laborers clean, sort, grade, and pack the beans. Coffee agents from foreign countries are at work in their offices. Trucks and wagons rattle through the streets, and drivers wait patiently while others block the way.

Bags of coffee flow in a seemingly endless stream down through long spouts from the warehouses to the steamers at the wharves.

Farmers in the coffee-growing region of Brazil raise other crops besides coffee. Corn, you remember, is one of the most important crops of the country. Beans, rice, potatoes, and other grains and vegetables are grown for food. Many farmers raise cattle and send them to the slaughterhouses and meat-packing plants in Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo. From both these cities meat and hides are important exports. The farmers in the coffee regions of Brazil, like those in the cotton and wheat belts of the United States, are learning that it is more profitable to engage in mixed farming than it is to use all their land for a single crop.

The coffee plant is a native of Ethiopia, in Africa. From this country the plant was introduced into Arabia, in western Asia, and later into Europe. From France some coffee seeds were carried to French Guiana, in South America, and from this colony a Portuguese sailor, in 1727, carried some seeds to Brazil. This was the beginning of the coffee industry in 

Brazil. In 1927 a celebration was held in the city of São 

Paulo to commemorate the beginning of this important industry. What anniversary of the industry was this?

CHAPTER VIII  

Brazil has other important industries besides those connected with the production of cacao and coffee. 

On the map opposite page 32 find the port of Recife, which is the capital of the state of Pernambuco. On the coastal plain in which this city is located so much sugar cane is grown that Pernambuco is one of the great sugar states of Brazil, producing about a third of the country's total. On some of the hundreds of plantations here the work is carried on by hand as it has been for years, but on many others the producers are now using modern machinery. So important is the sugar industry of the state that there are many sugar mills in Recife.

The cotton plantations in the state of Pernambuco would remind us of those in our Southern states. 

The Negro pickers, with their tall baskets filled with fluffy white balls of cotton, would almost make us think that we were on a cotton plantation in Louisiana or Texas; and the cotton mills in Recife, with their noisy looms and flying shuttles, are much like the cotton mills in our own country. Brazil has many mills where cotton, sugar, tobacco, and other products are manufactured. Every year more attention is being given to manufacturing, especially to cottonmanufacturing. It is better for the country to make 87

SOUTH AMERICA 

its own cotton into cloth than to send the raw material on long voyages to foreign nations and then buy from them the finished product.

Recife is one of the leading ports of Brazil, and of South America as well. One of the chief reasons for FIG. 36. Recife consists of a peninsula, a portion of the mainland, and an island between the two. All three parts are connected with one another by bridges. Why is the name "the Venice of Brazil" a good one for the city? its importance is its location on a number of ocean steamship routes. If you will locate Recife on a map of the world, you will see that it is the most easterly port of South America and that it lies in the path of ships sailing between the Atlantic-coast ports of the United States and Rio de Janeiro and Buenos Aires, in South America. Many of the steamers traveling between these South American cities and European ports make Recife a port of call.

· Another reason for the development of Recife has to do with its location in and near a productive region. 

In the higher lands a little distance in from the coast are some of the best farm lands of Brazil. Railroads have been built from Recife out into this region, and trains bring to the port sugar, tobacco, fruit, cotton, coffee, and other products.

Recife was formerly called Pernambuco from the name of the state. Recife means "reef," and the name was given to the city because of a low reef which extends along the shore here about a mile from the land. 

This reef rises a few feet above the water, forming a natural breakwater which protects the inner harbor of the city from the rough waters of the open ocean. 

Unfortunately, however, the reef, while protecting the inner harbor, for many years kept large steamers from entering it. Cargoes and passengers had to be transferred to small boats which could make their way through an opening in the reef. It was a thrilling experience for passengers to be lowered in a basket from the steamer to the boat. This is a common method of handling passengers in many ports of the world where for one reason or another large steamers cannot reach the docks.

In recent years a deeper opening has been blasted in the reef so that large ships can enter the inner harbor. On the top of the reef engineers have built a higher breakwater, and while waves dash, and foam and spray fly in sheets on its outer side, the inside harbor is calm and quiet. Warehouses line the water front of Recife, and, with its breakwater and its modern docks and wharves, the city is an up-to-date seaport.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Recife is in the path of some of the main air routes of eastern South America. To the north, on the coast, is the town of Natal, which is the chief airport of Brazil and the terminus of several important air lines. Here there are airplane hangars, as well as radio and wireless towers, a large flying field, and a school for aviators. 

The network of air routes in eastern South America, over which planes carry mail, passengers, and freight, centers at Natal. French, German, and Italian air companies have routes to South America by way of Dakar, on the westernmost point of Africa, and thence across the Atlantic to Natal. Much of their service has been suspended, however, since the outbreak of the war in Europe. American air companies operate routes along the coast and into the interior of Brazil, as well as between Florida and Brazil; and Brazilian companies also operate coastal and interior lines. 

Southern Brazil is in the temperate zone, and is often called the white man's land because its climate is more suitable for white men than is that of any other part of the country. The rainfall is heavy enough for farming, and the surface consists of rolling fertile plains, much like our prairie lands. Many people from 

Germany, Spain, and Italy have settled in southern Brazil, and there are fewer Indians and Negroes than in the warmer regions farther north. Most of the people carry on mixed farming much as do those in our south-central and Gulf states. They raise coffee, cotton, and rice, and many fruits and vegetables. So many grapes are produced in Rio Grande do Sul, the southernmost state of Brazil, that it is sometimes called the vineyard of Brazil. Find this state on the map

FIG. 37. These rice fields are in the state of São Paulo, in southern Brazil. There are many acres of irrigated rice lands in this part of the country. Much of the rice is exported to European countries

SOUTH AMERICA 

opposite page 32. On many farms there are cattle and hogs and large fields of wheat and corn. In recent years wheat production has increased in Brazil and more flour mills have been built, but as yet the country does not produce enough wheat or manufacture enough flour for its needs. Much of the wheat and wheat flour that is imported comes from the United States. 

More than a third of all the cattle of South America are in Brazil, many of them in the southern part of the country. Many people think that the advantages for raising cattle in southern Brazil are greater than on the ranching areas in the United States, Argentina, Australia, and other cattle-producing lands. The highlands of the state of Minas Geraes, south of Bahia, and those of the states farther south are the best cattle-raising lands of the country. Locate Minas Geraes on the map opposite page 32. The first settlements in this state were made by miners and men searching for mineral wealth, just as they were in parts of western United States; but, as in some of our Western states, many settlers found greater wealth in the rich soil and good pasture lands than in the mineral resources. Millions of cattle are now raised in Rio Grande do Sul, Minas Geraes, and adjoining states, and there is room for millions more on the uncounted acres of pasture lands. 

Minas Geraes, São Paulo, Rio de Janeiro, Rio Grande do Sul, and Santa Catharina are the chief dairying states of Brazil. The cattle there supply most of the country with butter and cheese, and Rio de Janeiro and other cities with milk.

On the big ranches in the cattle-raising states the work is carried on much as it was in early days in

Texas and other cattle states in our country. There are no fenced pastures. The animals roam freely for miles and are rounded up once a year by the cowboys, who drive great herds on a long and tiresome trip over the roadless regions to pastures farther east. After FIG. 38. Herds of cattle like this one in the state of Rio Grande do Sul are a common sight in the higher lands of central and southern Brazil the cattle arrive at their destination they are fed for several weeks on corn and other fattening foods, just as is done in our Central States. Then they are shipped by rail to packing plants in the seaports. There are large establishments in both Rio de Janeiro and São 

Paulo for slaughtering cattle, chilling and freezing meat, and preparing other cattle products. More recently such plants have been built in the coastal cities of the states farther south. 

Brazil's animal exports used to consist of hides and

SOUTH AMERICA 

dried, salted meat called jerked beef. Hides are still exported in large quantities, but because of the modern packing plants, ice plants, and refrigerating systems that have been established, the meat exports of Brazil now consist largely of frozen and chilled beef such as is prepared by packing companies in Chicago and other large cities in the United States. 

Farming and ranching are not the only occupations in southern Brazil. On the hills and slopes are forests of pine trees, and lumbering is becoming more and more important as the region becomes more thickly settled. Large quantities of lumber are exported to 

Uruguay and Argentina, for these countries have few forest areas. Much is used also in Rio de Janeiro and other Brazilian cities.

The name Minas Geraes has a meaning that tells us an interesting fact about the products of that state. 

This name means "all kinds of mines." It might well have been given to Bahia too, for the mineral deposits of that state are nearly as rich as those of its neighbor. 

Brazil used to be famous for its diamonds. Some are mined in Minas Geraes and Bahia today and exported from São Salvador, but the industry is not nearly so important as it was in former years. Brazil and British 

Guiana are the only important diamond-producing countries in South America at the present time. Most of the world's supply of diamonds, however, comes from southern and central Africa.

The most important mineral product of Minas Geraes is manganese. This metal, you know, is used in the manufacture of steel and for this reason has become of great importance. Not one of the countries which

manufacture large quantities of steel produces nearly enough manganese to supply its needs. The United 

States buys the greater part of all the manganese mined in Brazil, most of which is shipped to us from Rio de FIG. 39. On page 77 you saw a picture of the business center of São Paulo with its tall, modern buildings. This is a view of one of the sections of the city where manufacturing is carried on. São Paulo is the second largest city of Brazil and one of its leading manufacturing centers. (Photograph from R. I. Nesmith and Associates)

Janeiro. We have to import manganese also from other parts of the world, for Brazil does not furnish enough for our needs.

The state of São Paulo carries on more manufacturing than any other Brazilian state. Perhaps one reason for this is because of its coal deposits, which are the most important in Brazil. Although the cattle industry still holds first place in Rio Grande do Sul, manufacturing

SOUTH AMERICA 

has increased enormously there in recent years, especially in connection with such industries as the making of animal products, shoes, and cotton, woolen, and silk goods. There are canneries where the fruits and vegetables of the region are preserved, sawmills where lumber is manufactured, and factories where furniture and other wooden articles are made. 

As manufacturing becomes more important in Brazil, imports of machinery and iron and steel are increasing. A large share of these products is furnished by the manufacturing plants of the United States. Brazil buys from us in an average year millions of dollars' worth of automobiles and trucks, many farm tools and machines, and sewing machines, typewriters, and motion-picture films. We also send this southern neighbor of ours large quantities of gasoline and kerosene, and oils and grease for lubricating machinery.

CHAPTER IX RIO DE JANEIRO, FAMOUS CAPITAL OF A FAMOUS COUNTRY 

Trace a voyage southward from São Salvador to Rio de Janeiro on the map opposite page 32. The name 

Rio de Janeiro means "River of January." The first

Portuguese explorer who sailed up the bay on which the city is located thought that it was the mouth of a broad river and named it for the month in which he reached it. Later it was found that no large river entered the ocean here, but the name still clung to the bay and to the settlement which was made there. The people of Brazil usually call their capital city by the shorter name of Rio.

Rio is the largest city in Brazil and the second largest in South America. It is considered by many people the world's most beautiful seaport, for it occupies the shore, climbs the hills, and presses against the mountains which lie just behind it. The mountains and hills, the tropical trees and flowers, the blue sky, and the bluer ocean all contribute to the beauty of the city.

In the picture on page 99 notice old Sugar Loaf, the mountain which guards the entrance to the bay. 

Rising out of the water are other famous peaks. If we were to visit Rio, there are two things we should do. 

One is to go to the top of Sugar Loaf by the air trolley which you see in Fig. 41; the other is to climb Cor- 98

FIG. 40. Imagine how beautiful Rio must be, with the blue sky and the bluer water, the green hills and mountains, and the white clouds. The tall peak near the center of the picture is Sugar Loaf. (Photograph by Lionel Green)

SOUTH AMERICA 

covado by the electric cogwheel railway that zigzags up its side and enjoy the beautiful view from the top. This mountain is twenty-three hundred feet high and is the highest peak near the city. On all sides save one it is much too steep to climb. The electric cogwheel FIG. 41. The easiest way to get to the top of Sugar Loaf is by means of this cable car. What a thrilling trip it must be! Pacific Railway) railway has been built up the more slanting side to the summit. The name Corcovado means "hunchback." 

The view of Rio from the harbor is beautiful, but the outlook from the top of Corcovado is even more so. The blue waters of the bay, dotted with green islands, sparkle in the bright sunlight. The many boats with gleaming white sails look like huge tropical birds skimming over the water. Beyond the harbor

RIO DE JANEIRO 

101 stretches the wide expanse of ocean, until in the far distance the sky drops down to meet the water. Near the shore Sugar Loaf rises a thousand feet in height, its green cone standing out in sharp relief against the blue water. At its feet, surrounded by tropical foliage, are the fine streets and beautiful buildings of this famous capital of a famous country. 

One place in Rio which no tourist should miss seeing is the Botanical Gardens. Leading into the gardens is an avenue of royal palms, each about one hundred feet tall. On either side rise these tall, straight columns of silvery gray, each topped with a huge green tuft of long, waving leaves which cast flickering shadows on the smooth walk beneath. In the gardens is a single huge palm tree taller than any in the avenue and more than a century old. It is called the "mother of palms," because all the trees originally planted in the avenue came from its seeds.

There are other paths in these gardens through which we might like to stroll. Many people think that the avenues where the feathery fronds of giant bamboos meet in a graceful arch overhead are as beautiful as the palm walk.

The Botanical Gardens in Rio occupy more than two thousand acres and contain more than fifty thousand kinds of plants and trees. Many of these grow in Brazil, and others have been brought from countries in different parts of the world. There are spice-bearing trees such as cinnamon, nutmeg, and clove. There are the curious cow trees, which, on being tapped, yield a fluid that looks and tastes somewhat like milk. Lovely orchids, which are raised at great expense in the hothouses

SOUTH AMERICA 

of the North, bloom here in profusion. Tall poinsettias, with their huge scarlet leaves, would make us think of our Christmas decorations. Huge tree ferns are taller than a two-story house, and trees two hundred feet high are covered with brilliant blossoms. 

Long vines hang like green clotheslines from some of the big trees and wind around their trunks. Scattered through the gardens are many varieties of rubber trees, among them the tall Hevea, which made Brazil such a famous rubber-producer.

No capital in the world contains finer buildings than does Rio. Fig. 42 gives you an idea of what a fine, modern city it is. Its opera house is perhaps more beautiful than any building of the kind in the United States. Nearly four hundred trains enter and leave the splendid railroad station each day. Of special interest to people from the United States is the famous Monroe Palace, built for the Brazilian exhibit at the World's Fair at St. Louis. After the fair was over, the building was taken apart and carried to Rio, and again erected in that city.

Extending along the water front is a stone quay more than two miles in length. Back of this sea wall, on land which not many years ago was washed by the tide, a splendid palm-shaded avenue three hundred feet wide has been built, on which are fine blocks of warehouses and office buildings. On this broad street there is plenty of room for all the traffic which is carried on along the busy water front. The street is wide enough for railroad tracks, electric-car tracks, and driveways for wagons, trucks, and lighter vehicles. 

On the docks and in the warehouses is modern machin-

1 ery for loading and unloading ships. As we go about the water front we begin to realize, as never before, that Rio is one of the great commercial cities of the world. Ships come and go, bringing passengers and freight from every part of the world and carrying away to distant lands the products of Brazil.

There are many automobiles in the city and many beautiful drives to take in them. The Avenida Rio 

Branco, more than a mile long and a hundred and eight feet wide, runs through the heart of the city from the water on one side to the water on the other side. Several rows of trees add to the beauty of the street, and splendid business blocks, hotels, and public buildings line it on either side. 

Beira Mar is another beautiful avenue which runs along the shore where once were swamps and lowlands. 

The blue waters of the bay on one side, and the green hills covered with beautiful homes and backed by greener mountains on the other side, make the Beira 

Mar one of the loveliest boulevards in the world.

If we should visit Rio in the summer season, we should find the city very hot, and we should prefer to stay in some of the pleasant resorts in the mountains or along the bay. One of the coolest and most interesting of these places is Petropolis, a suburb located high on the mountain slopes a few miles from Rio. 

Here during the hot season many government officials, representatives of foreign countries, and wealthy people live. We could drive on broad, shady avenues through the middle of which, in stone-curbed channels, flow streams of clear mountain water. We could wander for hours in winding paths through the deep woods,

RIO DE JANEIRO 

105 and ride to the tops of some of the peaks, where we could enjoy the cool breezes and lovely views. The beautiful residences are surrounded by gardens filled with flowers larger and more brilliant than those which bloom in our gardens at home. There are hedges of dainty heliotrope, beds of tall, waving lilies, orange trees filled with luscious fruit, and tall geranium bushes with their gay blossoms.

There are other interesting sights in and near 

Rio, very different from gardens, parks, boulevards, and fashionable resorts. There are several cottonmanufacturing plants here, in one of which two or three thousand people are employed. We could visit shoe factories, flour mills, and establishments where furniture, hats, leather, paper, and other things are made. Manufacturing is growing rapidly in Rio; but no matter how many industrial plants may be built there or how noted the city may become for its manufactures, it will always be famous for its beauty.

CHAPTER X VENEZUELA, ONE OF OUR NEAREST SOUTH AMERICAN NEIGHBORS 

Venezuela lies in the extreme northern part of South America. We are especially interested in this country because it is our nearest neighbor in South America. 

We carry on more trade with the people there than does any other country in the world. There are consuls and other representatives of the Venezuelan government in many of the large cities of the United States. 

Venezuela is as large as all the states which lie on the western border of the Mississippi River, Minnesota, Iowa, Missouri, Arkansas, and Louisiana,-together with Wisconsin and half of Illinois on the eastern side. This northern country of South America lies entirely in the torrid zone, but there is as much difference between the climates of its low coast lands and its higher plateaus and mountains as there is between the climates of Florida and Virginia. The crops of the different parts of the country differ also. 

Venezuela has fewer people than live in the state of Missouri. It has only a few cities and towns, and most of these are near the coast. There are large areas where there are no prosperous farms, no roads, no railroads, and no settlements. In some parts of the country there are regions where you might travel for days without meeting a person or seeing a house or a village. 107

SOUTH AMERICA 

In previous chapters you have read about the Amazon River and the plains through which it flows. In the northern part of the continent is another large river, the Orinoco. This is bordered by deep forests, FIG. 43. There are few good roads in Venezuela, and goods are for the most part transported by boats, by mule pack trains, or by two-wheeled carts drawn by oxen. The coconuts being loaded on the cart in the picture will be taken to the nearest port. What do the wheels of the cart tell you about the condition of the roads in this vicinity? (By Thomas F. Lee, from 

Ewing Galloway, N.Y.) beyond which are many miles of grassy plains. Find this river on the regional map. Does it flow in the same direction as the Amazon or in a different direction?

The Orinoco is one of the world's great rivers. Together with its branches, it opens up the interior of Venezuela to trade and communication with other countries. Some day this great stream will be as important

VENEZUELA 

109 to Venezuela as the Amazon is to Brazil or as the Mississippi is to the central part of the United States. 

About a third of Venezuela lies in the plains around the Orinoco River. These are low, hot lands called the llanos. They extend west to the mountains of northern Venezuela and south into Colombia. In the dry season in the llanos the heat of the tropical sun is so great that the grass withers and dries. In the wet season there are showers nearly every day, the lowest lands are flooded, and the grass in other areas grows green and tall. It was in the wet season that early 

Spanish explorers, sailing near the coast of Venezuela, saw the natives going about in boats and noticed that their huts were raised on piles in order to be out of reach of the water. The explorers were reminded of Venice and its streets of water, and so they called the land Venezuela, which means "little Venice." The name, however, is not at all suitable for most of the country.

The regional map shows you that in the interior. of 

Venezuela and in the north and west are highland areas. What is the name of the highlands in the interior? To what system do the mountains in the north and west belong? The interior lands are hot, the swift streams are not navigable, and the hilly land is not good for farming. Under such conditions few people make their homes in this part of Venezuela, and few industries of importance are carried on there. 

Forests cover nearly half of Venezuela. They are made up of many kinds of hardwood trees which will some day be as highly prized as mahogany and ebony are today. As in the selvas of Brazil, some of the trees

SOUTH AMERICA 

yield substances useful in the tanning of leather and in making dyes and medicine. Scattered through the forests are thousands of rubber trees also. 

Several million cattle find their food on the tropical pastures of the Orinoco Lowlands, but a hundred times as many could easily find a supply of feed there. 

At present the ranchmen of the llanos let their cattle run wild and pay little attention to improving the breed. They round them up once a year and sell them for what they will bring. These methods of ranching are much like those in use in early times in the western part of our own country. In large areas of the llanos the only means of shipping cattle and cattle products away and bringing in provisions to the settlers is by means of the Orinoco River and its branches. 

Venezuela has a valuable resource in the water power of its rivers. With mountains in both the north and the south causing the streams to flow rapidly, the country has an abundance of power which can be used to generate electricity. Already the larger cities have plants to supply electric light and power. Much more power for manufacturing can easily be obtained in the future. 

Ciudad Bolivar is the only city of importance on the Orinoco River. It is two hundred and thirty miles from the Atlantic Ocean, but the river is deep enough to allow ocean steamers to sail up to the docks of the city. Beyond Ciudad Bolivar smaller vessels go up the river and its branches for long distances and cross the border into Colombia. Some products from that country reach the ocean by this route. 

Ciudad Bolivar is the distributing center for an immense area. What do you think is meant by a distrib-

Among the imports received at the city are automobiles and trucks, barbed wire, canned goods, drugs, iron and steel manufactures, kerosene, and flour. Most of these imports come from the United States. Can you tell why such goods are needed in a region like the interior of Venezuela? 

Sir Walter Raleigh was the first white man to sail up the Orinoco to the place where the city of Ciudad Bolivar now stands. This was in 1595. He was hunting for gold, but was obliged to turn back before he found it. Since that time large quantities of this precious metal have been taken from the deposits for which he was searching. 

Among the products shipped away from Ciudad 

Bolivar are bags of tonka beans. These beans contain a substance used in the manufacture of perfume, flavoring extracts, and some tobacco products. The trees grow near the Orinoco and other rivers, and the natives collect the beans after they have dropped from the trees. They are brought down the rivers to Ciudad 

Bolivar and from there are sent to the island of Trinidad, at the mouth of the Orinoco. Here they are dried and otherwise prepared for use. Practically all the world's supply of tonka beans is exported from this little island.

The coast line of Venezuela is so long that if it were stretched out in a straight line it would reach across the United States from New York City to Great Salt

Lake in Utah. This long stretch of coast forms most of the southern boundary of the Caribbean Sea. From

113 the water one can see the beautiful coast ranges which extend from east to west through the country. In places steep slopes rise out of the tropical forest near the shore. In other places the mountains are farther back and the slope of the land is more gradual. Some of the peaks of these coast ranges rise a mile above sea level and are landmarks for sailors far out on the water. 

In the high valley among these ranges are most of the important cities and towns of Venezuela. Why do you think people prefer to live there rather than on the low coast?

Along the coast there are thirty or more ports, at any one of which we could see products of the country being shipped away and goods which the people need being unloaded from ships which have come from distant countries. La Guaira is the largest of the seaports and the one nearest to the capital city. It lies on the narrow lowland facing the blue Caribbean. The redtiled roofs of the buildings rise out of a mass of tropical foliage. Close behind the city the green hills rise into wooded mountains, the peaks of which stand out bright and clear against the blue sky.

La Guaira is the gateway of central Venezuela. 

Products from the forests, farms, and ranches of the interior are brought by rail and mule carts to Caracas, the capital city, which lies in a green mountain valley behind La Guaira. From Caracas the products are sent down the twisting, curving railroad line to La Guaira or down the wonderful concrete highway that connects the two cities.

Most of the people in La Guaira are engaged in some work connected with the railroad, the shipping

SOUTH AMERICA 

of the exports, and the receiving of imports. The busiest and most interesting part of the city is along the water front. If we were to look in the warehouses, on the wharves, and in the vessels tied up at the docks, we should find out what some of the products of Vene- 

FIG. 45. This train is about to leave La Guaira for Caracas. The twenty three-mile trip over the mountains takes about an hour. (Photograph from R. I. Nesmith and Associates) zuela are and what kinds of work many of the people do. There are more bags of coffee than anything else, for coffee is Venezuela's most important crop and one of its most valuable exports. There are other bags filled with cacao beans and sugar, and there are also great bundles of hides from the cattle ranches on the plains. Much of the cacao and sugar is raised on the low, hot coastal plain, but the coffee plantations are on the higher, cooler lands. We are especially interested

VENEZUELA 

115 in these products, for the United States buys more of them than does any other country. 

It is very hot on the low plains near the shore and even hotter in the streets of La Guaira. There is no winter season here, and the temperature is high the year round. Visitors are glad when they can board a train which will take them up through the mountains to Caracas, the capital. The valley in which Caracas lies is so high and the breezes which blow down from the mountains are so refreshing that the climate is never so uncomfortably hot as it is in La Guaira. 

The trip up to the capital is full of thrills. The distance in a straight line between the two cities is only eight miles, but the climb is so steep that the railroad twists and turns for twenty-three miles before reaching the city. It crosses bridges, runs through tunnels, and creeps along steep cliffs where one can look down hundreds of feet into deep canyons. It rounds curves so sharp that passengers can look across narrow gorges into the cab of the engineer of the train. Looking back, they can see La Guaira in its setting of green with the blue Caribbean before it. 

From the highest point on the railroad, three thousand feet above sea level, one can get a glimpse of the beautiful valley, surrounded by mountains, in which Caracas lies. The low houses, the tree-lined streets, the fine public buildings, the parks and gardens and statues, and the La Guaira River with its many bridges make the capital an attractive place. The capitol building is large and imposing and contains many fine paintings. Miraflores, the palace where the president of Venezuela lives, is a beautiful home. Large foreign

Publishers Photo Service, New York 

FIG. 46.

Caracas is beautifully located high among the mountains and has a healthful climate

VENEZUELA 

117 banks have branches in Caracas, and there are fine schools and colleges. The people of the city are well educated and cultured. There are many foreigners here,― Germans, Italians, English, and people from the United States and other countries. Some of the people from the United States are engaged in the petroleum, coffee, sugar, and other industries.

Most of the houses in Caracas are one-story buildings. They have no lawns or gardens in front of them as many of the houses in our country have, but are built in Spanish style around a central courtyard called a patio. The walls of the houses come close to the edge of the pavements and make the narrow streets seem even narrower than they really are. If we could enter through the heavy street doors and go into the patios, we should find them in many cases beautiful with gardens, flowers, fountains, and trees.

Caracas is laid out in checkerboard form, with long, straight streets running north, south, east, and west.

In some places where the streets cross there are beautiful squares, or plazas, laid out with gardens and fountains. Nearly every plaza contains a statue of some national hero or famous statesman. The Plaza Bolivar, in the center of the city, is the largest and finest of all. 

It is surrounded by the big cathedral, the post office, the Palace of Justice, hotels, and other fine buildings. It is paved with tiles, and on festive nights hundreds of electric lights hang from the trees, many of which are covered with beautiful flowers. In the center of the square is a bronze statue of Simon Bolivar, the man who helped Venezuela and other South American countries to win their independence from Spain.

SOUTH AMERICA 

FIG. 47. In cities all over South America most of the better houses are built around a patio. Here the people enjoy themselves, protected from the eyes of passers-by and from the noise and traffic of the street. (Photograph from R. I. Nesmith and Associates)

In another plaza in Caracas, shaded by tall trees and surrounded by gardens, is a statue of George Washington. Simon Bolivar loved and admired our first president, and the people of Caracas honored the famous friend of their hero by erecting the statue and naming the square the Plaza Washington. In some of the public buildings of the city are hung the familiar

VENEZUELA 

119 pictures of "Washington Crossing the Delaware," "Washington on Horseback," and "Washington at 

Mount Vernon."

There are many plantations in Venezuela where cotton is raised, for the climate and soil are well suited to its growth. In Caracas there are several large mills where the fiber is made into cloth. There are also tobacco and cigarette factories which use about half of all the tobacco that the farmers of Venezuela raise. In other manufacturing establishments in and near the city are made paper, shoes, cordage, cement, chocolate, matches, soap, furniture, and other things. As in most other South American countries, manufacturing has increased in recent years.

Of course much machinery is needed in the mills. and factories. This is usually imported from foreign countries, most of it coming from our own United States. We send Venezuela also, as we do other countries in South America, typewriters, sewing machines, cash registers, automobiles and trucks, and many other manufactured products.

There are fine stores in Caracas, but many of the people shop in the big open market. There are more people and a greater variety of goods there on Sunday than on any other day. All sorts of products have been brought in from the country around in mule carts or oxcarts and on the backs of mules and donkeys. 

There are chickens, ducks, pigs, sheep, cheeses, vegetables, and fruit. During the night the merchants have been arranging their piles of goods, and over their little fires of charcoal the women have been making candies, sweetmeats, and savory soups. In the early

SOUTH AMERICA 

morning the plaza and all the streets leading to it are crowded with people and donkeys. Merchants bargain over their produce, pigs and hens are killed for the 

Sunday dinner, and the lottery-ticket venders cry to the people around to improve the last chance to pur- 

Publishers' Photo Service, New York 

FIG. 48. In the market at Caracas women haggle over the fruit, vegetable, and meat stands, driving good bargains with the merchants chase tickets for the great prize which is to be drawn later in the day. You can buy almost anything in this city market, from books and old bottles to lovely flowers and second-hand clothing. In the bird section you can have your choice of talkative parrots, mocking birds, shy brown thrushes, and tiny brilliant humming birds.

The valley in which Caracas lies is green with vegetable gardens and many large groves of orange and lemon trees. Much of the land is irrigated, and so the

VENEZUELA 

121 farmers can raise crops in the dry season, thus making their farms productive during the entire year. 

Large fields of sugar cane stretch away toward the mountains which surround the valley. Sugar cane is raised generally in Venezuela, except on the highlands, where it is too cold. On the big plantations near the cities there are modern mills and machinery as in the United States. On smaller farms in more remote country regions little machinery is used. The cane is brought from the fields in oxcarts, and the tall stalks are crushed between rollers worked by oxen. As the animals walk slowly round and round, the juice drips out into vessels under the rollers. This is boiled down into sirup over fires made of dried cane or wood. 

The sirup is poured into shallow wooden troughs and stirred until it is as thick as mud. Then it is turned into cone-shaped molds and allowed to harden. White sugar such as we use is common in the cities of Venezuela, but the country people use these brown coneshaped loaves. The production of sugar is one of the industries of Venezuela which has grown rapidly in recent years, and considerable quantities are now sent to the United States.

The coffee plantations in Venezeula are as beautiful as those which we saw in Brazil. Half of the cargoes of the vessels that sail from Venezuelan ports to foreign countries consists of coffee. This is another crop which has increased greatly in the past few years in Venezuela and even more in the neighboring country of Colombia. Large amounts of coffee from both these countries are now used in the United States. There are at present several million coffee trees on the plan-

SOUTH AMERICA 

tations of Venezuela. The soil is rich and the climate favorable; and as the country develops and methods of farming improve, more trees will probably be planted and more coffee raised. The average Venezuelan farmer FIG. 49. About a fifth of the people of Venezuela are engaged in farming. 

What things in this Venezuelan farm scene tell you that the land there is excellent for farming? The stream is an irrigation canal. Do you think that irrigation is necessary all the year round? His mean little hut has few articles of furniture, his tools are few and simple, and he knows little of machinery or of modern ways of working.

Southward from Caracas a road that winds over hills and through valleys leads through a district where cacao plantations flourish as well as they do on the

VENEZUELA 

123 coastal plain. The big brown pods hang thick on the trees, and on the road are many carts loaded with cacao beans. They are brought to Caracas and from there sent by rail down to La Guaira for export. 

From Caracas a railroad runs westward through the valley between the mountain ranges to Valencia, another interesting and growing city. The trip takes one between fields of tall, waving sugar cane, past acres of coffee plantations and groves of orange and lemon trees, near pastures where cattle are feeding, and through tropical forests, where in cleared spaces are little native villages.

Valencia is one of the important cities of Venezuela. 

It was formerly the capital of the country. There are large cotton mills here and mills where cottonseed oil is prepared for use in the soap factories and for other purposes. From the city a railroad runs to the coast, between thirty and forty miles away. Coffee, sugar, and cattle are brought from the plantations and ranches to Valencia and shipped from there down to Puerto 

Cabello, the seaport at the end of the railroad line. 

What queer names some of these old Spanish cities have! The Spaniard who first sailed into the harbor of Puerto Cabello found it so well protected that he gave it the name which means "the port of a hair," signifying that its waters were so quiet that vessels anchored by a hair would not break their moorings.

The harbor is one of the finest in the world, and the city is very picturesque, lying at the water's edge on a long, narrow peninsula, with mountains rising behind and a stretch of shining beach in front. At one time, when Valencia was the capital of Venezuela, Puerto

SOUTH AMERICA 

Cabello was the chief port, and most of the commerce was carried on there.

Find on the regional map the large gulf and lake in the western part of Venezuela. What are their names?

If you look closely at the map, you will see that Lake Maracaibo is really not a lake at all, but the southern part of the Gulf of Venezuela. It is separated from that body of water by a narrow strait like the neck of a bottle. 

In the country surrounding Lake Maracaibo are more coffee and cacao plantations and acres of fertile soil covered with the big leaves of the tobacco plant and with tall stalks of sugar cane. In the mountains are gold, copper, lead, and coal. With a strong government, good roads, and money to open mines, these deposits will some day yield large quantities of valuable minerals. 

Around Lake Maracaibo and in other parts of Venezuela another mineral has been found which is at present more valuable than any other. This is petroleum. 

So much of it is produced here that Venezuela now ranks among the leading petroleum-producing countries of the world. Large companies are operating in the 

Lake Maracaibo section and carrying on explorations and developments in other parts of the country. Pipe lines carry petroleum from the wells to the port of Maracaibo, where some of the crude oil is shipped to foreign countries. Large quantities, however, are sent to the two little neighboring Dutch islands of Aruba and Curaçao to be refined. Then the purer oils and other products obtained in the refining process are sent to Europe and the United States. Petroleum is by far the most valuable export of Venezuela.

FIG. 50. When oil is reached after a well has been drilled, it sometimes gushes into the air with great force. After a short time the "gusher" is usually brought under control. Through the pipes in the foreground the oil is pumped to the port of Maracaibo. (Courtesy of Venezuelan Oil 

Concessions, Ltd.)

SOUTH AMERICA 

In Venezuela and on the island of Trinidad off the coast, near the mouth of the Orinoco, are some queer lakes. They are filled not with water, but with pitch, or asphalt. This is the material used for paving streets, spreading on roofs, coating pipes, making lining material for walls of buildings, and other purposes. Firms connected with the asphalt industry advertise at least twenty-five varieties of products made from this substance. Several hundred years ago explorers stopped leaks in their vessels by filling the seams with asphalt from the pitch lake in Trinidad. Most of the asphalt used, today comes from petroleum. After the purer oils have been removed from certain kinds of petroleum by refining, asphalt is left. But large quantities of asphalt from the pitch lakes of Venezuela and Trinidad are exported every year. Most of the Venezuelan product comes to the United States.

Fig. 51 shows you that the pitch lake in Trinidad looks more like an enormous mud pie than a body of water, though from a distance we might think it a shallow, muddy lake. But if we went nearer, the color, the thick material, and the smell would tell us that this lake is different from any we have ever seen. On its borders the asphalt is hard enough to bear our weight; but nearer the center it is softer, and here and there are tiny pitch volcanoes, a foot or two high, in constant eruption.

The workmen dig out the asphalt with pickaxes and load it onto mule carts, to be taken to the shore. In the little shipping village on the coast of Trinidad all the men are employed in some industry connected with asphalt. Some are cutting it and bringing it

VENEZUELA 

127 from the lake, some are melting it, while others are loading it onto the ships which will carry it away. 

The petroleum found in Trinidad is even more valuable than the asphalt which is dug from the lake. Trinidad is a British possession and sends huge quantities FIG. 51. Although asphalt has been taken from this pitch lake in Trinidad for years, the lake is still nearly as large as it was when the work was begun. 

Nature seems to replace the material about as fast as it is taken out. (Photograph from R. I. Nesmith and Associates) of petroleum from its wells to England. It produces more petroleum than does any other British possession. 

The city of Maracaibo lies on the narrowest part of the neck of water which connects the Gulf of Venezuela and Lake Maracaibo. The city is an important one and contains seventy-five thousand people. It is a distributing center for western Venezuela, and through it flows most of the commerce of that part of

SOUTH AMERICA 

the country. Most of the exports shipped away come to the United States, and most of the imports sent to 

Maracaibo are from our country.

Since the development of the petroleum industry Maracaibo has grown rapidly, business has increased, FIG. 52. The many people and boats on the water front of Maracaibo tell us that this is a busy port. What product do you think the bags at the right may contain? (Courtesy of the Pan American Union) and many modern improvements have been made, especially along the water front. New piers and warehouses have been built, and a new customhouse has been erected. Besides these developments which help in the city's commerce, streets have been widened and paved, ice plants built, the use of electricity extended, and many pleasant modern houses built. 

Venezuela has industries on the water as well as on the land. A little way from shore is Margarita Island,

VENEZUELA 

129 famous for centuries for its pearl-fishing. This island was discovered and named by Christopher Columbus, and a settlement was made there more than four hundred years ago. The pearl beds off the shore were known to the Spaniards in very early times, and pearlfishing has been an important industry ever since. The fishing is carried on from small boats by means of rakes, and the pearls and mother-of-pearl shell are sold to local dealers, who ship them away. In some years these products are worth several hundred thousand dollars.

CHAPTER XI  

East of Venezuela, in northern South America, is

Guiana. It is the only division of the continent which is not an independent country. It is divided into three parts which are owned by the English, the Dutch, and the French. Look at the regional map and find out which of these colonies is the largest. 

The regional map shows you that much of Guiana consists of lowlands. Along the coast most of the lowland area is covered with tropical forests, but farther inland the forests change to grasslands. Still farther inland are the Guiana Highlands, which, as you remember, extend into Venezuela. The higher slopes of these mountains are covered with forests. 

The map shows you also how near Guiana is to the equator. You will therefore expect to find the low coastal lands very hot, as you did in Venezuela. The constant heat throughout the year and a very heavy rainfall make the lowlands unhealthful for white people and, combined with the rich soil, cause these lands to be covered with a jungle of trees, shrubs, and tangled vines.

The early settlers of Guiana were Dutch people. 

Their country, the Netherlands, you remember, is a lowland. Parts of it are below sea level, and so the people had to build strong walls to keep out the ocean. 131

SOUTH AMERICA 

Because of this experience the Dutch settlers in Guiana found it easier to reclaim the low, wet lands near the coast than to clear away the jungle forest farther back. 

So they made their settlements on the coastal lowlands and built dikes to keep out the sea, and ditches to drain away the water. In this way they made many acres of rich soil suitable for cultivation.

When the British obtained the part of the country now under their control, they continued the cultivation of the land near the shore and left the forest farther inland practically untouched. Today most of the people, not only in British Guiana but in the other colonies as well, live on a strip of coast land not over fifty miles wide. Here also are the plantations and farms. The greater part of the population of the three colonies is made up of Negroes and people from the 

East Indies.

There are some short stretches of good roads and a few short railroads in Guiana near the coast, but no highways or railroads lead into the interior. The rivers that flow through the country take the place of roads and railroad lines. There is a fall line on the rivers like that in our Atlantic coast states. It is where the hard rock of the highlands meets the softer rock of the lowlands. The rivers can wear away the softer rock faster than they can wear away the harder material, and therefore there are rapids and falls at the point where the two meet. Some of the waterfalls are among the grandest in the world. In one of the falls on a branch of the Essequibo River the water tumbles over a cliff seven hundred and forty-one feet high and then makes a more slanting drop of some

seventy-five or more feet. The amount of water that falls over the cliff is less than the amount that goes over Niagara Falls, but the height of this Guiana waterfall is about five times as great as that of Niagara. 

The forests forests of 

Guiana, like those in other tropical countries of South America, will some day yield much valuable lumber as well as dyes, medicines, tanning materials, and other products. At present the most important forest product is balata. This is a substance somewhat like rubber, and, like rubber, it is obtained by tapping the trees which produce it. It is used chiefly for making 

FIG. 53. This man is cutting down a mahogany tree in British Guiana. When mahogany is freshly cut, it is light pink in color; but after being exposed to the light, it turns dark red. (Photograph by Ewing 

Galloway, N.Y.) belting for machinery, cables, and other articles for which rubber or some substance like rubber is needed. 

Some Indians and Negroes live in villages in the forests near the rivers, but though uncivilized they are not savage. Tied up on the shore near the villages you would see dugouts or other boats; for since there

SOUTH AMERICA 

are no roads in the interior, the only highway is the water. Some of the men spend their time tapping the balata trees and preparing the product, which they ?

FIG. 54. Notice the V-shaped gash in this balata tree. From the cut the balata has dripped into the cup which the workman is emptying into his pail. How is the cup held in place? (Ewing Galloway, N.Y.) send down the rivers to the seaports for export. 

In other places the forest people prepare charcoal for fuel, get out lumber, or, in villages nearer the coast, raise fruits and vegetables for the markets in the seaports. In a trip up some of the rivers we might meet some of the natives in their dugouts, which are filled with products, or see them floating slowly downstream on rude rafts of logs from the forests.

Still farther back, in the depths of the forest, there are Indians living in the same wild, uncivilized state as when the country was first discovered by white men. Some of the tribes live in huts with thatched roofs supported by poles at the corners. The huts are furnished only with a rude fireplace in the center, a few earthen pots and kettles, and some hammocks which serve both for chairs and beds. The men hunt and fish,

and the women prepare the food. All meat and fish not eaten immediately must be smoked, since it would spoil in a short time in so hot a climate. 

The men of some of the wildest tribes often kill their prey with long blowguns. In the hole through the center of the blowgun the Indian places an arrow sharpened at one end to a fine point. Holding the gun to his mouth with both hands, with one powerful puff he sends the arrow straight at his prey, which is perhaps two hundred feet away. Sometimes the tip of the arrow is dipped in poison.

It is known that there are many minerals in Guiana, but only three are mined to any extent. Gold and diamonds are two of these. During long ages the rivers have washed out the gold and diamonds from the solid rock in which they were once embedded, and these minerals are obtained in and near the streams. Sometime in the future the original deposits may be discovered and deep mining carried on. Gold-mining is not nearly so important in Guiana as in former years; but diamonds have become one of the most valuable exports of British Guiana, the division where most of the gems are found. Only South Africa ranks ahead of Guiana in diamond production. How does Brazil rank as a diamond-producer?

When you were studying the United States, you learned about the deposits of bauxite in Arkansas and other Southern states. It is from this mineral that aluminum is made. About an eighth of the world's supply of bauxite is mined in British Guiana and Surinam, and most of it is sent to the United States and Canada. This mineral is our most valuable import from Guiana.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Life on the low plains near the coast of Guiana is very different from that of the Negroes and Indians in the interior. The low, flat land and the canals remind us of Holland; only instead of green meadows dotted with black-and-white cows we see acres of tall, waving sugar cane, coffee and cacao plantations, rice farms, FIG. 55. Most of the gold mined in British Guiana is obtained by hand methods or by dredging. Miners often find gold nuggets weighing several ounces. (Courtesy of the Pan American Union) and groves of coconut trees. Many coconuts are exported, as are the oil pressed from them and the dried coconut meat called copra.

Sugar is by far the most important of all these products, and the plantations where the cane is raised are very large, especially in British Guiana. The draining of the land on all the large plantations where the cane is grown is a great problem, for at high tide the land is lower than the ocean. A sea wall must be built in front to keep out the water and a dam constructed behind to hold back the floods which in the rainy

season cover large areas and would cause much damage to the plantations. Ditches and canals are dug for three purposes-to irrigate the land in the dry season, to drain it in the wet months, and to carry the cane FIG. 56. This field of sugar cane is in Guiana. From a little distance the tassels on the stalks look like flowers. (Courtesy of the Pan American Union) from the fields to the mill. Pumping plants must also be built to help in the drainage problem. All this work is expensive and can therefore be done more successfully on a few large estates than on many small ones. 

There are so many buildings on each plantation and so many people around that it seems like a small town or village. Hundreds of laborers are employed on one estate. Overseers plan and inspect their work,

SOUTH AMERICA 

a doctor tends them when they are sick, and a teacher looks after the children. There are also carpenters, blacksmiths, machinists, engineers, and other workmen to attend to the people, the animals, and the manufacturing of the sugar. There are rows of cottages which are the homes of the laborers, other buildings where the manager, overseers, and skilled workmen live, and usually a schoolhouse and chapel. Then, of course, there are workshops, and the mills where the sugar is made.

Guiana is thinly populated, and workmen are scarce. 

Thousands of people from southern Asia and the East Indies have come there to live and work on the plantations. These people have lighter skins than the natives of Guiana, and most of them wear turbans on their heads. The climate is much like that in their homeland, and they are used to plantation work. They make better and more dependable laborers than the native Negroes and Indians.

The laborers cut the tall cane with knives, strip off the leaves, and load the stalks on boats waiting in the canals. Each boat is pulled to the sugar mill by mules which walk along a path on the bank. At the mill the cane is crushed and the juice clarified, boiled down, and crystallized into sugar by machinery as modern as any that we might find in Louisiana or Cuba. Each of the big plantations in British Guiana produces several million pounds of sugar in a year, and large quantities are sent to England annually.

Georgetown, the capital and chief city of the British colony, is the largest city in Guiana. It is clean and attractive, with wide, shady streets, and plants

and vines blooming in every yard. The low houses are set on posts of wood, brick, or cement. This is necessary on account of the dampness, the insects, and the floods. The rains in Guiana are very heavy, and although the canals help to drain off the water, the streets are sometimes flooded.

Along the shore in Georgetown there is a splendid sea wall several feet wide at the top. During the pleasant afternoons and evenings many of the people promenade on the wall or rest in the seats which are provided, while they listen to the music of a band and enjoy the sea breezes. In and around the city are golf links, tennis courts, and a race track, for Englishmen, wherever they live, delight in sports. On the outskirts of the city are famous botanical gardens.

Here one may wander long distances on paths bordered by hundreds of varieties of tropical trees and plants and vines.

Surinam, as Dutch Guiana is called, is slightly more than half as large as British Guiana and has about half as many people. It is less developed than the 

British colony. Paramaribo is the chief city and is much like the cities in Holland. The clean, shaded streets are lined by narrow houses with steep roofs. 

The doors have brass knockers as bright and shining as knockers always are in Dutch cities. These and many other things are much like those in the homes of the Dutch people across the ocean, but there are other things which one would never see in Holland. In the streets there are Negroes and tall, dark-complexioned East Indians, many of whom are carrying bundles on their heads in a way that no Dutchman could imitate. 1

SOUTH AMERICA 

On the opposite bank of the river from the city is a dense tropical forest which would tell us, if nothing else did, that we are many hundred miles from Holland. 

One of the streets in Paramaribo is shaded on either side by a row of large mahogany trees. A lumber company once made the city an offer of fifty thousand dollars for these trees, but the officials refused the offer, which, if accepted, would have robbed the city of its most beautiful ornament.

The part of Guiana owned by France is smaller and of less importance than either the British colony or the Dutch colony. For many years France used its colony as a prison to which to send criminals. Some of the convict settlements were on the mainland, and some were on islands off the coast. On account of its reputation people have generally shunned the place, and it will be many years before much of a start is made in agriculture or any other industry.

Some Indians, convicts, and a considerable number of gold-diggers live in the forests of the interior. Some of these people tap the balata trees and prepare the product. The gold-mining industry is the most important in the colony. As in British Guiana, the gold is obtained from the river sands, and deep mining has not yet been attempted. The agricultural products of 

French Guiana are much the same as those in the other two colonies, but the amounts produced are less, and there is little for export.

About half of all the people in French Guiana live in Cayenne, the chief city and only seaport. The low houses of the city have overhanging roofs and broad balconies and are shaded by tall palm trees. In the

early part of the day there are more people in the streets than at any other time. There are uniformed soldiers, well-dressed Frenchmen, Negro workers, Chinese in blue blouses, and people from the East Indies with turbans on their heads. The East Indians do most of 

FIG. 57. If you were traveling in French Guiana, you would see many little villages like this one, with the houses clustered along the river bank under tall palm trees. (Photograph by Harriet Chalmers Adams) the work on the farms and supply the city market with fruits and vegetables. The shopkeepers are Chinese, and the city officials are French. 

As morning approaches noon and the tropical sun grows hotter, the streets are deserted, the shops are closed, and the people have a light meal, for they have had only coffee and rolls earlier in the day. Afterward they rest and sleep until the sun is lower and the heat less fierce. This is the custom in many tropical lands.
