CHAPTER XII  ON THE PLAINS 

You have been reading about several countries which lie on the plains of great rivers. The plains of the Amazon River, of which you have read, lie in the widest part of South America. North of these plains are those of the Orinoco River. Farther south in the continent are the plains of another great river system. 

The river for which this entire system is named is really only a bay which indents the coast between 

Uruguay and Argentina. The early explorer who first sailed up this river-bay named it the Rio de la Plata, or "the river of silver." He had found no silver in it or near it; but the Indians whom he met seemed to be well supplied with the precious metal, and he supposed that he would find deposits of it farther upstream. 

The Plata River is the broad mouth formed by the joining of two other streams, the Paraná and the Uruguay. This junction occurs about as far from the ocean as the distance between Boston and New York. In the 

Indian language the name Paraná means "mother of the sea." This river is about as long as the Mississippi. 

It rises in Brazil, in the state of Minas Geraes, and flows in a southwest direction, forming the boundary between Paraguay and Argentina. Trace its course on the map opposite page 168 and find the place where it receives the waters of the Paraguay River, a waterway 144

about as long as our Arkansas River. Here it bends sharply to the south. As the map shows you, these two rivers, the Paraná and the Paraguay, open the way into the very heart of South America.

The Uruguay River is shorter than either the Paraná or the Paraguay. For a considerable distance it forms the boundary between Argentina and Uruguay and receives the trade of both these countries. Farther up in its course it flows between Argentina and Brazil. 

These three rivers of the Plata system drain more than a fourth of South America. Southern Brazil, Uruguay, Paraguay, and northern Argentina lie in their valleys, and much of the traffic of these countries is carried on their waters. Many large areas in each of these countries would even yet be unsettled if it were not for these navigable highways. Towns and villages lie along their banks, and beyond these are rich pasture lands dotted with cattle, and level fields of grain. The only connection that many of the settlements have with the outside world is by means of these rivers and their branches, and most of the products of the inland plains are shipped by water down to Buenos Aires and Montevideo, the two great ports at the mouth of the Plata. 

Our first visit on these southern plains will be to 

Paraguay, one of the two countries of South America which has no seacoast. In the tropical forests which grow far inland on these plains we can learn about an industry carried on in no other continent. This industry furnishes material for a drink used daily by millions of people in South America.

If you were to name the principal beverages of the world, you would probably mention tea, coffee, and

SOUTH AMERICA 

cocoa or chocolate. You might not think of the drink which is obtained from trees that grow along the Paraná River in Paraguay and Brazil, as well as in the Brazilian Highlands. It is called yerba maté. The 

Indians here had another name for it; but the Spaniards called it yerba, meaning "herb," and maté, meaning "gourd." It was given this name be- cause the dried, hol 

FIG. 58. In South America the people drink yerba maté from a bowl or gourd through a brass or silver tube. (Courtesy of the Pan 

American Union) low fruit of the gourd vine was commonly used as a cup to hold the drink. 

Thousands of people in Argentina, Chile, Paraguay, Bolivia, and Brazil use yerba maté daily as we do coffee, tea, and cocoa. Brazil produces more yerba maté than any other country, but the best grade of this tea comes from Paraguay.

Let us imagine that we board a boat at Buenos Aires which will take us up the Paraná to the place where the river bends sharply to the east. From this point we might continue northward on the Paraguay River to Asunción, the capital of Paraguay, but we choose to change to a smaller boat for a sail farther up the Paraná River. The hot sun, the graceful bamboos, and the tall palm trees tell us that we are near the torrid zone. Trace our voyage up the river on the map opposite page 168.

As we go farther north the river grows narrower and the shores grow steeper. Deep forests on either side replace the grasslands. The water flows more swiftly than it did in the lower part of the river, and the course of the stream is more winding. Navigation is not easy here, and every night the captain ties his boat up to the shore in some quiet cove. Wood is used as fuel for the engine, and the boatmen bring on board the supply of firewood for the next day, which other workmen have cut in the forest and piled on the bank. 

We are near the place where an interesting river with a curious name flows into the Paraná from the east. This is the Iguassú. Find this river on the map opposite page 168.

We sail about twelve miles up the Iguassú between shores lined with big trees, tangled shrubs, and swinging vines, among which flit bright-colored birds and brilliant butterflies. Here, on the boundary between 

Brazil and Argentina, the river tumbles over high cliffs in a series of waterfalls. The roar of the water can be heard for miles through the dense forest. No dwelling is near, except a hotel which has been built to accommodate sightseers, whose numbers are increasing each year. When, at some future time, water power is needed in this region, the Iguassú Falls can supply a tremendous amount. In the highest fall the water drops two hundred and ten feet. Can you find out how this height compares with that of Niagara Falls? 

Along the upper Paraná many of the trees in the forests furnish excellent lumber. In some places we see logs floating in the water, and in small clearings on the bank are mills where swiftly flying saws change the

FIG. 59. Iguassú Falls, with their large number of cascades, are one of the most beautiful sights in South America. 

Above the falls the course of the river is interrupted by cataracts for about two miles

rough logs into lumber which is needed for many purposes in the large cities farther south. 

Few people live in this region, and the scattered settlements are miles apart. From time to time, especially in the latter part of our trip, we have seen on the high banks buildings with long chutes leading down to the water. These are warehouses where yerba maté is stored. The forest growth around the buildings has been cleared away, and in the open space are little houses where the workers live. The houses are built of bamboo and have roofs thatched with the big leaves of the palm or some other tropical tree. In most of them the floors are the hard-trodden earth, and the beds are hammocks. Nearly all the furnishings, except the dishes, are homemade. These have been bought from some trader who from time to time makes trips up the river.

Meals are usually cooked and eaten out of doors in the shade of some spreading tree. Should you like to stop and share the dinner of the workmen? The main dish is puchero, a kind of soup made from meat and manioc. For our drink we put some dried, crushed leaves of the yerba tree into a cup and pour boiling water on them. When the liquid is cool enough, we suck it through a tube, like the one in Fig. 58, which has a strainer at the lower end. We do not like the taste very well, but we find the drink refreshing. For dessert we have all the oranges we can eat. Oranges grow wild in Paraguay and are also raised in groves.

The people at these river settlements and the laborers in the forests are engaged in the yerba-maté industry. Most of them are Indians and mestizos who, in

SOUTH AMERICA 

charge of white overseers, gather and prepare the yerba leaves. In order to see more of this industry, we will take a tramp into the forest where the work is going on. 

The yerba tree is a cousin of the holly, which is much used for decorations at Christmas time. Like the holly, the yerba has dark-green, glossy leaves, but it has purple berries instead of red. The yerba trees grow wild in the forests, and because of the demand for the drink made from their leaves, groves of the trees have also been planted. The places where the leaves are collected are called yerbales. Here clearings have been made in the forest, and camps built for the laborers. 

From a camp the workers go out in groups of half a dozen or less. They carry with them small axes and some food and drinking water, since they may be gone for several days. They cut the small branches and twigs from the yerba trees and collect them in piles. 

Then they tie these into big bundles from ten to twelve feet in diameter and bring them back to the camp on their heads and backs.

At the camp the branches and twigs are heated over a log fire until the leaves are withered and dry and can be easily stripped off. The leaves are collected in nets and dried more thoroughly on a platform raised above another fire. Laborers with long poles stir the leaves constantly to keep them from scorching. After this they are pounded with clubs into small bits and packed in bags each of which holds about two hundred pounds. The bags are carried by men or mules or, in some cases, in oxcarts to the warehouses on the river bank. A steamer comes up the river twice a month or oftener to collect the bags. From the buildings on the

high banks the bags slide down through chutes to the steamer or are carried down on the backs of laborers. 

The yerba-maté industry is of great importance in eastern Paraguay and the adjoining parts of Brazil FIG. 60. These workmen are carrying branches of yerba maté to their camp. The bundles are so large that the men look like great green balls walking on two short legs. (Courtesy of the Pan American Union) and Argentina. Large quantities of the leaves are needed, not only for the thousands of people in South America who drink this beverage daily but for people in Europe and the United States. The greater part, however, is used in the countries of the Plata system. 

Most of the people of Paraguay are farmers. In addition to corn, rice, and other food crops, they raise

SOUTH AMERICA 

a good deal of tobacco, which is one of the chief agricultural exports of the country. They raise some cotton also, for the soil and climate are as favorable for this crop as they are in our Southern states. 

Oranges are another important crop of Paraguay, and are shipped in large quantities to supply the markets in Buenos Aires and other southern cities. One kind of orange tree in Paraguay bears bitter fruit which is not good to eat. A valuable oil, however, is obtained from the leaves. Large quantities of this oil are exported to Europe, where it is used in the manufacture of perfumes and flavoring extracts. 

Much of Paraguay is a natural pasture land with grass and water for millions more cattle than are at present raised there. Men from Argentina and Brazil and others from the United States have invested money in the cattle-raising industry. Most of the farmers in 

Paraguay keep some cattle, and on the rolling pasture lands are ranches belonging to large cattle-raising companies. Canned and dried meat, beef extracts, hides, and other animal products are exported. 

In the little homes scattered throughout the country we should find many of the women making beautiful lace. This work has become an industry of some importance, and considerable quantities of such lace are now sold in European cities.

Asunción, the capital of Paraguay, is located on the Paraguay River about one thousand miles from the mouth of the Plata River. It is an old city, for it was founded in 1536. Since the building of the railroad which now connects it with Buenos Aires it has increased in size and importance. In some parts of the

city where there are large business blocks, fine public buildings, and modern stores, and where automobiles and electric cars crowd the streets, we are reminded of some up-to-date city of the United States. In other sections the narrow streets, the oxcarts, and the low FIG. 61. This refrigeration plant is near Asunción. In it meat is frozen and stored for shipment. The animals to be slaughtered are driven in through the runway at the right. (Photograph from R. I. Nesmith and Associates) one-story houses set close to the sidewalks, with their flat roofs and iron-barred windows, make us think of the cities of old Spain from which the early settlers of Paraguay came. Some tall buildings have been built in recent years, but one-story houses are the rule. 

The busiest part of Asunción is along the river front. 

This is the meeting place for the large steamers which come up the Paraguay River from Buenos Aires and the smaller craft which go to the scattered settlements

SOUTH AMERICA 

farther up the tributary rivers and their branches. All passengers and freight must be transferred here at the city wharves, and this furnishes work of different kinds for many laborers. 

From Asunción the Paraguay River winds northward for more than a thousand miles through what is for the most part a wilderness. In places the jungle comes down close to the water. In the forests jaguars, tapirs, snakes, chattering monkeys, and bright-colored birds are nearly as numerous as they are in the deep forests of the Amazon Valley. On both sides of the river the forests alternate with wide, rolling grasslands. 

Once in a while we see some cattle feeding, and now and then we pass a lonely ranch house surrounded by acres of grassy plains. Here in the northern part of 

Paraguay there are fewer fields of wheat and corn than in the south. Their place seems to be taken by fields of tobacco and cotton, both of which are important products.

Still farther north the river grows narrower and swifter, and in the distance are hills which gradually rise into low mountains. The air grows warmer, the trees larger, and the vines higher and thicker. Soon we pass the tropic of Capricorn, the southern boundary of the torrid zone. Bamboos and palm trees become more numerous, and in the scattered villages are many orange and lemon trees and banana plants. As the boat passes one of these lonely settlements naked children stop their play to watch it, while the women continue their work of washing clothes in the river. 

In each village there are fields of manioc such as we saw in Brazil. If we stopped at some of the little ham-

lets along the river, we might be offered some bread which has been made from manioc flour. 

Most of the people here are Indians and mestizos who, shut off as they are from the outside world, are very ignorant. When railroad transportation takes FIG. 62. The large number of boats that are usually to be seen at Asunción are proof of the importance of the Paraguay River as a highway. More than four fifths of the total trade of Paraguay is carried on through this port. (Photograph from R. I. Nesmith and Associates) the place of the river transportation, when settlers begin to fill up the empty lands, and when the resources of this inland country become more widely known, the condition of the people of Paraguay will be greatly improved.

Notice on the map opposite page 168 how the Paraguay River divides the country of Paraguay into two parts. As you know, most of the yerbales lie east of the river. It is in this part of the country that most of

SOUTH AMERICA 

the people live and most of the industries are carried on. Yet even here there are as yet no railroads except the main line running between Buenos Aires and Asunción and its few short branches. The rivers are the main highways, for there are no good roads connecting the widely scattered towns and villages. 

Western Paraguay is even more lonely and less developed than the eastern part of the country. Lying west of the Paraguay River and extending over the Paraguay boundary into northern Argentina and into Bolivia is the Gran Chaco. In some parts of the Chaco the boundaries between these countries were in dispute for years, and as a result there were quarrels and wars. In 1938, however, the boundary question was settled. This region is probably larger than Texas. Its name means "great hunting ground."

The Gran Chaco was probably once covered by the waters of an inland sea, but the Paraguay River is all that is left of this ancient body of water. The Chaco contains large areas of swampy lowlands and tropical jungles in which alligators lie like logs in the rivers; peccaries, tapirs, and jaguars roam through the woods; huge snakes abound; and mosquitoes and other insect pests make life almost unbearable. During heavy rainfalls the lowlands are flooded. Except near the river there are few settlements, and parts of the region have never been explored. Most of the people are Indians, and in the northern part of the region live some of the wildest tribes of South America.

Not all the Chaco is a swampy lowland, however. 

Parts of it are very beautiful. In the open, sunny spaces many flowers bloom. Red and yellow cannas,

big, brilliant cactus blossoms, and many other flowers carpet the ground. Dainty orchids decorate the trees, and blossoming vines hang from the limbs. Quail, blackbirds, parrots, large hawks, tiny humming birds, and many other kinds of birds fly about in the sunshine. FIG. 63. Many of the Indians in the Paraguayan part of the Gran Chaco live in huts like this one. They make their huts of branches and grass because they are constantly moving about from place to place. (Courtesy of 

George H. Kingsland)

There are miles of open grasslands which make fine pastures for cattle, and cattle-raising is carried on in some parts, especially in the Paraguayan section and in the western part of the Gran Chaco near the foothills of the Andes. But the climate of the Gran Chaco, like the climate in the llanos of Venezuela, is a handicap to cattle-raising because of the floods during the rainy season and the lack of rain during the dry season. 

At present the greatest difficulty of the cattleowners of the Grand Chaco is in getting their cattle

SOUTH AMERICA 

and cattle products to the seaports for export. They must depend chiefly on the rivers for transportation. 

When railroads are built from the large cities near the mouth of the Plata River and from Brazilian seaports through Paraguay to Bolivia, there will be a great growth in the cattle industry as well as in the other industries of this inland region. 

In the miles and miles of forest lands in the Chaco, there are many kinds of trees which yield fine lumber, but at present only the edge of the forest has been touched. The rich soil, the heavy rainfall, and the warm climate cause a thick undergrowth of tangled vines, creepers, and shrubs beneath the tall trees. 

Lumbering therefore is carried on with difficulty, for making roads over which logs can be hauled is expensive work in such a region. The lumbering industry is carried on only near the rivers, down which the logs can be floated, and near the railroad leading from Asunción to Buenos Aires.

Of all the trees in the Chaco forests, we are interested especially in the quebracho. From the red heartwood of these trees (that is, the wood in the central part of the trunk) a product is prepared which is used in tanneries for changing stiff, dry hides and skins into soft, workable leather.

In the early years of the leather industry in our country the bark of hemlock and oak trees was the only material used in tanning hides and skins. Later the bark of chestnut trees was used also. These materials are still used today in large quantities. But the demand for leather has grown tremendously, and the hemlocks, oaks, and chestnuts remaining in our forests have

grown fewer in number, and so tanners have had to look for additional materials from which tanning extracts could be made. Some satisfactory chemical preparations have been made from various substances, but it is in the forests of tropical countries that the 

PUERTO PIRASO 

FIG. 64. In this factory in Paraguay thousands of pounds of quebracho extract are made. The factory is run by an American company. (From 

Jones's "Commerce of South America") greater part of the future supply of tanning materials lies. It is thought that from the bark, wood, roots, and leaves of as yet little-known trees and plants growing in the half-explored forests of hot countries there can be obtained enough material for tanning all the leather that is used in the world.

Both quebracho and mangrove trees yield much tannin. Mangrove trees grow in abundance in tropical 

South America, but at present they are little used. 

From tropical parts of the Eastern Hemisphere, however, much mangrove bark is sent to European markets.

SOUTH AMERICA 

The tanning extract obtained from the quebracho trees of the Gran Chaco is largely used in the United States and Europe. Millions of dollars' worth of the logs and the extract prepared from them are shipped every year from this inland region. It is a tremendous task to move the heavy quebracho logs and make the quebracho extract in a wilderness like the Gran Chaco, far away from cities, towns, and supplies of all kinds. 

Tons of food, machinery, building supplies, and many oxen must be taken to the place where operations are to be carried on; sawmills and other buildings must be put up; and camps must be built for the workmen and their families. Because of the amount of labor and the great expense involved, the work is undertaken only by large companies.

The name quebracho comes from two Spanish words meaning "ax-breaker." The wood is one of the toughest and heaviest known. A single log usually weighs five tons or more. Because of their great weight, quebracho logs will not float in water, but have to be carried down the rivers on rafts built of lighter wood or on boats. Fortunately many of the trees grow fairly near the rivers, but in many places railroads and roads have been built through the thick undergrowth far into the forest where the felling of the trees is going on. 

After the trees have been cut down, the trunks and sometimes the largest limbs are stripped of their bark and their outer layers of wood, leaving only the clear, tannin-carrying red heartwood. The private mark of the owner is stamped or painted on each log, which is then slung between low wheels and drawn by oxen out of the forest. Often one log is a heavy load for several

oxen. Because of the hard work and the heat, the animals have to be carefully fed and tended to keep them in good health. They can be used only half a day or a day at a time. Then they are usually turned out to graze and to rest for an equal length of time. FIG. 65. Peeling bark from quebracho logs is hard work. The quebracho trees are scattered among other trees in the Gran Chaco, and a path has to be cut to each tree. (Orient and Occident) 

In the early days of the quebracho industry the logs were exported to the United States and Europe, and the tanning extract was made there. This is still done to some extent, but in the Chaco large plants have now been built in which thousands of tons of the extract are prepared. Fig. 64 is a picture of one of these plants. 

The logs are cut by machinery into small chips. These are boiled in big tanks, and the liquid formed is treated with different substances. When the process is com-

SOUTH AMERICA 

pleted and the liquid is drawn off, the tanning material remains as a solid dull-red cake. This is shipped in bags and casks to Buenos Aires and other Plata River ports for export. Each year we import from the Chaco millions of pounds of this product for our tanneries. 

Smaller quantities are taken by European countries. 

Quebracho wood has other uses than the one about which you have just been reading. Its hardness makes it valuable for railroad ties, fence posts, and great beams used in building. Much quebracho wood is shipped from the Chaco to other parts of South America.

Between 1860 and 1870 a war occurred between Paraguay and the three countries of Brazil, Argentina, and Uruguay.

Perhaps no other war of modern times has been so disastrous to any nation as this was to Paraguay. A large proportion of the men of that country lost their lives, and a third of its territory had to be given up to the winning nations. Most of the cattle were killed, many of the farms and industries were ruined, and towns and villages were wholly or partly destroyed. 

What had been going on in the United States at about the same time?

CHAPTER XIII THE LITTLE COUNTRY OF URUGUAY 

On both sides of the wide mouth of the Plata River there is a large, beautiful city. Montevideo is on the 

Uruguay side of the river, and Buenos Aires is on the 

Argentine side.

Montevideo is the capital of Uruguay. The name monte video means "I see a mountain." The low mountain which gave the city its name is really only a coneshaped hill three or four hundred feet high; it is not at all remarkable, except that it is the only hill in sight along the level shore. Approaching Montevideo by day, one notices the fort on this hill, and by night the lighthouse on its summit which throws its beams out over the bay. You can see this mountain in Fig. 66.

Montevideo is a pleasant city — perhaps the one in all South America in which we should best like to live. 

It is modern, beautiful, clean, comfortable, and healthful. You can see one of its principal plazas on page 167.

The city is situated on a slope that rises gently from the river, and every heavy rain gives its streets a thorough washing. The ocean breezes that blow through the city temper the heat of summer and moderate the cold of winter, and the climate is so delightful that the city and the suburbs which lie along the shore have become popular resorts for people not only from 

Uruguay but also from Brazil and Argentina. 165

FIG. 66. The harbor of Montevideo is a busy place, for the city is the trade center as well as the government center of the country. In the distance is the hill made famous in the city's name. (Courtesy of the Consulate 

General of Uruguay, N.Y.)

THE LITTLE COUNTRY OF URUGUAY 

167 It is the wealth which has come from the ranches and farms on the grassy pastures and fertile plains of Uruguay that has made Montevideo the beautiful city it is. About a third of all the people in the country live in the capital city, and nearly all the imports

On the Avenida 18 de Julio, one of the principal streets of the city, there are fine stone buildings, made from stone quarried in the hills of Uruguay. The name of this street means "Avenue of the eighteenth of July," the date on which was published the constitution of the

republic of Uruguay. The Prado is a fine park with ponds of clear water, avenues lined with tall eucalyptus trees, smooth, velvety stretches of grass, and gardens of beautiful flowers. (Courtesy of the Pan American Union) and the air with fragrance. Electric cars connect the city with its beautiful suburbs and with its shore resorts, where many people play in the warm sunshine on white sandy beaches, swim in the clear sea water, and sail in swift motor boats far out toward the open ocean. 

The people of Montevideo are cordial and hospitable. 

They are alert, business-like, and progressive, as one would imagine they would be from the appearance of their capital city. They are interested in people from the United States, for many of the largest industries of the city and the country are in their hands

THE LITTLE COUNTRY OF URUGUAY 

169 Some of the great meat-packing plants from which large quantities of beef are shipped to northern countries are owned by companies from the United States. 

Uruguay is a lovely rolling country, never very hot, except in the north, and not cold enough for the most part to make any kind of heating necessary. It has no mountains, no deserts, and no barren land, but is one great pasture covered with rich green grass and crossed by several railroads. Stock-raising is its chief industry. The grass is green the year round, and the animals can usually find food for themselves. No barns are necessary or crops of alfalfa for winter feeding, and there are no large, fierce animals to trouble the flocks and herds. Animal products are the most important exports of the country. From Montevideo steamers bound for Europe, the United States, and other parts of the world carry frozen, chilled, dried, and canned meats, beef extracts, wool, hides and skins, fertilizers, horns, and other products from the ranches and meatpacking plants. Of all these animal products, wool is the most valuable of Uruguay's exports. 

One of the most famous manufacturing plants in Uruguay is located about two hundred miles to the northwest of Montevideo near the town of Fray Bentos, which is on the Uruguay River. The sail up the Plata and Uruguay rivers to this town is interesting. On the 

Uruguay side are broad pastures, with here and there groves of trees surrounding the buildings of some large farm or ranch. Not far from the capital is a large plant where frozen meat is prepared. In Uruguay such an establishment is called a frigorifico. Hundreds of cattle and sheep are killed here every day, and the meat

SOUTH AMERICA 

is chilled by the most modern methods. Refrigerator ships take the beef and mutton on the long voyage across the tropics to European countries, where it is received in as good condition as is that sent by rail from Chicago to New York and thence by steamer across the Atlantic.

Many people in South America and other parts of the world eat meat which has been salted and dried. 

The drying and salting preserve the meat so that it will keep in good condition for a long time. Thousands of tons of this "jerked" beef, as it is called, are prepared each year in Uruguay in establishments called saladeros. Near Montevideo is a saladero where one might see quantities of meat hung on wire fences, drying in the sunshine. Since the frozen and chilled meat industries have become so important, much less meat is dried than formerly.

There are many boats on the river some loaded with lumber or fruit, some with dried beef, and some piled high with horns of thousands of cattle. There are large ocean-going craft and small river boats, but it is a safe guess that each boat carries, as the whole or a part of its cargo, some animal product. 

You would be surprised at the variety of fruits carried by some of the steamers. In a trip through Uruguay we should find orchards of apple, pear, peach, plum, lemon, and orange trees, beds of strawberries, large cacao plantations, and banana plants. 

The many buildings of the famous beef-extract plant, a little to the south of the town of Fray Bentos, form a small village by themselves. The fame of the company and its products has spread to many coun-

THE LITTLE COUNTRY OF URUGUAY 171 

tries. When you have been sick, you may have enjoyed some beef tea made from beef extract manufactured hundreds of miles away in Uruguay. The company raises thousands of cattle on its own land and buys many more from ranchmen in Uruguay and Argentina. FIG. 69. After looking at this picture, which was taken on a cattle ranch in Uruguay, what can you tell about the surface of the country? What animal products of Uruguay can you name? (Courtesy of the Pan American Union)

In this great plant more than a hundred thousand cattle have been slaughtered in a single year to make the extract which fills millions of cans and jars. But, like the jerked-beef industry, the manufacturing of beef extract is less important than it used to be. 

The larger part of the animal products of Uruguay is sent to Europe, but some are imported by the United States. The raising of cattle and sheep has always been the chief industry of Uruguay, and the country

SOUTH AMERICA 

has become one of the leading live-stock nations of the world. Because of its fertile plains and the need of food supplies in the great cities of the world, it is possible that farming will be the great industry of the future, however. That is the way it has been in the 

United States, where today in many places farms green with growing crops occupy pastures in which in former years thousands of cattle and sheep fed. 

To help the farmers and the farming industry throughout the country, the Uruguayan government has started schools of agriculture where modern methods of farming are taught. As in many countries of 

South America, much of the land in Uruguay was formerly held in large estates owned by wealthy people, and few of the poorer people had farms of their own. 

Some of these large estates have now been divided into small farms which have been put into the hands of people who are unable to handle larger areas. 

There are already in Uruguay many wheat farms, large and small, and wheat ranks next to animal products in export value. The farmers of Uruguay raise so much corn that it ranks next to wheat as a farm crop. 

Unlike wheat, however, much of the corn is used at home as a cattle food, and little is exported. 

Flax is an important crop on many Uruguay farms. 

As you probably know, this plant is raised for its fiber and its seed. The fiber is used in the manufacture of linen, and the seeds are valuable for their oil, called linseed oil, which is used in making paint and varnish.

To obtain good fiber the flax must be pulled before the seeds are ripe, and so both crops cannot be obtained from the same plants. Farmers in many European

THE LITTLE COUNTRY OF URUGUAY 

173 countries raise flax chiefly for its fiber, but in both 

North and South America flax is raised for its seeds.

Linseed oil is now exported in large quantities from 

Uruguay to the United States and Europe.

History Questions

The first permanent settlements in Uruguay were made about 1624 by the Spanish and Portuguese. Both Spain and 

Portugal claimed the territory until 1777, when it was finally given to Spain. In 1811 the people of Uruguay defeated the 

Spanish forces, but Brazil took possession of the country, and it was not until 1828 that Uruguay was declared an independent republic. On July 18, 1830, its constitution was published, and later in the same year the people elected their first president.

CHAPTER XIV BUENOS AIRES, SOUTH AMERICA'S LARGEST CITY 

About one hundred and twenty-five miles west of Montevideo, across the wide mouth of the Plata River, is another beautiful city. This is Buenos Aires, the largest city of South America. It is also the largest city in the world south of the equator, and the largest Spanish-speaking city in any continent. It lies about one hundred and seventy miles from the Atlantic Ocean, but a deep channel has been dredged out so that large ocean liners can come up to its docks. 

Buenos Aires is the capital of Argentina, the country which occupies most of the great plain region of temperate South America. When the early Spanish explorers came to Argentina, they heard the natives there speak of the Pampa. The word means a large, open, level plain, and the name "Pampa" has clung ever since to these southern plains of South America. 

Buenos Aires is on the very edge of the plains. In this great city live a fifth of the people of Argentina.

Buenos Aires has excellent railroad and water transportation and is the outlet for a large part of the products raised on the Pampa. To its docks and wharves are brought most of the imports which are needed in the country. Then, by means of the rivers and railroads, they are distributed to towns and villages scattered far and wide on the plains. La Plata, a city 175

SOUTH AMERICA 

some thirty miles nearer the ocean than Buenos Aires, shares in this trade and, as a result, has grown rapidly into an important seaport.

Looking down on the capital city from an airplane, you could see how level the land is on which it lies. 

Not a hill or a mountain is in sight. You could see tall wireless towers, large mills and factories with their big chimneys, fine public buildings, long, straight streets, the great airport, and many green open spaces which you would know are parks and gardens. You would notice, too, the miles of docks and wharves and the forest of masts and funnels in the harbor and along the water front. There are big derricks and cranes and other modern machinery for loading and unloading ships and handling immense amounts of freight. 

Buenos Aires is one of the great shipping ports of the world, and thousands of ships enter and leave its harbor every year. The big cranes rattle to and fro, bringing up from the holds of some of the ships clothing and other manufactures of cotton and wool from France and Germany; wine and silk from France; iron and steel manufactures from England; farming tools, harvesting machinery, automobiles, and many other manufactures from the United States. Among the food products there are fish from Norway; sardines and olives from Spain; and cacao, bananas, and coffee from Brazil. Into other ships are being lowered endless bags of wheat, bales of wool, bundles of hides, and great quarters of chilled beef. Trucks crowd the wharves and rumble to and from the warehouses. Cars piled high with grain or animal products are pulled by puffing engines to the freight houses.

NORTE 

FIG. 70. Ships of all nations may be seen along the water front of Buenos Aires. Notice the modern sky line of the city in the background. (From Ewing Galloway, N.Y.)

SOUTH AMERICA 

Back of the wharves are tall elevators filled with grain, and great warehouses where wool is stored. 

Other buildings are filled with hides and skins, beef and mutton, horns, tallow, fertilizer, bone meal, and other animal products. Even if we knew nothing of 

Argentina, these grain elevators and warehouses would tell us that out on the Pampa are farms and ranches stretching away mile after mile.

Some of the old streets of Buenos Aires are very narrow. When the city was small, these narrow streets, shaded by the buildings on either side, were more comfortable in the hot summer than wider ones would have been, and were sufficient to accommodate the traffic which passed through them. Now that street cars, automobiles, heavy wagons, and trucks have become such a necessary part of the city life, these older streets are very crowded. In many of them, as in certain streets in some of our large cities, the traffic is allowed to move in only one direction, and in this way accidents are avoided and blockades prevented. 

The newer streets are wide. The Avenida de Mayo, the finest of them all, is one hundred and twenty feet wide, with twenty-foot sidewalks, and is bordered on both sides with tall shade trees. How does the width of this avenue compare with that of the street on which your schoolhouse stands?

In front of some of the restaurants are open-air cafés. There are also fine hotels, clubs, and modern business blocks. Avenida de Mayo means "Avenue of 

May." It was so named because it was in that month that the Argentine war for independence began with 

Spain. What South American city about which you

BUENOS AIRES 

179 have read has an avenue named for an historical event? 

Such names are common in many cities of the continent. 

In the heart of Buenos Aires is the Plaza de Mayo. 

The Avenida de Mayo runs from this square to the FIG. 71. The Palacio del Congreso is one of the most beautiful buildings in Buenos Aires. The legislature, which meets here, consists of the Senate and the Chamber of Deputies. SOUTH AMERICA 

it is so crowded with people intent on their shopping that automobiles are not allowed to enter. 

There are about a hundred parks in Buenos Aires, ranging in size from two or three acres to several hundred. The finest of all is Palermo Park, which is about three miles from the center of the city. In it there are 24 

FIG. 72. This bathing beach on the river front at Buenos Aires attracts many people. Band concerts are often given in the gardens close by gardens, lawns, trees, lakes, and fountains. Hundreds of people stroll on the shaded paths and rest on the green grass or on the seats which are scattered through the park.

Most visitors to the city are interested in the zoo. 

Here are animals from every part of South America and from other continents as well. As nearly as possible the grounds have been arranged to show each animal in its natural home. There are caves and grottoes, miniature mountains, and a great bird house which reaches above the tree tops.

BUENOS AIRES 

181 Of course a city as large as Buenos Aires has sections very different from its fine boulevards, lovely parks, and residential palaces. In the poorer parts of the city are large, low buildings surrounding small courts. 

In each building there are many small rooms, each one perhaps the home of an entire family. Often the door which opens on the court is the only means of light and ventilation. The washing, cooking, and much of the family life are carried on in the courtyard, which is usually swarming with children. In sections of the city where wide streets and avenues have been made, many of these crowded buildings have been torn down, but there are still many left.

Most visitors to Buenos Aires are impressed with its appearance of wealth and luxury. This is due to the level, fertile plains which stretch north, west, and south from the city. All the products which were in the warehouses, on the wharves, and being loaded on the ships tied up at the docks came from the plains. A few years of poor crops, of droughts, or of swarms of locusts, and there are poorer business conditions in the city, less money to be spent in it, and fewer luxuries for its people to enjoy.

There are very few capitals in the world where so many people are so directly dependent on the occupations and products of the country around as they are in Buenos Aires. In order to understand this fact, we must know something about the great plains which lie around the city. Let us therefore take an imaginary trip over these wide plains which have caused the growth of so beautiful a capital, and see what life in the country regions of Argentina is like.

CHAPTER XV  

In the short time which we have for our visit in Argentina we cannot travel all over the country, for it is between a third and a half as large as the United States. If a map of Argentina were laid on one of North America, Argentina would reach from Hudson Bay to central Mexico. 

The regional map shows you that the northernmost part of Argentina lies in the torrid zone, while the southern end is in the part of the temperate zone where the summers are cool and the winters are very cold. Notice the great plain which covers most of the country and reaches westward to the high Andes Mountains. The rainfall in its western part is light because the Andes shut off the rain brought by the winds from the Pacific Ocean. 

There is more rain in eastern Argentina because winds full of moisture blow in over the land from the Atlantic. 

Because of all the differences in temperature, rainfall, and surface, we shall find the people in different parts of Argentina living in different ways and engaged in different kinds of work. As we travel through the country, we can ride for days over vast plains or see some of the grandest mountains on the continent; we can endure the discomforts of the dry, dusty Pampa or enjoy the delicious fruit of orchards and vineyards watered by clear mountain water; we can mingle with gay crowds on the splendid boulevards of the capital 184

We can shiver with the Scotch shepherds on the wind-swept hills of Patagonia in the Far South or wander in palm groves and enjoy the tropical fruits of the warm lands in the North. Truly Argentina is a wonderful country. 

It is developing more and more every year, and its industries and products are constantly increasing.

SOUTH AMERICA 

On pages 6-7 you read about the difference in the seasons in North and South America. You know that

Argentina is one of the South American countries lying FIG. 74. Pampa grass is so named because it covers large areas of the Pampa. 

Its tall, feathery spikes often grow to a height of eight or nine feet south of the equator. 

When the sun is high in our sky, giving us much heat, it is low in the Argentine sky, giving but little heat to the people there. 

Therefore their seasons are the opposite of ours. Winter in Argentina comes in July, August, and September, and the summer months are January, February, and March. 

There is something else about Argentina which would seem queer to us. In the 

United States many people living in the northern part of the country go south in 

This is because our equator. In Argen- winter to get to warmer states.

Southern states are nearer to the tina people go north to get to the warmer part of the country. If you lived in Argentina, you would find the north rooms in the houses warmer than the south rooms in summer. Is that the way it is in your house?

Northern Argentina, in or near the torrid zone, is much like western Paraguay. Here, in the Gran Chaco, are many quebracho and yerba trees, and the work of making tanning extract and yerba maté is carried on here as it is across the border in Paraguay. 

Another product of northern Argentina is sugar.

The sugar industry centers around Tucumán, a city on the eastern slopes of the Andes. As you know, little rain falls here, and so the farmers have to irrigate their sugar-cane fields with water from streams flowing from the Andes. By this means they raise large crops. 

Owing to the plentiful supply of water for irrigation and the building of a railroad through the region, connecting Buenos Aires with Bolivia, the sugar industry has developed rapidly. Tucumán has grown in recent years until it has become a city of more than a hundred thousand people.

Northern Argentina, however, is not so important as the Pampa farther south. What did you read about this region on pages 175 and 178? The Pampa stretches from the Atlantic Ocean westward to the foothills of the Andes Mountains and from the Negro River northward to the Salado River. Find these rivers on the map opposite page 168. The region between them is the chief source of the growth and wealth of Argentina and its capital city.

Argentina has more miles of railroad than any other South American country. From one of the several fine railroad stations in Buenos Aires we can go by train many miles to the south, or we can ride north or west until we cross the borders into other countries. Perhaps you think it might be pleasanter to go by automobile.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Much of the pleasure of riding in motor cars, as you know, depends on good roads. Many such roads are being built near the large cities of Argentina, but there are few good highways far out on the Pampa. Villages and towns are separated by miles of open country, and the immense size of many of the ranches and farms FIG. 75. This is one type of cart that is used on the Pampa. Why do you think it has such large wheels? puts great distances between the dwellings. During the part of the year when there is little rainfall, the dust on the wide plains flies in clouds, and at other times of the year the road track is deep in mud. So for a long trip we shall find a train much more comfortable than an automobile. 

Near Buenos Aires we ride by farms which supply the capital with fruit and vegetables. Many of the farmers keep dairy cattle, and the milk, butter, and cheese which they produce find a ready sale in the city.

Large amounts of butter and cheese are exported also to Great Britain and other European countries. 

Farther on, a great sea of grain stretches on either side of our train. Argentina is one of the most important wheat-producing countries of the world. The fields extend for miles, with the yellow grain tossing and bending, rising and falling, and looking, as the wind blows over it, like the waves of a great yellow ocean. Journeying north, south, or west from Buenos

Aires, we should find the same level land, the same rich soil, and the same great stretches of waving grain. 

Trains coming from all three directions carry wheat to Buenos Aires to be loaded on the vessels in the harbor and stored in the elevators along the water front. At nearly every station sacks of wheat are piled high beside the track instead of being stored in elevators as in the United States, and in regions far from the railroads oxcarts filled with grain creep slowly over the dusty plain. In some of the smaller villages we see mule carts like the cart shown on the opposite page.

The winters in the wheat-growing region of Argentina are mild, and the farmers sow their grain in the fall or early winter and reap it in the early summer as farmers do in Kansas and other states in our winter-wheat belt. As in our own country, harvest time in Argentina is the rush season of the year. Thousands of laborers from Italy flock into the country, for there is plenty of work for all who come. Some of the largest landowners have their own harvesting machines, and those on smaller estates hire them. These machines are as large and modern as those used in our country. Indeed, many of them were made in the United States.

SOUTH AMERICA 

There are large areas of fertile land in Argentina as yet unsettled, for the country is very thinly populated. 

Though it is between a third and a half as large as the United States, it contains fewer people than live in New York State. Settlers are needed to take up the land and make their homes here. Many thousands have come from Italy, Spain, Germany, Russia, and other European countries. Steamship companies bring them at low rates, railroads carry them out on the Pampa for small fares, and the Argentine government helps them to find work on the large estates or to take up land and start farms of their own. 

Corn is another very important crop of Argentina. 

It requires more heat and moisture than wheat, and so it is raised largely in the east-central part of the Pampa, where the rainfall is heavier and the climate is not too cold. The United States, you know, is the greatest corn-producing country in the world. Argentina does not raise nearly so much corn as we do, but it uses so little of its corn crop at home that it exports more than any other country. The cornfields which we pass on our trip look green and flourishing. They would appear very different if our trip occurred after a swarm of locusts had passed that way. Locusts are the greatest pests in Argentina. Clouds of them sometimes fly over the land and swarms of them cover the ground. 

They have been known to stop trains from running because they covered the tracks in such numbers that the wheels slipped round and round without making any progress. They destroy every green thing in their path. When these pests arrive, the heads of wheat are usually too hard for them to eat, and some other

FIG. 76. This machine is threshing and bagging wheat. The sacks of wheat are loaded on huge wagons drawn by from ten to twenty horses. (Courtesy of the International Harvester Company of America, Inc.)

SOUTH AMERICA 

tempting crops have been harvested. But the juicy kernels and leaves of the corn make a fine meal for the hungry creatures. After they leave, not a kernel or a green blade is left on the stalks.

Farmers on the Pampa raise a great deal of flax as well as of wheat and corn. When the flax fields are FIG. 77. On large farms on the Pampa corn is shelled by machine and packed in bags to be taken to the nearest port. The cobs are stored in cribs made of sticks, wire, and cornstalks. What did you read about the flax plant and its two products on pages 172-173?

On the map opposite page 168 find the city of Rosario. 

On what river is it? This city, with over 500,000 people, is the second largest in Argentina. So much wheat, corn, and flax are sent to it from farms on the Pampa that Rosario exports even more grain than

Buenos Aires. The grain is taken to Rosario on the many railroad lines which enter that city, on oxcarts, and on motor trucks.

Rosario lies above the river on a high cliff, on the edge of which perch big warehouses. Large ships come up the river and tie up at the docks below, and the grain flows down into their holds through chutes. 

Much of it goes to Europe, but some of it is also sent to our country. In a recent year Rosario shipped to the 

United States more than six million bushels of flaxseed alone.

Some of the most interesting sights in Buenos Aires and in the port of La Plata, farther down the river, are the long trains filled with cattle which come from the grazing lands to the north, the south, and the west. 

As in Chicago, Omaha, and other cities in the United States, the animals are unloaded from the trains into stockyards covering many acres. From the stockyards they go to the slaughterhouses and thence to the packing plants. There are a dozen or more big slaughtering and meat-packing establishments in Buenos Aires and La Plata. Some of them are owned by the same companies which operate similar plants in Chicago. You see such a plant in the picture on page 194. In and near 

Buenos Aires between two and three million cattle are slaughtered each year, and the meat is prepared and chilled as it is in our country. The largest establishment where this kind of work is carried on is in La Plata, and more meat is exported at present from that city than from any other Argentine port.

Out on the Pampa we see many of the ranches which supply cattle and sheep for the stockyards in Buenos

SOUTH AMERICA 

Aires and La Plata. Distances on the Pampa are so great that we ride for long stretches without seeing a house, a person, or an animal. Again, from time to time we pass large numbers of cattle and sheep. In 

Fig. 2, on page 5, you can see cattle feeding on the FIG. 78. This slaughtering and meat-packing plant near Buenos Aires is owned and operated by a Chicago company. The large number of buildings shows you the importance of the industry carried on here wide plain. Many horses also feed in the pastures. 

The colts caper about their mothers or kick up their heels and gallop away as our train goes by. As in our own Western states, the old days of the open ranch in Argentina are passing. Miles of wire fencing now inclose and separate fields. We pass many fields of alfalfa, its deep purplish color making a striking contrast with the brown grass. Since Argentine ranchmen discovered that alfalfa is a good cattle food, they have been raising large quantities each year.

In the Spanish language ranches and very large farms in Argentina are called estancias. When we are still a long distance from an estancia, we can see the poplar, willow, eucalyptus, and other trees which have been planted around the buildings. Elsewhere the great plain over which we have been riding is bare. 

FIG. 79. On many estancias on the Pampa rows of Lombardy poplars have been planted to break the force of the wind The estancia which we are to visit is very large. 

There are many buildings, for hundreds of workmen are employed here. On every side stretch the vast pasture lands on which thousands of cattle and sheep find food the year round. On this estancia, as on many others, water is pumped by tall windmills, many of which were made in the United States. There are sheds where wheat, corn, and flaxseed are stored. But what seems queer to us in this land of many cattle is the fact that there are few barns. Most of the animals live out of doors the entire year.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Here and there, like black dots scattered over the plain, are the little huts of the laborers. Some are miles from the main buildings; for the cattle wander long distances in search of food, and the estancia is so large that the land is reckoned in square miles instead of in acres. 

E. M. Newman from Publishers' Photo Service, N.Y.

FIG. 80. Most of the cattle in Argentina graze out of doors the year round, but on some estancias where prize cattle are raised barns have been built to shelter them

The animals are divided into herds, and each herd is cared for by several gauchos, or cowboys, as we should call them. They may be far away on the Pampa for days or weeks at a time, and must carry with them the provisions they need. One of the articles each gaucho usually has with him is yerba maté. With a few fagots he can make a fire to heat the water for his favorite drink. 

The owner of a large estancia is generally wealthy and has a large and luxuriously furnished house. The

grounds are beautifully laid out, with gardens, groves, and tennis courts. The family usually lives only a few weeks or months of the year on the estancia, however, for they spend most of the time in Buenos Aires or traveling in Europe. The children are often educated in European universities and speak several languages. 

The part of Argentina south of the Negro River is called Patagonia. It is nearly twice the size of California and is about a thousand miles long from north to south. Like the part of Argentina farther north, most of Patagonia is a great plain. On the eastern slopes of the Andes Mountains and in the valleys between the ranges, this region is very beautiful, with many swift rivers, dashing falls, great glaciers, and sky-blue lakes. Farther west the high, cold peaks of the Andes stand out against the blue sky, while on the lower slopes, on both the Atlantic and Pacific sides, there are miles of forests as yet unexplored and unused. 

In northern Patagonia there are many cattle ranches; but sheep-raising is by far the chief industry of the region, and in the south are some of the largest sheep ranches in the world. A ranch in Patagonia has to be very large, for the grass is so scanty that the sheep must wander over large areas to find enough food. One great ranch in Patagonia is larger than the state of Rhode Island. Life on such a ranch is lonely, for the nearest neighbors are usually miles apart. Many of the shepherds are Scotchmen, some are Germans, and others are natives and half-breeds. 

Even though southern Patagonia has a cool climate, the sheep live in the open all the year round. In the summer they feed on the bleakest portions of the

SOUTH AMERICA 

ranches the parts most exposed to the piercing winds. 

In winter they roam over the more protected areas where the snow is not deep enough to prevent them from getting at the grass. But even with these and other precautions hundreds of sheep die during the winter. In the spring the shepherds go out over the plains to get the wool from the bodies. 

The shearing begins in the spring month of November. On most of the estancias the wool is clipped with electric shears. Only where the herds are small is the work done by hand. After the shearing is finished, the ranchmen load the wool on high, heavy carts. These are pulled by horses or oxen over the plain to the nearest railroad station or to some port. The sheep intended for meat are driven in large flocks to the nearest railroad or to packing plants.

Most of the sheep and wool finally arrive at the port of Bahia Blanca. Find this city on the map opposite page 168. Since ranching has increased in Patagonia, Bahia Blanca has grown rapidly, and most of the products of the southern ranches are shipped from its harbor. Some meat-packing plants have been built there, and the harbor has been improved. Large quantities of cattle products, mutton, wool, and hides are exported every year from Bahia Blanca to European countries. Wheat is also an important export from this port; for, as the map opposite page 168 shows you, Bahia Blanca has excellent railroad connections with most parts of the Pampa. 

Many nations in other continents are turning their attention more and more to manufacturing and are calling for larger and still larger supplies of bread and

FIG. 81.

This is part of a herd of sheep on an Argentine farm 

Publishers' Photo Service, N.Y.

SOUTH AMERICA 

meat. Therefore as South America develops and its countries become more thickly settled, we shall find ever-increasing areas in Patagonia devoted to cattleraising and sheep-raising. Much greater stretches than at present will be covered with the grain which furnishes flour for the bread to be eaten by the people of civilized nations.

In the early days of ranching in Argentina the flesh of the animals raised there was considered of very little value, and the wealth of the owners came entirely from the wool and the hides and skins. Things are very different today. Every part of the animal is made use of in Argentine slaughterhouses and meat-packing establishments as carefully as in our Chicago packing houses. From Buenos Aires, from Rosario farther north, from Bahia Blanca toward the south, and from other 

Argentine seaports ships sail for other South American countries, for Europe, for the United States, and for other parts of the world. They carry horses and cattle and sheep; cattle hides, sheepskins, and goatskins; smoked, dried, chilled, and canned meat; tongues, tallow, grease, hair, bones, glue, fertilizers, glycerin, oils, soaps, and other products prepared from the bodies of animals which feed on the Pampa. 

When you sleep on your hair mattress, put on your shoes or your wool sweater, eat canned meat, feed bone meal to your chickens, or fertilize the plants in your garden, you may like to remember that boys and girls in many other parts of the world are using similar products and that a large part of the world's supply of these useful articles comes from the southern plains of South America.

CHAPTER XVI  

There are three ways by which we can go from Argentina to Chile. We can travel by train from Buenos Aires across the Pampa and over the Andes Mountains, we can sail south around the southern end of the continent and then north to a Chilean port, or we can fly from Buenos

Aires to Santiago, the capital of Chile. 

On the regional map and the map opposite page 168 look closely at the southern end of the great western highland of South America. You see that the valleys between the mountains are so low here that the ocean has filled them. The mountain tops have been surrounded by water and thus made into islands. At the very tip of the southernmost island you will find Cape Horn. This famous cape is a giant rock jutting out into the southern ocean. The waters around the cape are rough and stormy and are more dreaded by sailors than those of any other trade route. 

It is possible, however, to sail to the western coast of 

South America without going around this dangerous point. Two hundred miles north of Cape Horn there is a channel more than three hundred miles long connecting the Atlantic and Pacific oceans and separating the large island of Tierra del Fuego from the mainland. 

This waterway, named for the explorer who discovered it, is the Strait of Magellan. Find it on the map. 203

SOUTH AMERICA 

In Magellan's time many sailors were trying to find a water route from western Europe to India and China. 

In searching for such a route Magellan with his five small ships skirted the eastern coast of South America. 

He finally discovered and sailed through the strait FIG. 82. These ships are sailing through the Strait of Magellan. From the description of the strait on page 205, near which end do you think this picture was taken? (Courtesy of the Chile Publishing Company, N.Y.) that now bears his name. It took him nearly a month to make the passage, which steamers now make in about forty-eight hours. Sailors today are helped by a lighthouse which stands at the eastern entrance of the strait and flashes a light that can be seen twenty miles offshore.

Magellan continued his voyage across the Pacific to the Philippine Islands, where he was killed by the natives. One of his ships continued on its journey, rounded the Cape of Good Hope on the southern point

of Africa, and so reached Europe and the port from which the expedition had set out. This was the first time that anyone had ever sailed entirely around the earth. The journey was made in ships smaller than we should think safe for such a voyage; the knowledge of the sailors was scanty compared with that of the captains of our great liners; and it took as many months to complete the voyage as it would take weeks at the present time. There was no wireless apparatus in those days, no lighthouses, no lifeboats, and no water-tight compartments such as safeguard the passengers who journey on the water today; and for months and even years the bold sailors were out of touch with the civilized world. All hail to Magellan and his brave crew! The strait bearing his name will always be to us something more than a narrow passage of water connecting two oceans when we think of the courage and perseverance of the man who discovered it.

The passage through the Strait of Magellan is safer than the route around Cape Horn, yet the names which some of the early explorers gave to different points in the narrow waterway tell of its dangers. Among these names are Fatal Bay, Fury Island, Famine Reach, Last Wreck Point, Desolation Harbor, and Hope Inlet.

The Strait of Magellan passes through Chilean territory. Its eastern half is a broad channel dividing the southern part of the great South American plain. The low, level land stretches away to meet the sky on the mainland to the north and on the island of Tierra del Fuego to the south. The western half of the strait is a deep, narrow gorge through the mountains. For much of the year the plains bordering the eastern part of the

SOUTH AMERICA 

strait are bare and brown; for that region lies in the path of westerly winds, and little of the rain brought by these winds falls east of the mountains. The peaks in the west are drenched with rain, and the steep cliffs, the wooded slopes, the waist-deep beds of moss and ferns, the blue-white glaciers, and the snow-capped mountain tops make the scenery there very beautiful. 

There is one way in which the two ends of the strait are alike in neither part will you see any sign of human life. A few Indians inhabit these desolate regions; but their tents and huts are farther inland, and they seldom come in their canoes to the more open stretches of water. It was doubtless the signal fires and camp smoke of Indians that caused Magellan to give to the group of islands south of the strait the name of Tierra del Fuego, which means "land of fire." At present only the largest island is called by that name. 

We will go from Argentina to Chile by railroad, taking a train at Buenos Aires which will carry us across the great plain and over the Andes.¹ To open up the western part of the country, Argentina built a railroad across the Pampa to Mendoza, on the eastern slopes of the Andes. Chile, in developing its resources, built a railroad up the western slopes. Later the two lines were connected. Before this was done, travelers could cross the mountain pass only at certain times of year and at great risk. Today the train trip of nearly nine hundred miles, from Buenos Aires on the Atlantic to Valparaiso on the Pacific, takes about thirty-six hours. 

¹ At the present time the section of the railroad which crosses the Andes is not in use because it has not yet been repaired after being badly damaged by floods a few years ago. The description given here applies to the through train route.

On the Pampa we race past fields of wheat, corn, flax, and purple alfalfa. We ride by widespreading pastures where thousands of cattle, sheep, and horses are feeding. We pass little mud huts of Italian laborers and catch glimpses of the buildings on large estancias. Once in a while we see a long-legged flamingo or SEGUNDA 29 

FIG. 83. Passengers on the Transandine Railway travel in comfortable cars. The building of this railroad line was a very difficult and expensive project, as you can imagine some ostriches feeding near a flock of sheep. These ostriches, called rheas in South America, are common here. They are similar to the African ostriches, but are smaller and their feathers are less valuable.

The track for many miles is without a curve. The 

Idust sifts in at the doors and windows and flies in clouds over a moving herd which we see in the distance. 

As far as we can see there are no hills larger than those built by ants and no valleys deeper than the ruts worn. by heavy cart wheels. In the distance a line of high

SOUTH AMERICA 

two-wheeled wagons filled with bags of wheat is creeping slowly over the plains. The few towns are far apart. In most of them the buildings are only one story high and are usually made of brick. At the stations many horses are standing and groups of cowboys and laborers loiter about, for the arrival of the train is the great event of the day.

Finally we reach the foothills of the mountains and see in the distance the high peaks of the Andes. We stop for a little visit in the pleasant city of Mendoza. 

High mountains, green valleys, rushing streams, and tumbling falls make the scenery here beautiful. In and around the city are miles of vineyards, peach orchards, and fig trees.

On the sides of the streets and in the orchards and gardens and vineyards are little streams of water which have been led in canals and ditches from the river which comes tumbling down the steep slopes close by. The river is thick with the mud which it carries, and so enriches the soil as well as furnishes water needed by the crops. The soil is dark and rich and is cultivated so carefully that no weeds are to be seen.

The vineyards stretch for miles in and around the city. The vines, pruned back and fastened to wire and posts, bear great bunches of grapes. At harvesting time men and women are busy picking the clusters of delicious fruit, millions of pounds of which are used each year in making wine. Loads of fruit are being carried to the wine presses, and high carts filled with casks of wine are on their way to the railroad station. Quantities of the grapes are also sent by train down the slopes and across the Pampa to Buenos Aires to supply

the city markets and surrounding regions. At Buenos 

Aires some of the fruit is loaded on refrigerator ships bound for New York, where it arrives in as good condition as does the fruit which comes from California and other states. The grapes from Mendoza bring FIG. 84. This is one of the many large vineyards in the vicinity of Mendoza. The workmen are spraying the vines to prevent disease. What two uses are made of the grapes raised here? (Orient and Occident) high prices in the United States, for they arrive during our winter and early spring. How do you account for this time of their arrival?

As our train climbs higher up the slopes, the cultivated lands are left behind. The air grows cooler and the vegetation scarcer. We pass small, lonely stations, each with its large woodpile close by and a few goats feeding on the stunted shrubs and grass. Now we are

SOUTH AMERICA 

so high that the mountains around are bare, with stony faces down which pour cascades and waterfalls. There are no trees here, and only a few blades of grass grow in sheltered places. Higher still we climb, until finally we arrive, ten thousand feet above the level of the ocean, FIG. 85. Snowplows are very necessary parts of the equipment of the 

Transandine Railway. This picture was taken in August. How do you account for the large amount of snow here during one of our summer months? (Courtesy of the Chile Publishing Company, N.Y.) at the last railroad station in Argentina. From here to the Chilean side of the mountains a tunnel two and a half miles long has been built. 

Before this tunnel was made, all travelers from the Atlantic to the Pacific were obliged to take a cold, dangerous trip on mule-back up over the high mountain pass called the Uspallata Pass. Now comfortable coaches are provided for those who choose this route.

FIG. 86. With a large cross supported in one hand and with the other uplifted in blessing, the great figure of Christ stands silent and serene amid the rocky mountain peaks of the Andes. (Photograph by J. C. Sawders from R. I. Nesmith and Associates)

SOUTH AMERICA 

We decide to ride up over the pass instead of going through the tunnel. We zigzag back and forth up the trail, enjoying the view of the distant mountains outlined against the clear blue sky. All vegetation has long since disappeared. Rocky slopes, snowy peaks, and bluish-white glaciers meet the eye on every side. 

One more sharp turn and we stop, holding our breath in sudden wonder. Before us, sharply outlined by the mountain background, rises a massive statue of Christ. 

The statue was cast from melted cannon and erected by Chile and Argentina as a symbol of peace between them.

The Christ of the Andes stands on the boundary line between Chile and Argentina. Close by is the boundary post with the word Argentina on one side and Chile on the other. Passing these, we begin our downward course on the Chilean side amid rocks, foaming streams, and deep gorges similar to those on the eastern slopes. Gradually the air grows milder, a few blades of grass and stunted shrubs appear, and hardy mountain flowers carpet the ground. We have left far above us the snow fields, the glaciers, the keen, frosty air, and the bare, rocky peaks of the Andes. 

Before us lies the country where we are to make our next visit, and with thoughts of the interesting sights and experiences that are in store for us we climb on board our train and speed down the slopes into Chile.
