CHAPTER XVII THE LONGEST, NARROWEST COUNTRY IN THE WORLD 

So far in our trip through South America we have spent our time in the countries which lie chiefly in the river plains east of the Andes Mountains. Now we are going to visit the countries in the western part of the continent through which that great mountain system runs. There are five of these mountain countries. 

What are their names?

There is no country in the world so long and narrow as Chile. A map of it laid on one of North America made on the same scale would stretch nearly from 

Juneau, Alaska, to the southern tip of the peninsula of

Lower California. Nowhere in its entire length, however, is the country wider than California, and much of it is narrower. If it were of a different shape, it would cover quite a slice of the United States; for it is as large as all the New England States, the Middle 

Atlantic States, and most of North Carolina.

Since we took the train on the Chilean side of the mountains we have been descending steep grades; running through tunnels; passing deep gorges; and catching glimpses of mountain rivers, some with swift, foamy rapids and others tumbling over high cliffs in beautiful falls. In the sky far above us a great bird is wheeling, circling, and floating in the thin blue air. It 215

SOUTH AMERICA 

is a condor, one of the largest birds in the world. In our travels through Chile we shall see the condor pictured and mounted in many places, for it is the national emblem of that country as the eagle is of the United States. Notice the picture of the condor on this page. FIG. 87. The outstretched wings of this condor measure ten feet from tip to tip. Do you know how the eagle compares with the condor in size? 

(American Museum of Natural History) 

You can imagine how large these great birds are when I tell you that the outstretched wings from tip to tip measure from nine to twelve feet. Condors often fly so high that it seems as if they could distinguish nothing on the earth; yet their sight is so keen that when a sheep or a goat dies in the high pastures, the condors soon swoop down, circling nearer and nearer the earth, until they finally alight on their prey. 

Before we come to the station of Los Andes the air grows milder and the gray rocks begin to have a thin

THE LONGEST, NARROWEST COUNTRY 

217 covering of green. The streets of this town are wide and straight, and on either side are little streams of clear mountain water kept full by the melting snows above. Beyond the streams are low houses built of stone. This is the cheapest building material here, for there is a never-ending supply of it in the surrounding region. Close by are large fruit orchards, and if we were to peep into some of the houses we might find the women canning and preserving pears, peaches, and other fruits.

Beyond the orchards lie fields of wheat, and higher on the slopes are herds of cattle and flocks of sheep. 

A line of small gray donkeys is climbing up the steep trail into the town. Each one is so loaded with firewood that only his legs can be seen. Wood is the only fuel here, and every day many donkeys with their drivers plod along the mountain paths to get it and bring it in. 

Some of the donkeys in the streets have large cans hanging on either side, and from these their milkmen owners measure out milk to customers.

The Transandine Railway, on which we are riding, follows a mountain stream that hurries down the slopes with many cascades, rapids, and falls. This is the Aconcagua River, named for the highest mountain in all South America. Now and then we catch glimpses of this lofty, snow-crowned peak rising above the other giants of the Andes. Look at the picture of Mount 

Aconcagua on page 218 and see how beautiful it is. 

Once in a while we pass between fields inclosed by low mud walls. A network of canals from the river extends through each field and furnishes the water for the wheat and corn and the vegetables and fruit that are growing there. Some of these products are sent

FIG. 88. Mount Aconcagua, on the boundary between Chile and Argentina, rises to a height of 23,080 feet above sea level. The Aconcagua River has its source on the southern slope of this mountain

THE LONGEST, NARROWEST COUNTRY 

219 down to Santiago to supply its markets, and some are shipped away from the port of Valparaiso to other countries. In the distance we see dry, cactus-covered hills and large fields, inclosed by stone walls, where cattle, horses, and sheep are feeding. The train runs near some scattered mud huts, the homes of laborers on some hacienda, as the Chileans call their large estates. 

We catch glimpses of heavy two-wheeled oxcarts loaded with wheat bound for the nearest railroad station, and we notice men plowing with yokes of oxen in some fields close by. 

At last we reach Santiago, the capital of Chile. It is not so level as Montevideo nor so rich as Buenos Aires, but it has what no city on the plains of Uruguay or Argentina can ever have — a beautiful view of high mountains which tower on either side. Ten miles to the east the magnificent Andes rise to a height of more than eighteen thousand feet, their great peaks always wearing their white cap of snow. On the west is a lower coast range; and toward the south, between the mountains, stretches a fertile valley green with growing crops and with grassy meadows in which cattle are feeding.

Santiago has often been called the most beautiful inland capital in the world, and the people there have done their best to make and keep it beautiful. It lies some seventeen hundred feet above the level of the ocean and about a hundred miles inland. The early Spanish explorer who founded this city built a fortress on a rocky hill now called Santa Lucia and laid out the town at the foot of the hill in long, straight streets which divide the city into squares like a checkerboard. Old cities in

SOUTH AMERICA 

Spain were often laid out in this way, and the Spanish explorers in the New World followed the same plan. 

Santa Lucia was first crowned by a fort, built to defend the city at its base. After this it was used as a cemetery. In more recent years it has been made into a beautiful park. It has winding roads and trails, grottoes and gardens, fountains and cascades, groves and observation towers, and rustic bridges and stone stairways. In odd little nooks and corners ferns and other plants have been planted, and comfortable seats invite one to rest on the climb to the summit. Bright flowers peep out from every cranny, creeping vines cover the rocks, and splashing brooks come leaping down over steep cliffs. At night the hill, as well as the city below, is brilliantly illuminated with electric lights. 

During our visit in Santiago we must certainly walk or ride down the Alameda de las Delicias, which runs through the heart of the city. This long musical name means "the avenue of delights," and the name is very appropriate. A part of this avenue is shown in the picture on page 221. In another part it is between three hundred and three hundred and fifty feet wide and is divided into sections by four rows of tall trees. On both sides it is bordered by some of the finest business houses and most beautiful residences in the city. The center division is a broad boulevard called the Paseo, along which are statues, flower beds, and stone seats. 

Crowds of people walk and loiter and rest along the Paseo, chatting with friends or listening to the music of the bands which play in the bandstands. Children run and play, and nursemaids watch over their charges. 

On either side of the Paseo are the tracks of electric

FIG. 89. The wide and beautiful Alameda de las Delicias is the chief avenue of Santiago. It is two miles in length. (Courtesy of the Chile Publishing Company, N.Y.)

SOUTH AMERICA 

street railways, and beyond these are wide driveways for automobiles. Do you know of any street in your neighborhood wide enough for all these activities to be carried on at the same time? 

Before leaving Santiago we will make a trip to the zoo and see some of the animals which live on the great highland of South America. Here we make the acquaintance of the llama, the chief burden-bearer of Peru and Bolivia. As we go into the mountainous portions of those countries we shall see great numbers of these animals and their Indian drivers. You will find a picture of a llama on page 269.

Here are some other animals that look somewhat like the llamas. They belong to the same family, and are called vicuñas. Their fur is very fine and silky and makes excellent cloth. These animals are not so common as they once were, and the goods made of their hair is valuable. The animals standing close by are alpacas. Look at the picture of the alpaca on page 273 and see how much it resembles a llama. Alpacas are raised for their long, fine wool, but the cloth which we call alpaca usually contains little, if any, alpaca wool. 

Fabrics woven of the real alpaca wool are very expensive and are seldom seen in our stores. 

Another South American animal of the same family is the guanaco. It is found chiefly in southern Chile and Argentina and on the island of Tierra del Fuego. 

The guanaco furnishes the Indians with flesh for food, wool for clothing, and skins for tents. It would be as hard for some of the tribes to live without the guanaco as it would be for the Eskimo or the Laplander to do without the reindeer.

THE LONGEST, NARROWEST COUNTRY 223 

In the zoo we discover another animal, much smaller than any of these mentioned, whose natural home is in the mountains. This is the chinchilla. Have you ever seen anything that was made of its soft gray fur? The chinchilla is a small animal, not much larger than a gray squirrel, and its fur is very expensive. It is caught in traps by the natives, and most of the skins are exported, though the number is constantly decreasing. 

In the evening we go to the Plaza de Armas, the most popular plaza in the city. The Plaza de Armas is the center of the city's life, especially in the evening. 

It is shaded by palm, fir, orange, oleander, and other trees, and has many plants and shrubs. The paved walks are bordered with lovely gardens and green lawns. Crowds of young people are promenading, while the older ones rest on the scattered seats and chat with friends.

If one is really to know Chile, there are three very different parts which one must visit.. The first is the famous Central Valley, near the northern end of which lies the capital city of Santiago. The mountains on either side make the valley plain narrow, and its area is about that of Massachusetts, Rhode Island, and Connecticut combined. Here are most of the cities and towns of Chile, here most of the people live, and here are the farms which supply the Chileans with food and the ships in the harbor of Valparaiso with a large part of their cargo. The Central Valley is so fertile, so productive, and so pleasant that it is often called "the 

Garden of South America."

On the slopes of the mountains on either side of the valley are pastures where large numbers of cattle and

SOUTH AMERICA 

sheep are feeding. Below the pasture lands are fields of alfalfa and many vineyards. The climate of the 

Central Valley is as favorable for the production of good grapes as is the Valley of California, and Chile is FIG. 90. These men are picking grapes in a vineyard in the Central Valley of Chile. Notice the size of the bunches of grapes. (Photograph from 

R. I. Nesmith and Associates) noted for its fine wines. The railroad which runs the length of the valley goes past fields of wheat and corn, peach and pear orchards, and flourishing gardens where beans, onions, squashes, tomatoes, strawberries, and melons look as familiar as if they were growing on our farms at home. The seasons in Chile are, of course, the opposite of ours, and these crops are raised during our winter. Ships carry fruits and vegetables from

THE LONGEST, NARROWEST COUNTRY 225 

Valparaiso to New York by way of the Panama Canal and help to supply our winter markets. 

Farming in the Central Valley is carried on chiefly on large estates which employ many laborers, and there is an increasing number of small farms where the work is done by the owner and his family. The few wealthy farmers who own most of the land in the valley estimate the size of their estates in square miles rather than in acres. They usually live in Santiago for at least a part of the time, and leave the care of their farms to overseers. The Indian or mestizo laborers are given their homes on the estate. These are usually mud huts of one or two rooms. Each laborer has some land on which to raise beans, onions, wheat, corn, and other crops, and is paid a little money. In return he works in the fields of the owner, cultivates his land, and tends his crops. There is usually a store on the estate where the laborers can buy provisions at prices which the proprietor may fix to suit himself.

Many of the cultivated fields in the Central Valley of Chile are inclosed by low banks of earth. These hold in the water, which is let into the fields from time to time. There is little rain here during the summer, and most crops must be irrigated. The water from the swift mountain rivers gives to the plants not only the moisture that they need but much food as well. Rushing down the steep slopes, the streams bring large quantities of silt. This is dropped in the valley, and every year adds to the fertility of the soil. The valley of Chile has been built up in this way by the soil washed from the mountains and therefore it contains some of the richest farming land in the world.

SOUTH AMERICA 

The mountain streams are useful not alone for irrigation but also for the power in their swift waters, which can be used for generating electricity. These rivers give to Chile greater advantages for manufacturing than the countries on the plains possess. 

From what we have seen of the farms of the Central Valley we can guess some of the products that Chile exports. Many of the live cattle and sheep and much of the dried meat go to Bolivia and Peru, while most of the wool and of the fresh and frozen meats go to England and other countries of Europe. The hides and skins are sent chiefly to the United States and to 

European countries. The wheat, oats, and barley go largely to European countries and to Peru. Our most valuable imports from Chile are the minerals, of which we shall read in another chapter, but hides, skins, wool, and some vegetables are also important.

CHAPTER XVIII SOUTHERN CHILE 

The second division of Chile which we must visit if we wish to know the country well is the southern part. The regional map shows you that the great 

Andes System extends to the southernmost tip of South America, and that in this southern part of Chile the mountains descend to the water's edge. You will notice, too, the many islands just off the coast and the numerous fiords, or inlets. Long ago the coast here sank, so that the valleys between the mountains were filled with water and the higher peaks were surrounded by water and so became islands. The high cliffs, the steep shores, the deep, winding fiords, the blue glaciers filling the mountain passes, and the splashing waterfalls tumbling down the slopes remind us of the coast of Alaska in 

North America and of Norway in Europe. On page 229 you can see one of the beautiful glaciers in southern

Chile. People from the warmer parts of the country come to this southern region during the summer to enjoy the cool ocean air, the green forests, the splashing brooks, the tumbling falls, and the fishing and hunting. 

Southern Chile lies in the south temperate zone, in the path of the westerly winds. These winds are filled with moisture which they have taken from the Pacific 

Ocean as they blew over it. As they pass over the mountains they become chilled and drop much of their 228

SOUTHERN CHILE 

229 moisture, so that heavy rains fall on the islands off the coast and on the seaward slopes of the mountains. 

Deep forests grow here. Walking through them, we should find ourselves waist-deep in wet ferns, moss, and grass and shaded from the sun by big trees. Because of the heavy rainfall the ground is wet most FIG. 91. Beautiful lakes lie hidden among the mountains of southern 

Chile. In the picture you see a glacier ending in one of these lakes. (Courtesy of Grace Line) of the time, and therefore lumbering operations are unusually difficult to carry on. Nevertheless, many small sawmills have been set up in the forests, and the industries connected with lumber and other forest products have become increasingly important. 

Magallanes, in southern Chile on the Strait of

Magellan, is one of the southernmost places in the world. It is a thousand miles nearer to the south pole than is Cape Town, at the southern end of Africa.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Magallanes has a population of about twenty-five thousand. Most of the ships that pass through the 

Strait of Magellan stop at this port to leave mail and to take on wool and mutton. Wool, the chief export, is brought here in great quantities from the ranches in this region, some of which are among the largest in the world. As in southern Argentina, shepherds are taking up the land. In their lonely huts, miles away from any neighbors and with no company except their faithful dogs, they care for their large flocks of sheep. So rapidly has the sheep industry spread in the region around Magallanes, in southern Argentina, and on the island of Tierra del Fuego, that millions of sheep now feed in the pastures there. Look at the large flock shown on the opposite page. 

Nearly all the trade connected with the lumbering, hunting, and fishing industries of southern Chile centers in Magallanes. Many improvements have been made in the city in recent years. Electricity is available for lights, and there are wide, paved streets and a number of fine modern buildings. 

Sailing north from Magallanes along the coast of

Chile, we pass many miles of unexplored forested wilderness. A cold current called the Humboldt Current, or the Peru Current, sweeps northward along the

Chilean and Peruvian coasts. Its cool waters chill the winds that blow over it, and the winds, in their turn, chill the coast of the country. Captains of northbound ships plan their courses so that they may stay in this current, which helps them along. At the same time they keep well out from the coast to avoid the dangers of the rock-bound, island-dotted shore.

FIG. 92. There are many sheep ranches in southern Chile. In summer the sheep graze in high pastures, and in winter they are driven down to the more sheltered valleys. (Courtesy of the Chile Publishing Company, N.Y.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Our first stop is at Valdivia, one of the most important places in southern Chile. The town and the surrounding region are peopled largely by Germans. Between three and four hundred thousand people of German descent live in southern Chile. They are thrifty, hard- FIG. 93. The rocky coast line of southern Chile makes navigation dangerous near the shore. What other countries that you can think of have similar coast lines? (Photograph by Lionel Green) working people who live much as they did in their home country, keep many of their old customs, and speak the German language. There are similar prosperous German colonies in southern Brazil. Here in southern Chile these people have cleared away the forests and are working as dairy farmers, fruit-growers, and lumbermen.

Can you find on the map opposite page 168 the Biobio River? For many years this was the boundary

SOUTHERN CHILE 

233 between the Spanish settlements in northern Chile and the Indians who lived in the southern part of the country. The Indians, who were called Araucanians, were one of the bravest tribes in the Western Hemisphere and much superior to any other Indians in South America. The Spanish had many conflicts with them; but there were so many of them, and they fought so fiercely, that the Spanish were unable to conquer them as they had conquered other tribes farther north. 

Many descendants of the Araucanians still live in southern Chile. Their homes are low thatched huts with earth floors and a skin hung for a door. Some of the Indians have given up their old life to become farmers or laborers on the large estates of the Chileans, but others, in the more remote places, live much as their ancestors did centuries ago. Some of them come on horseback or in clumsy oxcarts to southern towns to trade their wheat or corn for a new blanket, a gay handkerchief, or some farming tool. 

As we sail north along the Chilean coast, we may stop at Lota or Coronel, where our ship takes on coal. 

Because of the large coal deposits close by, these towns have become important fueling stations for ships sailing around southern South America. Chile is the principal coal-producing country of South America. 

It produces about a million and a half tons each year. 

In spite of this production, Chile has to import coal because manufacturing is increasing so fast.

In several places the mine openings are on or near the shore, and the gangways extend out for a considerable distance under the ocean itself, so that the miners work hundreds of feet beneath the blue waters of the

SOUTH AMERICA 

Pacific. As we entered the harbor of Coronel, we must have sailed directly over the miners' heads. There is little difference between the mines here and those in our country. All the work is carried on in the most modern way. The gangways are lighted by electricity, and cars run by electricity carry the coal through the tunnels to the shafts, where it is hoisted to the surface. 

Not far away from Coronel is Concepción, the largest city of southern Chile. It is located a few miles from the mouth of the Biobio River. Where this river joins the ocean there is a splendid harbor. This body of water is Chile's southern naval base, and there is always more or less activity here, with naval vessels and merchant ships coming and going. Whaling vessels leave the harbor every year for waters farther south. They bring back whalebone and a large quantity of oil, which is refined here at Concepción before it is sent away. 

The plentiful rainfall in the region around Concepción makes irrigation unnecessary. Had we time to go out into the country, we should see many prosperous farms, most of them owned by Germans. Vegetables and wheat are growing in the rich, dark soil, and cattle and sheep are feeding in green pastures. Some of the farms are on land where the forests have but recently been cleared away, and in places the stumps may still be seen in the fields. Stretching southward from here for about three hundred miles is some of the best farming land in Chile, well-watered and fertile. It is in this region that large quantities of wheat are produced. 

Because ranching and farming are spreading farther south in Chile and because of the coal deposits close by, Concepción is growing in importance.

SOUTHERN CHILE 

235 Chile has a long coast line of over twenty-six hundred miles. On it are more than sixty seaports. On our way to the northern part of the country we will stop at the largest of these ports. This is Valparaiso. 

It is not only the largest seaport in Chile but it is the largest port on the west coast of South America. It is also the door to Santiago, the capital, which lies a hundred and seventeen miles away, on the other side of the coast range of mountains. Most of the trade of 

Santiago passes through the port of Valparaiso. 

Many cities in the world have poor harbors because their waters are not deep enough. In Valparaiso the opposite is true. Mountains border the coast, and their steep slopes descend to a great depth close to the shore. 

In such deep water it is very difficult and expensive to build breakwaters and sea walls to protect the shipping in the harbor from the high winds and great waves of the ocean. Many improvements have been made, yet even now much damage is done to shipping when the fierce "northers" sweep down during the winter months.

The word valparaiso means "vale of paradise." 

The city was named not for its beauty, but because the homesick explorer who captured the Indian village which stood here wished to have something to remind him of his home, the sunny town of Valparaiso in Spain. 

The Chilean city lies in a half-circle behind a fine sea wall which extends around the bay. Between the mountains and the sea there is only a narrow plain which in some places, as you can see in Fig. 95, is only wide enough for two or three streets. This low part of the city near the water is the business quarter and the

SOUTH AMERICA 

home of many of the poorer people. The streets are full of trucks, automobiles, and high two-wheeled oxcarts similar to those which we saw in Argentina. In some cases the carts are loaded with fruits, vegetables, and produce of different kinds for the city markets. FIG. 94. The docks of the harbor of Valparaiso are well equipped with machinery for loading and unloading ships. In the center of the picture you can see part of the mole, or breakwater, which helps to protect the harbor and alongside of which ships may anchor During the morning hours the markets are crowded with customers buying their supplies for the day.

On the cliffs and hills back of the plain is the section. of the city where most of the well-to-do and wealthy people live. You can go from the lower to the upper city by winding paths, stone stairways, inclined railways, or public elevators. At night the many lights curving along the water front and rising higher and higher on the cliffs and hills make a beautiful sight from the water. It is pleasant and cool on the heights,

FIG. 95. Valparaiso is built on nineteen hills of varying heights. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

SOUTH AMERICA 

and the view of the lower city and the bay is lovely. 

Yet even here among the fine houses and beautiful gardens there are sights which seem strange to us. Peddlers pack their vegetables and fruits on the backs of donkeys, and the milkmen use donkeys to carry their cans of milk. It is easier for the animals to carry their loads on their backs up the very steep hills than it is to pull heavy wagons.

In the harbor of Valparaiso there are ships flying the flags of many nations. The smaller craft sail back and forth between the west-coast ports of South America. There are large sailing vessels and great ocean steamers from far-distant parts of the world. 

Quantities of freight are brought to Valparaiso. There are cotton, woolen, and silk goods from England and France; iron and steel manufactures from the United 

States, Germany, and England; coal from England; and automobiles and petroleum from the United States. There are many other products brought to 

Chile, the greater part of them being manufactured goods.

Manufacturing is growing in Valparaiso, Santiago, and other cities and towns of Chile. Highest in value among the manufacturing industries of the country are foodstuffs. The leather industry is also of importance. 

Hides and skins are made into leather which is used in the shoe factories. Other leading manufactures are clothing, textiles, and lumber.

CHAPTER XIX NORTHERN CHILE AND ITS DESERT PRODUCTS 

Northern Chile, the third division of the country, is the least attractive part, yet for many years its resources proved to be more valuable than those of either of the other two divisions. It is very different from central or southern Chile. In the south there are miles and miles of rain-soaked forests, but in the north there is a forbidding desert where no trees grow. 

In the south it is always cool or cold; in the Central 

Valley the summers are hot; and in the north it is warm or hot the year round, except on the high mountains. Southern Chile is well-watered all the year; in the Central Valley rain falls in the winter, and the summers are dry; but in the northern part of the country there is no rain at any time of the year. The records of one place in this part of Chile show that only one inch of rain has fallen in twenty years. In central Chile and in large areas farther south, farming and ranching are carried on. But there are no cattle or sheep in the northern part of the country, for no grass grows there, and no crops can be raised because of the lack of rain. 

Perhaps you are wondering why there is so much rain in southern Chile and such a lack of it in the northern part of the country. You read on pages 228-229 about the rainfall in southern Chile. Why is it so heavy there? Now, if you look at the map, you will 241

SOUTH AMERICA 

see that northern Chile extends into the torrid zone. 

It is therefore in the belt of the southeast trade winds, which blow across South America from the Atlantic Ocean. It receives no rainfall from these winds, however, for the high ranges of the Andes force them to 

FIG. 96. Tocopilla, an important nitrate and copper port of northern

Chile, lies close to the shore, with bare brown mountains rising close behind. (Courtesy of Grace Line) drop their moisture on the eastern slopes of the mountains, and there is none left for the lands west of the mountains. Therefore along the coast of northern 

Chile, across the southwestern corner of Bolivia, and all along the coast of Peru stretches a belt of desert land. Yet out of the wealth found in its northern deserts and in the fertile strip of the Central Valley, Chile has made for itself a name as a strong, progressive nation.

DESERT PRODUCTS OF NORTHERN CHILE 243 

About one hundred and thirty miles north of Valparaiso we come to Cruz Grande, a small seaport but a busy one because of the beds of iron that are found a few miles inland. Long docks have been built out from the shore, and we see several ships beside them. The ore is brought from the mines by electric railway, and the cars run out on the docks on trestles that are high above the ships. The cars are built in such a way that the ore slides down from them directly into the holds of the ships. Most of the iron ore shipped away from this port comes to the United States. The iron deposits here, together with the coal beds farther south, are of great value to Chile, for they help to develop manufacturing industries.

Farther north along the Chilean coast we notice that the trees along the shore and on the slopes of the hills and mountains gradually disappear, then the lower shrubs and bushes, and finally the green grass, until at last there is nothing except a bare brown plain near the water, backed by bare brown hills and mountains. This desolate region in northern Chile is called the Atacama Desert. Nothing grows here; yet from this desert region comes nitrate of soda, the most valuable product of the country.

If we go up into the desert, we can find out about this product, but our visit will not be very pleasant. 

We are on the edge of the tropics, and the country is hot. As it never rains here, the dust blows everywhere and penetrates every crevice. The trip on some of the railroads will take us so high that we shall be troubled with mountain sickness. Nitrate of soda is one of the best foods in the world to make plants grow and im-

SOUTH AMERICA 

prove farmers' crops, yet in the region where it is found there are no plants, trees, or shrubs. Isn't it odd that while foods of all kinds must be shipped into this region, one of the best food-producing materials in the world is the chief product shipped away?

In order to live and thrive, plants, like people, need water and food. They obtain their food from the earth, and in time they take much of the goodness out of the soil. To insure good crops this plant food must be replaced by fertilizers. One of the foods that plants need most is nitrogen, but they can make use of it only when it is mixed with other substances, thus forming compounds known as nitrates. Nitrate of soda is the only fertilizer which contains this food in a suitable form, and Chile is the only country in the world that possesses large deposits of such nitrates. Many companies have invested large sums of money in the desert lands of Chile to build railroads, establish plants, and induce men to live here to mine and prepare the material and ship it to foreign ports. 

We can go into the nitrate region from several ports in northern Chile where short railroads lead up from the coast. The picture on page 242 shows you one of these ports. The shipping of nitrate and minerals and the importing of articles needed by the people living here form the only business of importance in these places. All food and clothing supplies, tools, machinery, and everything needed both in the homes and in the nitrate industry must be brought to these ports and distributed throughout the desert. Even the drinking-water has to be piped from the mountains many miles away. In some of the ports the salt ocean

DESERT PRODUCTS OF NORTHERN CHILE 245 

water is distilled and made fresh. This is used for trees and plants which grow in a few parks and gardens and which give the only touch of green to the land. 

As you look on the map opposite page 168 at this desert coast of Chile, you will notice that several of the seaports are connected by railroad with the long line which runs through central Chile and with the railroads leading up into Bolivia. 

Antofagasta, a city of over fifty thousand people, is the largest of the nitrate ports. It is wedged in on a narrow plain between the sea in front and the brown hills and mountains behind. Brown seems to be the prevailing color. The houses are brown, the streets are brown, and the hills that rise behind the town are brown and bare. It never rains here, and water has to be brought from the mountains nearly two hundred miles away. In the parks and squares are trees, shrubs, and blossoming plants, and in the outskirts, by means of irrigation, farmers raise some fruit and vegetables for use in the city. In Figs. 97 and 98 you can see two contrasting views of Antofagasta.

An immense amount of freight is carried on the railroad leading up the slopes from Antofagasta into 

Bolivia. Everything needed for the people farther inland must be carried to them, and not only nitrate but large quantities of copper from some of the richest mines in the world and of silver and tin from the Bolivian mines are taken down to Antofagasta to be exported. 

About one hundred and sixty-five miles northeast of Antofagasta a branch railroad joins the main line on which we are riding. The branch extends out to the town of Chuquicamata, where a company from the

SOUTH AMERICA 

United States is carrying on mining in a mountain built of copper. The Chuquicamata mines are famous. 

Look at the picture on page 248 and think how many workmen there must be to occupy all the houses that you see. The ore is dug out by great steam shovels and loaded on cars which carry it to the smelters, FIG. 97. In this view of Antofagasta there are no trees, flowers, or grass in sight. On page 247 is a view in another part of the same city. How do you account for the difference in the views? (Courtesy of Grace Line) where the copper is separated from the rock and other impurities with which it is mixed. The copper is molded into small bars, which are carried in long trainloads down to Antofagasta and exported to other countries. Much of this copper comes to the United States. 

Copper and nitrate are the two most important products of Chile and are by far the most valuable of its exports. All the nitrate and much of the copper are obtained in the northern part of the country, although there are copper mines also in the south near Lota, where the rich coal beds are.

Directly after leaving Antofagasta we begin to climb the slopes. Never before have we seen such a country. Great bare hills rise into bare mountains; bare plains and plateaus dip into bare valleys. Nowhere do we see a green thing-a tree, a shrub, or even a blade of grass. We pass gray salt plains large FIG. 99. The small houses in this picture are the homes of the laborers who work in the famous copper mines of Chuquicamata. The copper deposits here are some of the largest in the world enough to supply the world with that much-needed article. We see white, glistening lakes covered with hummocks, not of ice, as it looks to us from a distance, but of borax, and we see men at work digging up the borax and loading it onto cars. Farther on there are stretches of black lava, the result of eruptions of some old volcano which has long been quiet. Along the railroad track are telegraph and telephone poles, and close beside it there runs for miles a great pipe line through

DESERT PRODUCTS OF NORTHERN CHILE 249 

which flows water for the railroad, the stations, and the city of Antofagasta.

As we ride along we cannot help thinking what a difference in the appearance of the country a few good rains would make. The slopes would be green and the valleys filled with flowers, and it seems as if the people who live here would be much happier. Such is not the case, however. This is one place in the world. where the people do not want rain. If the climate should change and a large amount of rain should fall, the wealth of the people would disappear, for the water would gradually dissolve the nitrate and carry it away to the ocean.

For nearly five hundred miles the nitrate deposits extend north and south in a long, narrow strip about two and a half miles wide. They are found from fifteen to one hundred miles inland on a plain varying from 3600 to 10,000 feet above sea level. The largest deposits lie in a high valley four or five thousand feet above sea level between the Andes Mountains and coast ranges. 

Every now and then branch tracks put off from the main line, on which we are riding, and run out to long, low buildings with tall chimneys. These are the oficinas, where the nitrate rock, called caliche, is crushed and treated. On the sidings at the stations and on these branch lines we can see hundreds of bags waiting to be sent down to Antofagasta. The cars coming up the slopes are as full as those going down. Thousands of men work in the nitrate fields, and the supplies that they need must be brought in by train. 

There are a good many buildings in a nitrate plant. 

The little cabins of corrugated iron are the homes

SOUTH AMERICA 

of the laborers, and the larger, better buildings are the homes of the overseers, engineers, and other skilled workmen from the United States and European countries. We can see some tennis courts and, a little farther on, a schoolhouse and a store. Before us is a large FIG. 100. All these cars loaded with caliche are waiting to be unloaded at the oficina of which you see part of the buildings in the background. What will be done to the caliche? (Courtesy of Nitrate Corporation of Chile) building with tall chimneys, and at one side there are a number of big open tanks. Men are shoveling up the grayish-white material, filling bags, loading cars, and letting water into huge vats.

The deposits of caliche usually lie four feet or more below the surface. Workmen bore a hole deep enough to reach below the caliche beds, put powder into it, and light a fuse. Then they run to a safe distance.

DESERT PRODUCTS OF NORTHERN CHILE 251 

Soon there comes a terrific blast, and earth and stone are hurled high into the air. After the explosion laborers sort out the caliche and load it on cars or mule carts to be taken to the oficina. The superintendent tells us that these pieces of gray rock are nearly half pure nitrate. In some places, however, the rock does not yield more than a third or a fourth of its bulk in nitrate.

The rocky mass is crushed by machinery into a powder. This is carried to immense tanks filled with water and fitted inside with steam pipes. In these tanks a great steaming and bubbling goes on. When the nitrate is dissolved, the worthless part sinks to the bottom of the tanks, and the liquid is drawn off into large shallow vats. Here it cools, and the nitrate forms in crystals on the sides and bottom. The water is drawn off, and workmen shovel the white, sparkling stuff onto the drying boards, where it remains exposed to the hot sun and dry air until every bit of moisture has disappeared. It is then graded according to quality and put into bags to be shipped away. The very best nitrate is used in making powder and other explosives; some goes into different compounds, such as bleaching powders and dyestuffs; some is used in the manufacture of glass and steel; but the great bulk of the product is used in making fertilizers. Large quantities are shipped to European countries for use on farms, and thousands of tons come to the United States. 

Some of our corporations engaged in the manufacture of explosives own large areas in northern Chile and import large quantities of nitrates to be prepared in their factories in the United States.

FIG. 101. This is another view of the oficina shown in Fig. 100. Study this picture carefully as you read about the work carried on in the oficina described on page 251. (Courtesy of Nitrate Corporation of Chile) FIG. 102. These bags of nitrate will be shipped to the United States. 

Chilean nitrate goes to all parts of the world, but our country imports more than any other. (Courtesy of Nitrate Corporation of Chile)

DESERT PRODUCTS OF NORTHERN CHILE 253 

Have you ever used iodine to heal some cut or bruise?

Iodine is useful also in medicines, in photography, in making dyes, and for other purposes. The liquid which is left after the nitrate crystallizes is treated in various ways and with different substances until the iodine appears as violet-colored crystals. Iodine is a valuable product. It is shipped in small casks, each of which, when filled, is worth several hundred dollars. 

No one knows how long the Chilean nitrate deposits will last. Some authorities say that they will be exhausted in fifty years, while others think that they will last for two or three centuries. Sooner or later, however, the time will come when they will be gone. For years people have wondered where we shall then get the plant food which the crops of the world demand. 

The air contains large quantities of nitrogen. By taking this nitrogen from the atmosphere, combining it with other substances, getting it into such a form that plants can use it, and doing all this cheaply enough to make the product commercially valuable, we should have enough of this plant food for all time. In the great laboratories of the world, chemists have worked for many years on this problem, and today artificial nitrates are made in large quantities.

CHAPTER XX  

We have already visited one country in South 

America which has no seacoast. What is it? Now we are to visit Bolivia, the only other inland country of that continent. Unlike Paraguay, Bolivia once had a seacoast. This coastal part of Bolivia was given to 

Chile, however, after a war with that country in the latter half of the nineteenth century. 

Bolivia is shut off from the Pacific Ocean by Chile and Peru and lies a long distance inland from the Atlantic. Its only connection with the coast is by railroads which join lines running through other countries. It is on one of these connecting lines, beginning at the Chilean port of Antofagasta, that we began our trip into the nitrate region of Chile, and it is on this same line that we shall continue into Bolivia. On the regional map some of these railroads are shown. 

On our trip up into Bolivia we shall climb to a great height, for much of the country lies in the midst of the Andes System. This great system consists not only of mountain ranges but of vast plateaus, some of which are among the highest in the world. In Bolivia the plateau region has an average height of more than twelve thousand feet above sea level and stretches north and south for more than five hundred miles. 

High mountain ranges stand on either side of this 255

SOUTH AMERICA 

plateau, and some of the peaks here rise more than a mile higher into the blue sky. The highest mountain in the Americas is on the boundary between Chile and 

Argentina. What is its name? 

Strange to say, most of the cities of Bolivia are on the high plateau between the mountain ranges, and most of the people of the country, both white and Indian, live there. As in the case of the nitrates in 

Chile, it is the minerals found in the Bolivian mountains which have led people to make their homes in this high, cold, desolate region. To see the people and to learn about the famous mines, we shall have to visit this part of the country. 

Ever since leaving Antofagasta we have been climbing up the steep slopes of the Andes toward the Plateau of Bolivia. Everything has been bare and brown, and we see no trees, shrubs, or grass until we reach the valley of the Loa River. In the valley of this little river are some villages where grain and vegetables are growing and where cattle and sheep are feeding in green pastures.

Farther on, the little river rushes to the ocean through a deep, narrow gorge over three hundred feet below the bridge which crosses it (Fig. 103). Not only is our train more than three hundred feet above the river, but it is ten thousand feet higher than it was when it left the railroad station at Antofagasta.

Climbing up the slopes, we have noticed from time to time a large pipe line beside the track. Through this pipe line water is led down to Antofagasta. The water from small mountain rivers is stored high on the slopes in big reservoirs blasted out of solid rock. From the

reservoirs the water is piped down to the coast. This is the only water supply for the railroad, for workers in this part of the nitrate fields, and for Antofagasta. No other water can be obtained in the region except by distilling the salty ocean water and making it pure and fresh. FIG. 103. This is the bridge which spans the narrow valley through which the Loa River flows. Why is the land in the valley fertile while the steep slopes are bare and brown? (Courtesy of Grace Line)

After crossing the first mountain range, we descend a short distance into a high valley. Here a wonderful view spreads out before us. To the east and west stretch high snow-capped mountains. Their bare, rocky walls, stained by mineral deposits, glitter and sparkle in rainbow colors. In the center of the valley lies a glistening lake covered with borax, a white material that looks like snow, with stretches of blue-green water showing here and there. There are other borax lakes in this dry part of South America. This mineral

SOUTH AMERICA 

is found in many rainless regions, but most of the world's supply comes from South America and California. Laborers dig out the borax in great lumps, which are spread out to dry. Then they are heated until a mass of powdery crystals forms. The crystals are packed in bags and sent by rail down to the coast towns to be shipped away.

After we cross the mountain valley, we again begin to climb up the steep slopes on its eastern side. We pass by and sometimes stop at stations where branches run out from the main line to copper mines hidden among the mountains. Some of the mines in this region were worked for centuries before the Spanish explorers left their homes in Europe to find a new world. Among the copper mines more recently opened are some of the richest and most famous in the world. Great quantities of copper ore are brought on the little branch railroads to the main line and sent down to Antofagasta for export. From some mines mules and llamas bring the ore on their backs to the railroad, though fewer of these animals are used now than formerly. Near some of the mines great smelters have been built in which the copper is separated from the worthless rock. Then the refined copper is exported.

Still climbing, we cross the boundary between Chile and Bolivia, the highest, coldest, barest country in all South America, and proceed to Uyuni. The air is so thin here on the plateau that the sun's rays are hot, and if we stay in the sunshine we shall keep fairly warm during the daytime sometimes perhaps uncomfortably so. But on cloudy days, and especially at night, the weather is cold and disagreeable. Indeed, there

are few nights on the plateau when ice does not form. 

With no fuel to heat the houses and with the air so thin that violent exercise is impossible, the only way to get warm is to go to bed. 

On the regional map find the railroad that runs from Uyuni down through Argentina to the ports on the Plata River. This is Bolivia's only outlet to the Atlantic 

Ocean. We are not going in that direction, however, but will continue our trip northward on the main line of the railroad from Antofagasta for some distance and then branch off to the east to reach Potosí. This was once an important city, much larger than it is today. During the rule of the Spanish in Bolivia the mines in and around Potosí produced more than three and a half billion dollars' worth of silver. The city is still famous as a mining center, but its tin mines are now more noted than its silver mines. It is also noted for being one of the highest cities in the world. It is about fourteen thousand feet above sea level.

An old mint, built in 1775, is still standing in Potosí. 

In the days of the Spanish rule the Indians brought to this mint the silver which the Spaniards forced them to mine. One can still see the little cars built to carry the silver bars to the stamping machines, the old dies, and the records made centuries ago. The carved stone head on the front of the building, which smiled down on the glittering treasures of the Spaniards, still looks down on Indians and droves of llamas which gather around the fountain beneath it.

Only a few countries in the world contain deposits of tin. Bolivia is one of the world's greatest producers, yielding each year on the average about a fifth of the

SOUTH AMERICA 

world's production. Only British Malaya and the 

Netherlands Indies produce more tin than Bolivia. Silver and gold were the minerals for which the Spanish conquerors searched in bygone days, and the tin ore was thrown away as useless. Today Bolivia's tin exports FIG. 104. The waters of this swift-flowing mountain river, not far from 

Potosí, contain tin ore, which is obtained by dredging. Notice the huts of the Indian laborers in the foreground are far more valuable than all its other exports combined. Some tin is dredged from rivers and some is obtained in deep mines, as you see in the pictures on this page and page 265.

Not far from the city of Potosí is a mountain of the same name. Mount Potosí is famous, for it is composed largely of silver and tin and is one of the most important mining regions in Bolivia. Before the building of the branch railroad to Potosí, all the products of the many mines in the vicinity had to be brought to the

main line on the backs of mules and llamas, and all the machinery, food, and other supplies needed in the city and at the mines had to be carried back in the same slow, difficult way. It is not to be wondered at, then, FIG. 105. The roof of the old mint in Potosí is covered with red tiles, and the windows have heavy bars across them. (Courtesy of Grace Line) that since the building of the branch road to Potosí the output of the mines there has greatly increased. 

Some of the mines around Potosí are equipped with modern machinery. There are smelters too, for crushing the ore and separating the metals from the rock Iwith which they are found. In contrast with these mines there are others where Indians are working just as other Indians did three or four hundred years ago, crawling in on their hands and knees through small

SOUTH AMERICA 

openings, digging out the rock with pickaxes, bringing it out of the mine in sacks on their backs, and pounding it by hand to break up the ore. The miners wear thick knitted caps to protect their heads from falling stones, and leather pads over their knees. Those that carry the ore out of the mine wear heavy leather aprons on their backs.

The greatest need of Bolivia in developing its mineral wealth is railroads, but in such a mountainous country the building of either railroads or highways is extremely expensive. Toward the close of the last century railroads began to open up the way to some of the richest mineral areas, especially those rich in tin. 

Some branch lines were built by the government and others by the companies which were developing the mines. One of the largest of these companies, operating near Potosí, has built a high cable line for carrying the ore in buckets from the mine to the smelter, six miles away. This line, a small part of which you can see in 

Fig. 106, is sixteen thousand feet above sea level and is probably the highest line of its kind in the world. 

At the smelter the tin is separated from the rock waste and sent sixty-eight miles farther by motor cars over a highway built by the company to the main Bolivian line. From here it goes down to Antofagasta for export. 

Think how many mules, llamas, and Indian drivers were needed to carry the ore over rough, stony trails to the main railroad line before the cable line and highway were built! Other companies have banded together and built branch railroads and smelters. 

Mining is being carried on in Bolivia on a large scale and by the most scientific methods known. One

FIG. 106. Each of the buckets which slide down this cable line, one after the other, holds half a ton of tin ore. The cables are supported by towers placed at regular intervals along the line. In the background are the buildings of the mining camp

SOUTH AMERICA 

mining company employs about five thousand workmen, who, with their families, bring the number of people to be provided for up to about thirteen thousand. Many houses are needed for all these people, besides stores, offices, and other buildings. There is a church, a school, a hospital, a hydroelectric plant, and other conveniences that are found in a modern city. 

All this development is in a bare, desolate region fourteen thousand feet above sea level, where, on the warmest day ever recorded, the temperature was below sixty degrees F., and where every day the chill wind blows across the bleak plateau.

Much of the tin ore exported from Bolivia is sent to Great Britain to be smelted, and some is smelted in the 

United States. But more and more smelters are being built near the mines, and the tin is being exported in refined form. Very little tin is produced in the United 

States and its possessions, but we use more than all other countries of the world put together. 

One of the most interesting places in Bolivia is the old city of Sucre. Find it on the map opposite page 256 and notice that it is at the end of the branch railroad which connects Potosí with the main line. Sucre is the legal capital of Bolivia, but until the completion of the railroad line in recent years it was so difficult to reach that most of the government business was carried on at La Paz, a larger and more important city and a more conveniently located one. With the help of the railroad line, Sucre may become of greater importance as the capital in the future. The city has many ancient buildings, low houses, quaint markets, and old customs. 

Many of the wealthy families of Bolivia live here.

Oruro is the next place of importance at which we shall stop on our railroad trip. Find it on the map. 

This city is both a railroad center and a mining center. 

We should know that much mining is carried on here from the piles of rock waste and the chimneys of the smelters. 

If we had time to make some trips off into the hills, we should find copper, tin, and silver mines, the most important of which are the tin mines. Some of the mines are equipped with modern machinery, shafts, elevators, cars, smelters, and stamp mills.

There are other mines where the Indian laborers work with pickaxes and shovels and climb in and out of the mines through small holes just as they did in the days of the Spanish explorers. Some of the mines are connected with the main railroad by short branch lines; from others the ore is still sent to the railroad in bags on the backs of llamas. 

FIG. 107. These men are in a tin mine near 

Oruro. They are fastening a car loaded with tin ore to a cable which will hoist it through a shaft to the surface Oruro, one of the principal cities in Bolivia, has a population of about thirty thousand. Several large mining companies have offices here, and among the people

SOUTH AMERICA 

are Englishmen, Germans, Chileans, and people from the United States. There are llama trains in the narrow streets, Indian women with full, bright-colored skirts in the market place, and smelters with tall chimneys outside the city. Most of the low, one-story houses are built of sun-dried brick or stone. As is usual in 

Spanish cities, there is a large central plaza surrounded by shops, public buildings, and a large cathedral. In the hills and mountains on either side of the city are the mineral treasures to which Oruro owes its existence. 

Let us take the branch line that runs northeast from Oruro and visit the city of Cochabamba, about one hundred and twenty-five miles away. This city is one of the most important in Bolivia. It lies in a fertile valley several thousand feet lower than Potosí, and therefore has a milder climate than that city and other cities in the higher parts of the Andes. Unlike most of the Andean cities, Cochabamba is more important as an agricultural center than as a mining center. Wheat, corn, and other crops are grown in the vicinity of the city. 

It is the business center for a large number of smaller neighboring towns. We enjoy visiting some of its fine public buildings before returning by train to Oruro. 

From Oruro the railroad on which we are riding continues northward over the high plateau. On the east rises a great wall of snow-capped mountains, so high that their heads seem to touch the sky. On the west also rise high peaks, and ahead of us stretches the vast plain. We pass some little groups of mud huts, near which Indians are working in the fields. 

Their plow is a crooked stick with one handle and a flat piece of iron fastened to the point by a string of

rawhide. This makes a furrow only five or six inches deep. Instead of a yoke, the bullocks which draw the plow wear a crosspiece of wood lashed behind the horns. 

A woman and child follow in the furrow to break up FIG. 108. Do you think that the Bolivian Indians can raise large crops when their farming methods are of the crudest sort? What crop may this 

Indian be going to plant in the field he is plowing (see text below)? (Courtesy of Grace Line) the heavy clods of earth. In the picture on this page you see some bullocks drawing such a plow. The only crops that can be raised on the cold, dry plateau are barley, wheat, corn, potatoes, and quinoa. The quinoa plant looks like a weed, but it is very useful to the Indians here. They boil its leaves for greens and make the seeds into a kind of mush.

More than half of the people of Bolivia are Indians.

SOUTH AMERICA 

They work in mines, drive the llama trains, and raise the stunted grains and vegetables that will grow here. 

Most of the land in Bolivia, like that in Chile and other South American countries, is divided into large estates. 

These are dotted here and there with little stone or mud huts occupied by the Indians, who cultivate the ground and tend the cattle, sheep, goats, alpacas, and llamas. In return for their work the landowner supplies his laborers with wool for clothing, and with salt, sugar, and other articles of food which they cannot produce. 

Each Indian has also a small piece of ground to cultivate for himself.

There are more Indians in South America than in any other continent. You have read about those who live in the interior of Guiana, Brazil, and other countries and in southern Chile and Argentina, but more Indians make their homes on the high Andean plateaus than in any other part of South America.

Many of the Indians of both Bolivia and Peru are pack-animal drivers and spend their time on the trails. 

Much of the ore and other freight has to be carried long distances over steep, rocky paths. These trips take days and weeks and even months. The freight is carried by donkeys and mules and, on the higher elevations, by llamas.

The llama is a graceful animal with a long neck and slender legs. Its little head is carried high in the air, and its soft brown eyes and sharp-pointed ears are always alert like those of a high-bred dog. Notice these things in the picture on page 269. The llama is as useful to the Indians of South America as the reindeer is to the Eskimos of the Far North and the camel to the

people of the great deserts. The Indians weave cloth from the wool of the llama, eat its flesh, and make it carry their burdens. 

Like the camel, the llama can go for days without food. When turned out to forage for itself, it satisfies FIG. 109. The Indian is loading his llama for a trip over the trails of the plateau. The bag on the animal's back weighs about forty pounds. Notice how intelligent the llama appears. (Courtesy of Grace Line) its hunger by nibbling the tough grass or the low, prickly shrubs that grow on the high tableland. On long trips, when a place is reached where there is good food for the animals, the driver always halts and lets them feed for a while. It makes no difference to him whether he stops for a few minutes or a few hours, for time is of little value to a Bolivian Indian.

The llama knows well just how heavy a load it can

SOUTH AMERICA 

carry over the rough mountain paths. If the driver fastens too heavy a load onto its back, it will lie down, and no amount of coaxing or whipping will make it get up until the load is made lighter. 

On our right we see a train of llamas making their way across the wind-swept plateau. There must be forty or fifty of them, and they are accompanied by an Indian driver and his wife, who walk beside the animals and who can make as long a day's march as their fourfooted companions. The man wears a hat like the one worn by the Indian in Fig. 109. He wears a poncho, which is a sort of blanket with a hole in the center for his head. The poncho was once very brilliant, but the dirt on it has softened and toned down its bright colors. 

His feet are bare, though the morning was so cold that ice formed on our drinking-water. 

His wife wears a small felt hat, from under which her hair hangs in two long braids down her back. She has on many wool skirts, very full around the waist, and each one of a different color. If the weather grows warm or the sun too hot, she may remove the red outside skirt and show a purple one beneath. Under this there may be one of bright green, and the next one may prove to be a vivid blue. The women are very proud of these skirts and like to show their number and their bright colors as well as some women in our country like to show their fine dresses.

The woman is spinning as she walks. It is seldom that one sees an Indian woman with empty hands.

She has very little time to sit down and spin the llama wool, and so, even when climbing the hardest trails, her fingers are always busy twirling the spools and

winding the wool into yarn. Later she will dye the yarn with the juice of some plant and knit a cap, or weave a poncho so close and firm that it will shed water as well as does a raincoat made in the United FIG. 110. The loom which this Indian woman is using consists of two logs between which are stretched the long warp threads. Her shuttle, with which she weaves the woof thread over and under, is made from the bone of a sheep. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

States. The picture above shows you a woman weaving llama wool into cloth on her rude homemade loom. 

The man carries with him a sling shot and a little bag filled with small stones. With these he urges the slow animals forward and checks those which would stray from the trail.

Both the llama driver and his wife are chewing, not gum, as one might think, but the leaves of the coca

SOUTH AMERICA 

plant. Do not confuse this plant with the cacao tree, which bears the beans from which our cocoa and chocolate are made, or with the coconut palm tree. The coca plant is a shrub which grows on the eastern slopes of the Andes Mountains, over toward Brazil. Sometimes the shrubs are raised on large plantations called cocales. 

The Indians pick the leaves and spread them out to dry. Then they pack them in skin bags, which are carried by mules and llamas up over the mountains to the cities and towns on the plateau. The cultivation of the coca plant is an important industry in parts of eastern Bolivia. In one season during a recent year there was sent to La Paz, from a region where there are many plantations, more than half a million baskets of coca leaves, each basket weighing about seventy pounds. Some of the leaves are shipped to Europe and the United States to be used in the manufacture of cocaine, which is used by dentists and doctors to deaden pain. When you study about eastern Bolivia you will learn more about the coca industry. 

The Indians of Peru and Bolivia think that they cannot work unless they have coca leaves to chew. In whatever occupation he may be engaged, a workman is given, besides his regular wages, a quantity of these leaves. The Indians' food is not very plentiful or very nourishing, and we do not wonder that they need something more to enable them to do the hard work in the mines and on the farms and to take their long journeys over the rough mountain trails. One of their favorite dishes is chupe, a kind of stew made of scraps of llama meat and frozen potatoes and other vegetables, and highly seasoned with red pepper. In any village into

Underwood and Underwood

FIG. 111. Notice the long, fine wool of this alpaca. When it is sheared from the animal, it will make warm clothing which we might go we should be sure to find a kettle of chupe cooking over the little clay stove.

As we go northward we see many llamas feeding on the plateau. There are also herds of alpacas, which at a distance we mistake for llamas, and we catch glimpses now and then of herds of vicuñas. Small quantities of vicuña wool are produced, and most of this is used in South America for making beautiful ponchos, warm rugs, fine blankets, shawls, caps, and mittens.

SOUTH AMERICA 

There are many alpacas in Bolivia and Peru. These animals live and graze at great heights. They are seldom found on lands lower than six thousand feet above sea level and seem to keep in the best condition on lands between twelve and sixteen thousand feet above the sea. Their wool is fine and long, often hanging nearly or quite to the ground.

Most of the alpacas are owned by Indians, who let them roam on the high lands. Once in two years the owners clip off the wool with knives or shears just as Indians on the plateau have done for centuries. The shearing is done in large inclosures made by surrounding patches of ground with stone walls. The walls were probably built hundreds of years ago by the Indians who then lived on the plateau and needed the wool of their alpacas for warm clothing. 

On the bleak, level tableland which stretches for miles to the distant mountains, we stop at the station of Viacha, over thirteen thousand feet above the sea.

Here the railroad divides into three branches. One line goes on to La Paz, another swings off to the southern end of Lake Titicaca, and a third winds its way through the western mountains down to Arica on the Chilean coast. Find these three branches on the map opposite page 256. Which of the three is the longest?

We are going on to La Paz, the largest city in Bolivia. 

Already we can see the white head of Mount Illimani, which watches over it. We are told that we are approaching the city, and we look eagerly for our first view. On the high, level tableland we see some Indians working near their mud huts, and many llamas, but we catch no glimpse of houses, streets, churches, and shops

FIG. 112. In spite of the fact that La Paz lies in a deep valley, it is one of the highest cities in the world, being nearly two and a half miles above sea level. The snow-capped peak is Mount Illimani

SOUTH AMERICA 

such as we should expect to see in a large city like La Paz. The train stops and we alight, but still no city comes into view. Suddenly, over the edge of a steep cliff, fifteen hundred feet below the level on which we are standing, we see its red roofs, tall spires, white and bright-colored houses, and tree-lined streets.

When the Spanish explorers first entered Bolivia, they found that the Indians of this region washed gold from the sands of a little river here. The Spaniards started a settlement on its banks and forced the Indians to continue the washing of gold for them. Goldmining has long since been abandoned here; but the sheltered position of the settlement, the abundant water supply, and the opening at one end of the gorge where a trail led down to the eastern slope of the Andes caused La Paz to grow and prosper, until today it is a city of about a hundred and fifty thousand people. 

Notice in the picture on page 275 the valley in which the city lies and the mountains around it. 

Formerly a narrow roadway carved out of the side of the cliff led from the plateau down to the bottom of the gorge where the city lies, and a stage drawn by mules carried the passengers down the winding path. The driver, liking to show his skill, did not always slow up around the sharp corners or on the steepest places. Today two railroads zigzag down the cliffs, at every turn giving the passengers a different but always beautiful view. 

The sides and bottom of the canyon in which La Paz is situated are so uneven that few buildings stand on level land. Some of them are one story high on one side and two or three stories high on the other. How steep the hills are! It is no wonder that there are few

FIG. 113. Some of the streets of La Paz are so steep that it has been necessary to build steps instead of a roadway. (Courtesy of Grace Line) wagons in the city, for no animal could pull a load up such steep grades. Some of the freight is carried on cars, but much is still borne on the backs of mules, donkeys, llamas, or Indians, and in some places the narrow streets are crowded with these burden-bearers.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Here come some llamas carrying bags of coca leaves. 

Behind these come others loaded with the bark of the cinchona tree, from which quinine is to be made. 

Some of the animals are bringing balls of rubber and bags of fruit into the city, while some are climbing down the steep trail from the plateau with goods which have been brought from other countries — cloth, boots and shoes, machinery, sugar, rice, and dozens of other things, large and small, which the people of the city need.

On the streets there are many well-dressed Spaniards, as well as Indians in ponchos and bright-colored skirts. 

Besides the Indians and the Spanish, there are also many cholos, or half-breeds. The men dress much as the men in our country do, but the women still cling to the gay shawl and the many full, bright skirts. Some of them wear shoes with stockings as bright as their skirts, while others wear neither shoes nor stockings.

Many of the cholos are business men and keep the stores in the older part of the city. Some of these shops are small, dark rooms with no windows. Through the open doors we can see the proprietor and his assistants at work, for in many cases the merchant makes the articles which he sells. The little shops are so small that the men sometimes do their work in the narrow streets, thus making them seem even narrower. 

Formerly all the shops of La Paz were like these small, dark, and dirty. Now, in the more modern parts of the city, there are large well-lighted stores and attractive displays of goods. 

Many of the people, especially the Indians, do not trade much in the stores, but buy what they need at

the market. Sunday is the best time for us to visit the market, for the crowds there are greater on that day than on any other and the quantities of goods displayed are larger. Not only in the market buildings but in the FIG. 114. Fruits and vegetables are among the many products offered for sale in the La Paz market. What are some of the other products that may be seen here (see page 280)? (Courtesy of Grace Line) streets and squares around there are many buyers and sellers. The narrow alleys are crowded with loaded llamas and donkeys, Indian women with bundles of produce or babies on their backs, and Indian men chewing coca leaves.

The sides of the streets are lined with piles of goods, with the owners squatting on the ground behind them. 

There are no scales for weighing, for people buy by the

SOUTH AMERICA 

pile instead of by the pound. On one side of the street are mounds of wheat, barley, quinoa, and attractivelooking fruit. On the other side there are piles of parched corn, dried peas, beans, onions, and other vegetables, and close beside them some small, hard, white balls. These are potatoes which have been frozen and dried, and are one of the most common articles of food of the Bolivians. Such potatoes keep, sometimes for years, until they are needed in the stew which is eaten almost daily by the Indians. In some stalls the 

Indian women have displayed some bright blankets, gay striped ponchos, and the heavy woolen cloth of which their skirts are made. There are stalls also where one can buy native jewelry-chains, earrings, bracelets, and the long pins with which the women fasten their shawls.

We must not think of La Paz as a city of Indians, llamas, and open-air markets only. Some of its streets are wide and paved, and it has fine stone buildings, beautiful plazas, electric lights, and street cars. Riding down El Prado, one of the principal streets, we can hardly believe that we are in a valley some twelve thousand feet high and far away from any great center of civilization. The street begins in a beautiful plaza just where a statue of General Antonio de Sucre, the first president of Bolivia, stands. At the other end of the street is a fine statue of General Bolivar, for whom the republic of Bolivia is named, pointing to the whitecapped mountain peaks beyond.
