CHAPTER XXI IN EASTERN BOLIVIA 

The chief products of Bolivia have always been its minerals. As you know, these come from its high plateaus and mountains. For this reason most of the people live in the high lands of western and central Bolivia and most of the cities are located in that region. 

The part of Bolivia which lies east of the snowcapped Andes is a part of the hot, rainy Amazon Lowland and so is very different from the cool plateaus and mountain regions. It belongs to the region known as the montaña, which extends also into eastern Peru and Ecuador. The montaña has been less developed than the high plateaus, and much of it has never been explored. It has no railroads as yet, and goods have to be carried long distances on mule-back before they reach some stream on which they can be shipped down to the Amazon and Plata rivers or up over the steep mountain trails to La Paz. Can you find on the regional map any rivers of this region which you think can be used to reach the Amazon?

The montaña has fertile soil and is well forested. 

The most important of the forest products is rubber. 

Indeed, the rubber exports of Bolivia rank next to its mineral exports in value. There are also many cinchona trees, from which at one time South America supplied the world with quinine. Realizing the value 283

SOUTH AMERICA 

of the product, England, years ago, set about cultivating the cinchona trees in its possessions in and around Asia. Following its lead, other nations planted large numbers of the trees in several of the islands of the East Indies, until today the larger part of the FIG. 115. The people living on the eastern slopes of the Andes depend on pack animals for many of their supplies. These mules, loaded with barley, are fording a stream on their way to a Bolivian mining town world's supply of quinine comes from the Far East, most of it from the island of Java. The cinchona industry of Bolivia is carried on at present in a very simple way. Long, narrow strips of bark are peeled off from the trunk, and the wound is left to heal over. 

Then the bark is dried and taken through the tropical forests to some branch of the Amazon or up over the steep mountain trails to La Paz, and thence is shipped to Europe and the United States. Besides the cinchona

IN EASTERN BOLIVIA 

285 trees that grow wild in eastern Bolivia, many have been set out on plantations there. 

There are thousands of acres of good pasture lands for cattle in eastern Bolivia, and many coffee and cacao trees grow there. Coca plants, about which you read on page 272, are raised in great numbers. In the picture on page 286 the Indian laborers are picking coca leaves on a large cocal. Many of the cocales, however, are only small plots of cleared land in the tropical forest. How different this part of Bolivia is from the high plateaus where minerals are the chief product! 

When the products of the lowland region are further developed and railroads are built here so that the products can be easily carried to the seaports, the exports from this tropical lowland will be very valuable. 

Most of the people here are Indians. They live in huts, eat the oranges, bananas, and other tropical fruits which grow here, and fish and hunt for other food. Some of the tribes use blowguns and poisoned arrows to kill their prey. In some places the natives have small patches of manioc, corn, and other crops. near their huts. They cultivate the ground with wooden plows and very short-handled hoes and spades. 

Sheep, goats, and cattle feed in some of the pastures.

The Indian women do the family washing in wayside streams, weave the cloth for their scanty clothing, and do most of the work in the gardens. 

Caravans of mules and llamas carry some of the products of this part of Bolivia to the high plateaus and bring back the articles which the people who live here cannot get or make for themselves. The Indian drivers rise long before daylight, prepare their simple

FIG. 116. This cocal in eastern Bolivia extends up a mountain side for over a mile.

(Courtesy of Grace Line)

IN EASTERN BOLIVIA 

287 breakfast, saddle and load their animals, and start out on their long march. At night they stop, turn the beasts out to forage for themselves, eat their supper, and, wrapping themselves in their ponchos, sleep on the ground under the stars. The next day the same FIG. 117. After the coca leaves have been gathered they are spread out to dry in the hot sunshine. Why do the Indians chew coca leaves (see page 272)? routine is repeated, and the next, and so on for weeks. 

The driver is never in a hurry. Why should he be?

The days are all alike to him, and it makes no difference whether the trip takes two days or twenty. Often he has no home, and if he has, it may not be any more comfortable than his sleeping-place on the ground. 

You will notice on the map that the Gran Chaco, of which you read in Chapter XII, extends into Bolivia. 

The Bolivian part of this region is much like the Para-

SOUTH AMERICA 

guayan and Argentine Chaco. Some sections are too dry to be of much use, but there are large areas covered with grass which will make good pastures for cattle and fertile land for farms. Nearer the Paraguay River there are deep forests, and here, among other varieties, many quebracho trees grow. Can you think of any reasons why less tanning material is made from the trees which grow here than from those in the Paraguayan Chaco? 

Do you remember the railroad of which you read on page 259, which runs from Uyuni in Bolivia down through Argentina to Buenos Aires? Now that you have learned how hard it is to get products from eastern Bolivia to the coast for export, you can realize better how useful this railroad is. It is a long way from parts of eastern Bolivia, however, and other railroads have been planned to open up these distant areas. A railroad from Santos, Brazil, now runs westward as far as the boundary of Bolivia. The Bolivians are planning to continue this road through Santa Cruz and up to the plateau to connect with railroads there. 

Santa Cruz is the most important city in eastern Bolivia, and the region around it is fertile and capable of producing large crops. Near the city also are petroleum deposits which are being developed by a company from the United States. 

Besides the railroad line from Santos, another

Brazilian railroad has been started from Rio de Janeiro toward Bolivia. When the connections in that country are completed, eastern Bolivia may be expected to develop more rapidly.

CHAPTER XXII SOUTHERN PERU 

Our next visit will be in Peru, another mountain land. You will get many thrills on this journey, for you will sail into the country on the highest lake in the world on which boats make regular trips. You will ride on the highest railroad in the world; you will visit the highest and richest mines in the world; and you will see famous ruins of cities where people lived and worked long before the Spaniards or any other people in Europe had even heard that there were great continents across the Atlantic Ocean. 

As we travel by train from La Paz toward the Peruvian border, great mountains rise on either side, and ahead of us stretches the plateau on which we have been traveling during most of our trip through Bolivia. 

After we cross the boundary of Peru we shall find that the mountains and high plateau continue through that country. Near the little town of Guaqui we see 

Indians harvesting hay. Notice them in the picture on page 292.

At Guaqui our train connects with the steamer which will take us across Lake Titicaca to the Peruvian shore. 

The steamers which ply on the lake were not made here. 

They were brought in parts to the port of Arica, sent by rail up to the plateau, and put together near the lake. Why was this necessary? The oldest steamer on 291

SOUTH AMERICA 

the lake, the Yarvari, is shown in Fig. 119. It was brought up from the coast in sections on the backs of mules and Indians. 

Fig. 120 shows you one of the boats which the natives use on the lake. It is called a balsa. No trees FIG. 118. This is a harvesting scene at Guaqui, near the shore of Lake 

Titicaca. Notice the shape of the haystacks which the native workmen are building. (Courtesy of Grace Line) grow here on the high plateau, and so for centuries the Indians have made their boats of reeds which grow on the shores of the lake. The reeds are taller than you are and not much larger round than your thumb. The men tie them into large bundles with strings of grass and sew the bundles together in the shape of a boat. 

The balsas look frail, but some of them are large enough to carry passengers, animals, and freight. 

The little blue spot that represents Lake Titicaca on the map is so small that it is hard to realize how

FIG. 119. The boat being unloaded is the first steamer to be used on Lake 

Titicaca. It was launched in 1861. How old is it now? (Courtesy of 

Grace Line) FIG. 120. Some of the balsas used by the Indians on Lake Titicaca have sails made of reeds. Those used near the shore have no need of sails and are propelled only by poles or paddles. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

SOUTH AMERICA 

large the lake really is. This body of water, over twelve thousand feet above sea level, is nearly three times as large as our state of Rhode Island, and we shall sail for a hundred miles before we reach its northern end in Peru. It was once much larger than it is today and stretched through Bolivia nearly to the border of Argentina. During our Bolivian trip we traveled much of the time over its ancient bed of sand and gravel, and a lake that we passed on our way to La Paz is another remnant, not yet evaporated, of this ancient body of water.

The color of the water in Lake Titicaca is a cold, steely blue, not at all like the soft, deep blue of lakes on which the rays of the tropical sun beat down. The lake is fed by mountain streams. If you put your hand into the water, you will find it icy-cold. Ice forms around the shores nearly every night in the year, although the body of the lake never freezes. 

Lake Titicaca has no known outlet to the ocean.

Yet, in the ocean waters along the shores of northern Chile, there are found at times reeds, grains, and bits of plants that grow only around this high mountain lake; and it is thought that these materials find their way to the Pacific through underground streams. 

There are several islands in Lake Titicaca, some of which are occupied by Indians. Two of the islands are held in great reverence by the natives. They are called the Island of the Sun and the Island of the Moon. 

An Indian legend relates that from the Island of the Sun came the first Inca, or ruler, and that from the Island of the Moon came his wife. From this couple were descended a long line of Incas. When the first

SOUTHERN PERU 

295 Spaniards came to South America, the Incas ruled over the largest Indian empire in the world. It stretched through the Andean region, from Ecuador, in the north, down through Peru and Bolivia and more than halfway along the narrow strip of Chile. 

According to the old Indian legend, the Creator had given the first Inca who came from the Island of the Sun a rod of gold which he was to drive into the earth wherever he stopped in his travels. When he reached a place where the rod went into the ground without pressure, there he was to build a city which should be the capital of his empire. This happened at Cusco, Peru, a quaint old city which we shall visit later. Here the capital was built, and here began the reign of the Incas.

Millions of Indians lived in this old Inca empire. 

These were hard-working farmers, shepherds, and mechanics. They built terraced gardens on the slopes of the mountains, dug canals, and irrigated millions of acres of land; they tended large flocks of llamas and alpacas and hunted the timid vicuñas in the mountains. The women spun and wove the wool of these animals into cloth, which they dyed in brilliant colors with the juice of plants. The men made beautiful ornaments and decorations for their temples with the gold which they washed from the sands of the streams on the eastern slopes of the Andes. They used copper and silver also, which they dug from the rich veins on the mountain slopes. Fig. 121 shows some old Inca ruins.

The Incas had roads built to connect different parts of their empire and established rest houses for travel-

SOUTH AMERICA 

FIG. 121. On the walls of the narrow "Street of the Snakes," in Cusco, people have built their houses on the foundations of old Inca ruins. The foundations are still in perfect condition. (Courtesy of Grace Line) ers. They had a regular system of runners, so that news was carried great distances very quickly. Runners, who traveled in relays, took the fruits gathered from the low, tropical regions near the ocean, and even the fish caught in its waters, to the rulers who lived on the high inland plateau.

Most of the roads and many of the irrigation systems and canals built by the Incas have been destroyed. 

The Indians of today use but a small part of the area which was cultivated under the Inca rule, and many of the mountain farms are now brown and bare. The 

Indians carry on their farming in just the same way

SOUTHERN PERU 

297 that their ancestors did hundreds of years ago. They stir the ground with a little wooden plow which turns up the soil only a few inches, and they thresh their barley by driving their oxen over the grain again and again.

Many conflicts took place between the Indians of Peru and Bolivia and the Spaniards led by the conqueror Pizarro. The Spaniards were greedy to obtain the gold with which the temples of the Incas were decorated and which the Indians could also obtain for them from rich deposits not far away. In some places the rulers were killed, the temples were robbed of their golden treasures, and the Indians became the slaves of Spaniards. In many cases, rather than see their sacred vessels and ornaments fall into the hands of the Spanish, the Indians buried some in the ground and some in lakes and caves, so that in parts of Peru today one hears stories of riches which are hidden in the earth or water.

Perhaps the most treacherous of all the acts of the Spaniards was the killing of Atahualpa, an Inca emperor. Atahualpa went to visit Pizarro, who imprisoned him in a room some fourteen or fifteen feet square. 

In return for his freedom Atahualpa promised Pizarro to fill the room with gold as high as he could reach. 

Pizarro promised to free him, and the order went forth to all parts of the empire to bring in the gold. Statues, vases, plates, chains, and other articles of solid gold were brought on the backs of Indians and llamas until the room was filled higher than the line agreed upon. 

Pizarro, however, broke his promise to the Inca ruler, kept him a prisoner, and later put him to death.

SOUTH AMERICA 

At the Peruvian end of Lake Titicaca we leave our steamer and continue our trip by train through the land of the Incas to its ancient capital, Cusco. Near the end of the trip our train runs through the valley of a little river, and from the car windows we see large herds of sheep, llamas, and alpacas feeding on the coarse grass. The land around is dotted with the huts of the Indians. Wheat, barley, patches of quinoa, and potatoes and other vegetables are growing in the fields, and the steep sides of the valley are lined with little gardens terraced up with stone walls to prevent the soil from washing down the slopes. Many of the farms here are irrigated, and some of the fields are surrounded with mud walls to hold in the water. As in many parts of South America, the land here is divided into large estates owned by wealthy white men or half-breeds, for whom the Indians work. 

Cusco, once the capital of a powerful empire, is now only a small city, and it is more famous for what it was than for what it is. It is interesting chiefly because of its ruins of the old Inca civilization. Chief among these are the ruins of the old Temple of the Sun. History tells us that its roof was covered with golden plates which gleamed and glittered in the sunlight. The walls and ceilings inside were covered with sheets of gold. On the eastern wall an immense oval golden plate, sixty feet in diameter, represented the Sun, the god whom these ancient people worshiped. In this part of the temple were golden thrones and shrines, and the vessels used in worship and all the images and decorations were of gold. In the garden of the temple were golden statues of men, birds, animals, and reptiles.

SOUTHERN PERU 

299 The Spaniards took millions of dollars' worth of the precious metal from this one temple, besides much from other places, and the Indians carried away and hid millions of dollars' worth more. There were tem- FIG. 122. This old church in Cusco dates back to 1570. Were there any churches of this size in the United States at that time? (Courtesy of 

Grace Line) ples sacred to the sun in several cities of Peru, and many other temples and palaces in the capital city. 

On a hill behind Cusco are ruins of other temples and of palaces and forts which are more remarkable than anything in the city itself. The walls of an old fortress here are made of enormous blocks of stone, some of them from ten to fifteen feet high and nearly as wide. Notice the stones in Fig. 3 on page 6. How the Incas, with no knowledge of machinery, man-

SOUTH AMERICA 

aged to draw the heavy blocks from the quarry several miles away, lift them to the top of this hill a thousand feet above the plain, and fit them together is a mystery which has never been solved.

The walls of other ancient buildings are made of blocks of stone, each of which weighs several tons. 

These were brought from long distances and were fitted together without mortar or cement. Many houses of Cusco present a peculiar appearance because their lower stories are formed by the thick walls of ruined buildings, as you saw in Fig. 121. One of the largest churches in the city is built on the walls of an old temple. Most of the houses are low, one-story buildings, usually painted in bright colors and with red-tiled roofs. Many of them are built around a patio like the houses in other South American countries of which you have read, and the walls are set close to the narrow sidewalks, which, in turn, border narrow streets. 

Today most of the interior towns of Peru have no connection with one another. Not only are there no railroads in these regions, but there are also no roads, and goods must be carried over rough, stony trails on the backs of llamas and mules. In a trip far away from the railroads and in some places near them we shall meet pack mules and llamas, each with its load of ore or provisions. They climb rough, stony paths on narrow trails where in places a single false step might hurl them to death on the sharp stones hundreds of feet below. Behind the animals trudges the Indian driver wrapped in his poncho. Often he is accompanied by his wife, who, as she walks, twirls the spools on which she is spinning some sheep or llama wool. Both man

SOUTHERN PERU 

301 and woman are chewing coca leaves, for without them they might not be able to endure the hard climb, the cold, and the scanty food. 

In going from Cusco down to the coast we ride on the Southern Railway of Peru, one of the two chief FIG. 123. In and around Cusco most goods are carried on pack animals. (Courtesy of Grace Line) railroads of the country. It is the main route from the ocean into southern Peru and thence onward, by way of Lake Titicaca, into northern Bolivia. The route takes us through the pleasant river valley of Cusco and out into the wide, high plain, where we are two miles above the level of the ocean. But we shall gradually wind half a mile higher before we reach the bleak, wind-swept mountain pass that separates the high central plateau of 

Peru from the Pacific slopes.

Near this high pass we see large flocks of alpacas feeding. There are few homes in any country of the

SOUTH AMERICA 

world higher than the little huts here in which the Indian shepherds live. Farther down the slopes llamas and herds of cattle are feeding on the tough, wiry grass. Here and there, where a little stream furnishes water, are Indian villages with patches of grain and FIG. 124. These Indian women are sorting potatoes. They will carry the larger ones to the market in Arequipa. They will keep the smaller ones, letting them freeze and later thawing them to use in stews 

vegetables. In many cases the huts are no better than hencoops, not so good, in fact, as many hencoops which may be seen on prosperous farms in the United States. The door is a hole cut in the side, through which one must stoop to enter. There are no windows and no furniture. At night the people huddle together on the stone floor or lean against the hard wall, with their ponchos wrapped around their heads and shoulders and their feet bare to the frosty air. In one

SOUTHERN PERU 

303 corner is a little fire of llama manure, the only fuel which can be found on the high plateaus. On the hot stones surrounding the coals is a dish in which is cooking a stew made of goat's meat and frozen potatoes.

These people desire nothing better because they know nothing better. For a few cents a day they work on the hard trails, in the mines, or on the farms, asking for little except plenty of coca leaves. 

The railroad from Cusco down to the coast follows the route used by the relay runners of the Incas. The only city of importance on the route is Arequipa. 

Before the Spaniards came, a rest station for the runners and other travelers occupied the site where that city now stands. Pizarro saw that a little river furnished plenty of water and that the route leading up to the plateau was easier than any other. So he made a settlement here which grew rapidly. Today Arequipa is the chief city of southern Peru and one of the most important cities in the country. 

Look at the picture on page 305, and you will agree that no city in South America, or perhaps in the world, has a lovelier location than Arequipa. It is higher than the top of Mount Washington, in New Hampshire, and the air is clear and pleasant. The sun is hot in the middle of the day, but the nights are cool and refreshing. The water of the little river which flows through the city makes possible the cultivated fields and gardens. Running swiftly down from the mountain slopes, it also furnishes power for making electricity. This is used to run cars and light the city. It is used also in some of the mills and factories here. 

Great mountains three and four miles high, wearing

SOUTH AMERICA 

their white caps most of the year, look down on the city. They are volcanic peaks, but there has been no eruption here since the coming of the Spaniards. Mount 

Misti, most beautiful of them all, is frequently seen with smoke coming from its crater, showing that the interior fires are not dead. On the seal of the city of 

Arequipa there is a picture of Mount Misti with smoke rising from it. Arequipa was once almost entirely destroyed by an earthquake, and thousands of people were killed.

All along the western coast of South America we are in earthquake land, and care is taken to make the houses "earthquake proof." Here at Arequipa most of the buildings are only one story high, and the walls, which are of stone, are very thick and strong. These are plastered over and painted in gay colors, and passing along the street one does not realize how thick they are.

There is, of course, a large plaza in the center of the city, with the old cathedral on one side of it. On other sides are fine public buildings. The city has factories for making cloth from the wool of the sheep, llamas, and alpacas which feed by thousands in the high pastures. There are car shops where the employees of the Southern Railway of Peru make and repair cars. 

There are cotton mills, a flour mill, a chocolate factory, and other industries which tell us that the people are gradually developing their opportunities for manufacturing and commerce. 

Leaving Arequipa, we zigzag downward toward

Mollendo on the Pacific coast. This part of our ride takes us through one of the dreariest deserts to be 1

FIG. 125. The people of Arequipa have a beautiful view of Mount Misti. What were Pizarro's reasons for founding a city here (see page 303)? (Courtesy of Grace Line)

SOUTH AMERICA 

found anywhere on earth. It is a continuation of the great desert strip which we found along the coast of Chile and which stretches northward through Peru almost to the boundary of Ecuador. We are in the torrid zone here, where the trade winds from the Atlantic Ocean blow. They have lost their moisture on the eastern side of the mountains and have none to give to the land on the Pacific slopes. The cold Humboldt Current, which sweeps up the western coast of 

South America, chills the air and causes clouds and fogs to hang over the shore and lower slopes of the Andes for weeks at a time. These clouds and fogs increase the gloomy appearance of the desert. 

The land here is warmer than the ocean, and the clouds are condensed into rain or snow only near the summits of the mountains. This is the reason why, on the high slopes and the high plateau between the ranges, some grass grows and vegetables and grain can be raised. Near the coast not a blade of grass or a green shrub can be seen, except in the valley of some mountain stream. Wherever we find a town or a city on the coast of Peru we are sure to find also a river rushing down to the sea.

As we descend nearer the coast the country becomes more and more barren. Dark, furrowed mountains, black volcanic peaks, and stretches of sand, pebbles, and jagged rocks are all that we can see. Near the railroad there are some curious-looking hills of sand, shaped exactly like crescent moons. Fig. 126 shows you how these hills look. The conductor tells us that the strong, steady wind which blows here sweeps them slowly along. Sometimes they block the railroad track,

SOUTHERN PERU 

307 and in places large stones have been piled up to break the force of the wind and so prevent the sand from interfering with the passage of the trains. 

Mollendo is the Pacific port at the end of the Southern Railway of Peru. It lies close to the shore, hemmed in by bare brown hills. There is little that is attractive FIG. 126. The sand dunes of the desert region of western Peru are always in the shape of crescents. The sand is blown slowly up the outer side and then drifts down into the hollow side. The dunes range from 30 to 100 feet in length and from 6 to 15 feet in height in the town. Even the drinking water has to be brought in pipes from a river miles away. Gray clouds, fogs, and mist make the place appear even more gloomy than it otherwise would be. 

Although it is not beautiful, Mollendo is important, for it handles the great amount of freight carried on the railroad up to Peru and Bolivia. The harbor of 

Mollendo is one of the roughest on the Pacific coast, for it has no shelter from the rough waters of the ocean. 

Ships have to anchor some distance out from the shore,

FIG. 127. Mollendo is not a large city, but it is an important port, for it serves as an ocean gateway for Arequipa and for Bolivia. Why are the ships anchored so far out in the harbor? (Courtesy of Grace Line)

SOUTHERN PERU 

309 and passengers and freight are brought to land in boats called lighters. Notice the ships in the harbor in Fig. 127. 

Because of the rough water, the lighters are larger and heavier than those used in other west-coast ports. 

When the surf is very high, no goods can be loaded on the ships or unloaded from them. Because of the fogs of this region, there are days when the coast cannot be seen, even though one may be sailing dangerously near it. 

Oftentimes when nothing can be seen through the fog, the snow-capped mountains stand out clear above it.

CHAPTER XXIII DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND TROPICAL LOWLANDS 

Peru may be divided into three sections, each of which is very different from the other two. Along the coast is the strip of desert, the southern part of which was described in Chapter XXII. Back of the coastal lands is the high central plateau with its mighty mountain ranges. The eastern part of the country, where the mountains slope down to tropical lowlands, is a part of the montaña, which, as you know, extends also into Bolivia and Ecuador.

The two most important occupations in Peru are mining and farming. Most of the minerals come from the plateau region, and most of the farm crops are raised on the long desert strip of coast. As you read further you will find out how it is possible for farm crops to be grown in a desert.

The coastal region of Peru is some fourteen hundred miles long and from fifty to a hundred miles wide. 

Across it fifty or more short rivers flow from their sources in the mountains to the sea. They widen somewhat on the more level parts of the land near the coast; but in their courses down the steep slopes their waters are swift and their valleys narrow like that of the Rimac River, a picture of which is shown on page 312. 

During the summer months, when the rainfall is heaviest on the western slopes of the Andes, the streams are 311

SOUTH AMERICA 

full; but during the dry season there is so little water in the rivers that some of them cannot reach the sea. 

The valleys watered by the little rivers are the only parts of this coastal portion of Peru that are green. 

All the other parts are dreary places, bare and brown. FIG. 128. This picture, which shows part of the valley of the Rimac River near Lima, was taken during the dry season when the stream was not much larger than a brook. How can you tell how wide the river becomes during the rainy season? (Photograph by J. C. Sawders from R. I. Nesmith and Associates)

The level land and the soil of the river valleys are excellent for farming, and water for irrigation is supplied by the streams. On these irrigated lands sugar cane, cotton, rice, coffee, tobacco, wheat, and other crops are raised. You will not be surprised to learn that about a third of all the people of Peru live in this coastal region.

DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND LOWLANDS 313 

The map opposite page 256 shows you that Peru has no railroad line extending along the coast. The river valleys are separated from one another by rough desert lands and, for the most part, are connected only by sandy roads over which travel is difficult. So most of the com- FIG. 129. In most of the harbors along the coast of Peru ships must anchor far out from shore. The lighters moored beside the large ship in the picture are loaded with vanadium ore that will be carried to the United States. (Courtesy of W. Spencer Hutchinson) munication between the valleys must be carried on by water between the ports scattered along the coast. 

The map also shows you that Peru has a regular coast line, with few indentations. Therefore we shall not expect to find many good harbors. There are thirty or more ports of varying size along the coast, however, each of them at or near the mouth of a river. At some of the ports a point of land juts out

SOUTH AMERICA 

far enough to provide a little protection for ships, and at a few of the ports breakwaters and sea walls have been built. But usually, as at Mollendo, ships have to anchor some distance out from the shore, and the freight and passengers have to be carried back and forth in lighters.

The smaller ports all look much alike. They consist of sandy streets, houses of sun-baked mud, and a store or two where provisions may be bought. There may be also a long pier, a lighthouse on a point of land, and, in a few cases, a railroad line leading back into the irrigated valleys from which come the crops for export. In other places goods are brought from and carried to regions farther inland by mules. 

The cotton and sugar cane grown on the irrigated lands of the river valleys are the two leading crops of Peru, and both are among the most valuable exports of the country. The Peruvian cotton plants are taller than those grown in our Southern states, and the fiber is longer and of a different texture. The greater part of the crop is exported to Great Britain and the United States, but some of it is used for manufacturing in the seaports nearest the cotton-growing areas and in Arequipa, where, you remember, there are cotton mills. 

Most of the sugar cane is grown on estates in the northern valleys of the coastal strip. Only a small part of the crop is used by the people, and the rest is exported, chiefly to Chile and Great Britain. 

Paita, a small town in northern Peru, is an important port for the shipping of cotton. A railroad extending some sixty miles inland from the port has greatly helped in its growth, for much freight from the irri-

DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND LOWLANDS 315 

gated regions is sent to the coast by rail. Paita is noted for other exports besides cotton. Sugar, tobacco, hides, and Panama hats, or, perhaps more properly, Peruvian hats, are all shipped away from this little port. FIG. 130. Petroleum is pumped to this large refinery in one of the seaports in northern Peru from a field about fifteen miles away. What is done in a refinery? (Courtesy of Grace Line) 

Northern Peru has rich fields of petroleum. There are big refineries here, and both crude and refined oil are exported from Paita as well as from other ports. 

The central Andean region of Peru is a high plateau averaging more than two miles above sea level and ranging from fifty to two or three hundred miles in width, with ranges of high mountains rising from it on the eastern and western borders and other lower ranges crossing it. It is a great treeless waste with some

SOUTH AMERICA 

swamps and lakes. It has a few fertile river valleys, but most of the region is covered with a coarse grass which furnishes food for llamas, alpacas, and sheep. 

Many of these animals, tended by Indian shepherds, feed on the mountain slopes hundreds of feet higher than the plateau.

There is no mountain pass less than fourteen hundred feet high by which one can get from the low coast lands up onto the plateau. It was in this central plateau, or table-land, that the Inca Empire developed, and it is here that many people live today. Only two railroads in Peru lead from the coast up to the high plateau. We have already traveled on one of them from Cusco down to Mollendo. What is its name? The other and more important line is the Central Railway of Peru. 

It runs from Callao, on the coast, through the capital city, Lima, up to the plateau. Find it on the map opposite page 256. All the other railroads of Peru are short lines leading inland from the little seaports for a few miles. There are a few good roads, suitable for automobiles, extending out a few miles from the larger cities, but everywhere else in the country one must travel on the backs of mules.

Before returning to the plateau region, we shall stop at Callao, Peru's largest and most important seaport.

All the commerce of Lima and of central Peru passes through this port. It has one of the best harbors on the 

Pacific coast, and the largest ocean steamers can come directly to its wharves to land passengers and freight. 

Many improvements in the harbor and on the docks are constantly being made to keep pace with the growth of the country's industries and commerce.

BANCO ITALIANO 

FIG. 131. Here you see a part of the water front of Callao and some of the business houses. This is one of the busiest of the west-coast ports. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

SOUTH AMERICA 

You remember that for hundreds of miles north and south of Callao the coast of Peru is rainless and no trees grow there. Lumber is therefore in great demand. 

Many of the sailing vessels which come to Callao bring loads of lumber from our states of Washington and Oregon and from British Columbia. On the homeward journey the ships carry minerals and other raw materials for manufacturing.

On the wharves and in the warehouses at Callao are hundreds of bales of cotton and wool, bags of sugar, piles of hides, packages of coca leaves, and bars of copper, silver, and other minerals. The quantities of these and other products, the number of vessels in the harbor, and the work going on in warehouses and on the docks and wharves all tell us what an important port Callao is, how rich must be the region which lies behind it, and how important are the industries carried on there. To see something of this part of Peru we will make another visit on the high plateau. 

We can go from Callao to Lima, eight miles away, by train or electric car, or by automobile over a fine boulevard. Along the way are many vineyards, fields of sugar cane, and acres of tall, waving corn. Lima, the capital city, lies in the valley of a mountain river, with high mountain peaks always in sight when the weather is fine. From November till June it is usually clear and pleasant. During the rest of the year the sky is cloudy much of the time, fogs and mist prevail, but rain seldom falls.

Lima is an old city, for it was founded by Pizarro in 1535. He called it the City of Kings. On one side of the central plaza is the cathedral shown in Fig. 132.

DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND LOWLANDS 319 

Pizarro is buried in this old church, of which he himself laid the corner stone. 

The Rimac River, which rushes through Lima on its way down from the mountains, furnishes water for the FIG. 132. This cathedral at Lima is the oldest and largest one in the 

Americas. Built by Pizarro in 1535, it was later partially destroyed by an earthquake and was rebuilt in 1620. (Courtesy of Grace Line) people of the city and for the neighboring fields of grain and vegetables, vineyards, and orchards. The electricity which lights the city and which supplies power for the cars that run to Callao and to several seaside resorts has been made possible by the power in this river. 

Lima is a modern city in many ways. Many of its streets have been paved, and good highways lead from the city to towns and resorts a few miles away.

SOUTH AMERICA 

As in other South American cities, however, most of the streets in Lima are narrow, with the houses close to the sidewalks. On the two-story houses iron balconies similar to those in Fig. 133 often overhang the FIG. 133. Notice the contrast between the old and the new on this business street in Lima. In the foreground, on both sides, are the older buildings, with their overhanging Spanish balconies, and in the background are modern business buildings. (Photograph by Lionel Green) sidewalks. They enable the women to see, without being seen, what is going on in the street. The roofs of the houses are flat, and many of those in the poorer parts of the city are made of light poles covered with earth. A few heavy rains would spoil most of them; but heavy rains seldom come, and the people prefer to have their buildings earthquake-proof rather than

DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND LOWLANDS 321 

rain-proof. There are many large, beautiful houses in 

Lima, but there are also poorer crowded districts, such as are common in most large cities. 

In the afternoon the shopping streets are crowded. 

Everyone seems to be out for a good time instead of for business. No one seems in a hurry. The women linger to laugh and chat, and the men saunter along in a leisurely way. It all seems very different from the hurrying crowds and the bustling people who throng the sidewalks of our large cities.

Leaving Lima, we continue our climb toward the clouds on the Central Railway of Peru, which is said to be the most wonderful railroad, as well as the highest, to be found anywhere in the world. It was built under the supervision of a man from the United States after several engineers had said that such a road was impossible. On page 3 is a picture of the highest station. on this road. Before we have gone a hundred miles from the Pacific Ocean, we have climbed three miles toward the clouds or perhaps have risen above them. The road clings to the sides of the hills, creeps along the edges of cliffs hundreds of feet high, winds through dark tunnels, and goes over bridges where, far below us, we can see dashing torrents rushing down to the ocean. We are scarcely well out of a tunnel or across a high bridge before we plunge into or onto another. 

Before we reach Oroya, at the end of the main line, we shall have gone through more than sixty tunnels and crossed more than sixty bridges. In building the road there were places where the workmen had to be let down over the cliffs by ropes from above, and had to cut ledges in the rock on which to stand while they worked.

SOUTH AMERICA 

For most of the way we follow the Rimac River, a picture of which you saw on page 312. It rushes down from the glaciers on the mountains, falling in sheets over tall cliffs, foaming through deep gorges, on the way losing some of its waters to irrigate fields of cotton, to fill ditches between rows of sugar cane, and to make green terraced fields of alfalfa. Terraced farms extend high up on the mountain sides, yet far above them are long-abandoned, wall-inclosed fields which were cultivated by the Incas in those early days when they ruled all this Andean land. 

Nowhere on the lower slopes, except in the irrigated patches, is there any vegetation, and it is not until we are at a height of nearly two miles, where it is so cold that the air is chilled and thus made to drop the moisture which it contains, that the bare, brown desert changes to green. Here and there we pass Indian huts clinging to the rocks. On a steep, rocky trail are llamas and their Indian drivers. Still higher up on the slopes 

Indian women are spinning as they watch their flocks. 

You saw pictures of similar scenes in Chapter XX. 

In the little terraced fields some men bend over their work, hoeing what appear to be small patches of potatoes. They must be careful, because a backward step or two might send them rolling down the steep slopes.

Now we have climbed a mile higher and are nearer the sky than we should be if we were on the top of the highest mountain in the United States. When we are nearly sixteen thousand feet above sea level, we go through a tunnel under the lofty mountain pass and, descending a little, find ourselves for the second time on the central plateau of Peru. The vegetation is

DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND LOWLANDS 323 

scanty, but the coarse grass and moss supply food for the flocks of sheep, llamas, and alpacas. Most of the land is divided into large farms cared for by the Indians who belong on the estates. A few half-breeds and 

Indians have small flocks of their own, but by far the larger number work on the big haciendas. The owners of some of these large estates own thousands of animals, which produce quantities of wool every year. 

The main line of the railroad ends at Oroya, but by looking at the map opposite page 256 you will see that branch lines extend for some two hundred miles to the north and south. Both branches open up mining regions. The northern branch, one of the bridges of which you see on page 324, was built by a United States company to their silver and copper mines at Cerro de Pasco. These mines are among the richest and most famous in the world. 

The minerals of Peru are worth more than all the other products of the country combined. It was the desire to obtain these minerals, especially the gold, that made Pizarro and his followers treat the Indians so cruelly. Most of the gold used in decorating the 

Inca temples came from the eastern slopes of the mountains, where the Indians obtained the precious metal by washing it from the sands of the rivers. Today hundreds of Indians are obtaining the gold in the same simple way, but the greater part of the present production is found in connection with the great copper mines at Cerro de Pasco. Millions of dollars' worth of silver have been taken from the mines on the central plateau, and the production of this metal, although much smaller than formerly, is still so great that Peru ranks

SOUTH AMERICA 

fourth among the silver-producing countries of the world. At the present time, however, copper is the most valuable mineral of the plateau region. 

In a visit to the great Cerro de Pasco mines we should find the work of mining carried on by the most

A few miles away are some coal mines that belong to the company, and at Oroya, where the Cerro de Pasco railroad line joins the Central Railway of Peru, a smelting plant has been built in which ores are melted to separate the copper and silver from the worthless rock. company has built near a waterfall here can furnish more electricity than is needed at the present time. 

This company employs several thousand Indians and many white men - managers, overseers, engineers, FIG. 135. The copper-smelting plant of the Cerro de Pasco company at 

Oroya is modern in every respect. The copper ore is brought here from the mines over the railroad line shown in Fig. 134. (Courtesy of Grace Line) doctors, and other skilled mechanics and professional men. It has built better homes and stores for the 

Indians than most of them have ever known before. 

The white employees have good houses, clubs, tennis courts, ball grounds, and other places of amusement such as you might find in most cities of the United States. 

Other large United States companies have invested huge sums of money in the mining industry near Cerro

SOUTH AMERICA 

de Pasco and in other parts of Peru. It is due largely to these great corporations that Peru has become famous for its mineral production. 

Not far from Cerro de Pasco are mines where much of the world's supply of vanadium is obtained. Vana- 

Idium is used in manufacturing steel to make it hard and strong. Manufacturers use vanadium steel in automobiles, airplanes, and fine machine tools. The vanadium mines, like many of the silver and copper mines, are in the hands of people from the United States, and most of the metals mined in Peru are exported to our country.

The part of Peru least known is the montaña region east of the mountains where the land slopes down toward the Amazon Valley. On the higher western lands of this region are rich timberlands. Farther down the slopes are great grassy pastures, and still lower are dense tropical forests which spread eastward for a thousand miles or more along the upper Amazon and its branches. There is a heavy rainfall here, which, combined with the heat, causes as heavy a growth of trees, shrubs, and vines as can be found anywhere in the world. Thousands of rubber and quinine trees and varieties of valuable hardwoods grow in the forests of the montaña. Many coca shrubs grow wild in the forests, and many more are grown on cocales on the terraced slopes of the mountains. On page 286 you saw a picture of one of these plantations. There are also large areas of fertile soil where cacao and sugar cane might be raised. At present the cost of getting products out of this region prevents settlers from developing the land, and few people except uncivilized Indians live here.

DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND LOWLANDS 327 

In this rich region are many navigable rivers, and even the upper waters of the great Amazon itself. In 

Peru the upper Amazon is called the Marañon River. 

Look at the pictures of the Marañon on this page and on page 51. Find the river on the map opposite page 256 FIG. 136. Natives living along the Marañon River, in the montaña region of Peru, use rafts like the one in the picture for carrying forest products to the nearest steamer landing and trace it to the ocean. Find also on the Marañon the city of Iquitos. It is twenty-three hundred miles from the mouth of the Amazon, yet the river is wide and deep enough here for ocean steamers to come up to its wharves. Smaller steamers go even farther into 

Peru on the Marañon and its branches. 

Our last glimpse of Peru as we go northward from Callao to visit our next country is of some interesting islands near the coast. No people live on the islands, and yet they are very thickly settled. As you know, the cold Humboldt Current cools the ocean water

SOUTH AMERICA 

through which it flows. In these cool waters, especially along the coast of Peru, vast numbers of small fish breed and live. Attracted by the fish, which serve them as food, millions of sea birds haunt the coast and use FIG. 137. This great flock of birds on one of the Chincha Islands, off the coast of Peru, is about to leave the island for the daily flight in search of food. For what product are these islands noted? (Courtesy of Grace Line) the islands off the shore as nesting places. Sometimes they fly about in such numbers that they actually darken the sky overhead. Notice the birds in Fig. 137.

The Chincha Islands are the most important of the island groups used as nesting places. Find these islands on the map opposite page 256. 

For centuries such birds have fed on the fish in the waters here, nested on the islands, and lived and died

DESERTS, HIGHLANDS, AND LOWLANDS 329 

there. Their dead bodies and their droppings accumulated to a depth of many feet and, in the dry air, decayed into a gray powder called guano. This is one of the best fertilizers known. For many years large quantities were shipped to foreign countries; but so much has been exported that the old accumulations are gone, and no guano is sold now except to farmers in Peru. The wasteful methods of collecting the guano have given place to better ones, the birds are protected, and on some islands they are left undisturbed for two or three years. The government decides from year to year from what islands the guano shall be taken, and on all except these it is left to accumulate.

CHAPTER XXIV  

We have sailed north along the western coast of South America from the southernmost city in the world almost to the equator. What different sights we have seen! The deep forests of southern Chile; the great desert region in northern Chile where the nitrate industry is carried on; and the bare, brown Peruvian coast with its green-bordered mountain streams flowing down the slopes to the sea.

Now, after a trip along the coast of Peru, we have reached Ecuador, or, as its name tells us, the equator country. It seems as if a miracle had taken place. 

Instead of bare, brown shores and cloudy skies, we see a fairyland of green on which a tropical sun is shining. 

In other ways, too, Ecuador differs from the countries of western South America. Unlike Peru and

Bolivia and Chile, Ecuador has few minerals of importance. It is much smaller than any of those countries. We do not know its exact size, however, for the boundary between Ecuador and Peru has been in dispute ever since early Spanish colonial days. If Ecuador can prove its right to the land it claims, the country will be more than twice the size shown on the maps in this book. If the claims of Peru are accepted, Ecuador will become the smallest country of South America. 

Its area as given at present is about that of Texas. 331

SOUTH AMERICA 

There are points of likeness as well as of difference between Ecuador and the rest of western South America. 

FIG. 138. This pleasant-faced Indian woman is carrying a bundle of firewood on her back. 

Perhaps she will sell it, and also the jars in her hand, at some house in the city. (Courtesy of 

Grace Line)

As in the countries farther south, the white people in Ecuador are chiefly of Spanish descent.

They control much of the business and direct the chief industries of the country. But they are far outnumbered by Indians, most of whom live on the high plateau. The Indians carry on farming or spend their time on the mountain trails, driving their loaded animals long distances as the Indians of Peru and

Bolivia do. The Indians who live in the highlands are much superior to those who live in the warmer lands of eastern Ecuador. The laborers on the coastal lowlands of the country are mostly Negroes. Besides the white people, Indians, and Negroes, there are many mestizos. Do you remember who the mestizos are? If not, turn back to page 10.

A glance at the regional map will show you another way in which Ecuador and the other countries of western South America resemble one another. They each have a share in the Andes Mountains. In Ecuador, as in Peru, there are three distinct regions, each very different from the other two. There is the low strip along the coast; the high plateau between the mountain ranges; and the eastern montaña, where the mountains slope down to the Amazon Valley.

The montaña of Ecuador is even wilder and less known than the montaña in Peru and Bolivia. It is a lonely tropical jungle, hard to get into and harder to get out of, with few people (and those, uncivilized Indians), few settlements, and few industries. The forests resemble those in Brazil, and many rivers flow through them into the Amazon and its branches. In spite of its handicaps, however, the montaña is a region of great future possibilities, for it contains large pastures suitable for cattle-raising, and trees which yield fine lumber and other valuable products. Cinchona and rubber trees grow wild, and some of the Indians prepare the cinchona bark and the biscuits of rubber and carry them to river stations from which they are sent down to the Amazon.

Think of living on the equator and having cool days and colder nights all the year round and snow always in sight as the people in central Ecuador do! The high plateau, with higher mountains on either side, extends north and south through the country for some three hundred miles. Other ranges crossing the plateau divide it into three sections, and still other ranges in the north and south shut Ecuador off from both Peru

SOUTH AMERICA 

and Colombia, so that in the highland region there is no easy passage from one country to another. 

Only one railroad leads from the low coastal lands up over the mountains onto the plateau. Elsewhere people have to climb difficult, dangerous trails like the FIG. 139. Many of the mountain trails in Ecuador are not so wide nor so well worn as this one one shown in Fig. 139. In order to cross the mountains some of these trails zigzag up to mountain passes between twelve thousand and fourteen thousand feet high. From here they wind down to the level of the plateau, some two or three thousand feet lower. 

On the plateau an old road runs north and south through the country. A part of this, rough though it is, is wide enough for oxcarts. A few trails also accommodate such carts. Everywhere else in the region,

except in its few cities, there are only narrow paths, uneven, stony, and often steep. Over these trails Indians drive their burros and mules, each animal carrying a load of from one hundred and fifty to two hundred pounds. The loads are made up chiefly of goods needed by the people on the highlands or of products raised there which are to be sent down to the coastal lands. 

Before any great development can take place in Ecuador, good roads and railroads must be built to connect the different parts of the country. 

For the most part the Indians carry on their farm work just as their ancestors have for centuries. Some of them tend their own little farms and others work on large estates. Except on a few of the estates, they do not plow the ground thoroughly, use fertilizers, or take much care of the growing crops. They raise some wheat, barley, and corn, as well as potatoes, beans, and other vegetables. The potatoes are small, the ears of corn short and not well filled, and the other crops smaller in amount and poorer in quality than those we raise on our farms; yet the plateau region is called the granary of Ecuador, and from it come most of the food crops for the people of the country. 

Many cattle feed in the high pastures and live in the open the year round. Some of the owners of large haciendas are importing blooded stock to improve their grade of cattle, and as a result beef and dairy products are becoming of more importance. 

The coastal plain of Ecuador is much wider than the coastal plain in the countries farther south, and more people live in this region than in any other part of the country. It has a rich soil, a hot climate, and a heavy

SOUTH AMERICA 

rainfall, and here the most important industries are carried on and the most valuable crops are raised. On this plain there are cacao, sugar, and cotton plantations. Oranges, bananas, pineapples, and other tropical fruits also grow here, and large quantities are sent to Peru, Chile, and other South American countries. On the slopes of the mountains which border the plain there are coffee plantations. 

We enter Ecuador through Guayaquil, its only port, which is on a small river about forty miles from the ocean. In the harbor there are large steamers from distant countries, and smaller craft that carry on shipping up and down the coast. There are clumsy-looking boats hewn by hand out of logs, and rafts made of light balsa wood, similar to the material that is used for the boats on Lake Titicaca. Such a raft is shown in Fig. 140. These rafts are the homes, for the time being, of families who live near some stream farther inland. They have brought down to Guayaquil the products of the fertile lowlands, which find a market in the port or which will be exported from it. There are many Negroes in the streets and on the wharves. 

Some are carrying heavy burdens on their backs, while others are driving loaded donkeys and mules or clumsy oxcarts to the market and the warehouses along the water front. Very gradually trucks are taking the place of animals and carts.

Guayaquil is the first large west-coast port of South America south of the Panama Canal, and as the gateway of Ecuador it handles nearly all the commerce of the country. Coastwise and ocean vessels are always at its docks. You see some of these in Fig. 141. Many

products are brought down the rivers to Guayaquil, stored in warehouses there, and loaded on ships in the harbor. Among them are cotton, coffee, rice, and fruit. 

All these are important, but there are three other products for which Ecuador is especially famous and FIG. 140. Balsa rafts like the one in the picture are a common sight on the Guayas River, on which Guayaquil is situated. This raft is the home of the people whom you see on it. (Courtesy of Grace Line) which we buy from it in large quantities. One of these products is cacao beans. For many years Ecuador produced more cacao beans than any other country in the world. Today such immense quantities come from the Gold Coast of West Africa and from Brazil that 

Ecuador no longer holds the highest rank, but cacao beans are still its most important product and in some years its most valuable export.

The cacao trees are grown chiefly on the large plan-

SOUTH AMERICA 

tations of the coastal plain. To visit some of these plantations we may travel either on mule-back or in a boat. It is always summer here, and the year is divided into wet and dry seasons. And how it does pour in the GUAYAS 

RAPID CONDON 

FIG. 141. The river front of Guayaquil is a busy place, as this portion of it shows you. What kind of cargo do you think the small boats in the foreground have brought in to be shipped away? (Courtesy of Grace Line) rainy months! The Guayas River (on which Guayaquil is located) and smaller streams overflow, and water covers much of the land. The little houses in the scattered villages are built on posts so that the floors are above the water and are reached by notched tree trunks which serve as stairs. In the dry season, hens and pigs live under the houses. When the floods come, the animals are taken up into the huts with the family. 

The people go about in boats, and the products of the

cacao and other plantations are taken down to Guayaquil in boats or on rafts.

In Chapter VI you read about the cacao plantations in Brazil and how the beans were prepared for market.

If you have forgotten about this, reread pages 67-73. 

The cacao industry in Ecuador is carried on in much the same way. The beans produced are of excellent quality, and manufacturers in the United States buy large quantities. In the chocolate factories the cacao beans from Ecuador are mixed with beans from other countries to get just the flavor desired. 

Another important product of Ecuador furnishes the material from which some of the buttons on your clothes are made. This is the fruit of the tagua tree, which grows in several countries in Central and South America, but especially in Ecuador. The tagua is a kind of palm usually less than twenty feet tall, with a large trunk and a bunch of long, feathery leaves at the top. The cases, or pods, which hold usually from ten to thirty nuts, are as big as your head and are covered with a sort of bur like a chestnut bur, only much thicker. 

When ripe, the burs burst open and the nuts fall to the ground. They are soft and sweet inside when they are green, and are eaten both by people and by animals. 

When they are ripe and ready to fall to the ground, the soft part inside has changed into a solid substance, hard and white like ivory. For this reason it is often called vegetable ivory.

Several natives usually work together in gathering the nuts. In the part of the forest where there are plenty of tagua trees they build a rough camp raised far enough above the ground to escape the floods.

SOUTH AMERICA 

From fibers which they find in the forest they weave baskets to hold the nuts which they gather. The baskets must be large and strong, for, when filled, they often weigh from one hundred and fifty to two hundred FIG. 142. The workmen are "bagging" tagua nuts that have been brought in from the forests. The bags of nuts will be shipped to the coast and thence to the United States and European countries to be made into buttons pounds. In the dry season the laborers gather the nuts after they have fallen to the ground; in the wet season they go about in boats and get the nuts from the trees. 

Unless the region is flooded so that the nuts can be transported in boats, the heavy baskets must be carried through the trackless forest to the bank of some stream. Here they are loaded on balsa rafts or big dugouts, which are paddled or poled down to Guayaquil. Thousands of tons of these nuts are shipped to

the United States every year, and large quantities go also to European countries. 

The chief use of tagua nuts is in making buttons, and one writer humorously says that the tagua nuts of Ecuador hold up Uncle Sam's pantaloons. The nuts are used also in the manufacture of small articles such as umbrella handles, chessmen, poker chips, and dice.

In our country there are a number of factories which use vegetable ivory. In a button factory the nuts are first dried until they shrink enough to rattle in the shells. 

Next they are passed through cylinders fitted with special contrivances for cracking and removing the shells. 

After this the nuts are sawed into slices of the required thickness, from which the round buttons are turned out. 

All this work requires skilled laborers and fine tools in order that the ivory may be used to the best advantage.

Another important product which comes to us from 

Ecuador is Panama hats. At the time that gold was discovered in California many people traveled to and from the gold fields by way of Panama. There they found for sale hats of fine, soft straw different from any they had ever seen. The travelers who bought them called them Panama hats. Many of these hats are made in Panama and other parts of Central America and in Colombia, but more are made in Ecuador than in any other country.

Panama hats are made from the leaves of the toquilla plant. Indian workers cut the leaves from the plant before they are fully opened. They plunge them once or twice into hot water, shake them thoroughly, dry them in the shade, and then spread them in the sun for further drying and bleaching.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Indian women do most of the weaving. Using their thumb nails, they divide the leaves into the desired width. They are careful to keep the fibers damp, for dry fibers are brittle and break easily. When a strand FIG. 143. These women are expert weavers of toquilla fiber. The fiber of the hat in the foreground is especially fine. Notice the large number of strands that must be woven together to make the brim of the hat. (Courtesy of Grace Line) breaks, the weaver fastens on a new piece so carefully that the place where it is spliced cannot be seen. The length of time that a hat will last depends largely on the skill with which the strands are spliced. 

The Indians are taught when very young to weave hats. The children use such coarse straw that they can make a hat in a day or two. Some of the women, how-

ever, use such fine straw that the weaving of a hat takes several months. Such hats are expensive. Wealthy planters of South America sometimes pay a hundred dollars or more for a hat so fine and soft that it can be 

FIG. 144. At the stations along the railroad between Guayaquil and Quito native women sell fruits and vegetables to passengers on the trains. (Courtesy of Grace Line) folded and carried in a small pocket. We seldom see such fine hats in the United States, but the best ones which we import are well woven and last for years. 

Before leaving Ecuador we must see Quito, its capital.

Quito is a quaint old city, once the capital of an Indian empire older than the great empire of the Incas. Before the coming of the Spaniards the Incas had conquered the older empire and made it a part of their own.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Quito is located almost on the equator. How hot it must be there, you say. No, it is usually cool in Quito, for the city is on the plateau, between nine and ten thousand feet higher than Guayaquil. Towering mountain peaks, some of them always snow-capped, are FIG. 145. Cattle graze in the high pastures on the slopes of Cotopaxi. 

Why are such high pastures better grazing lands than the coastal lowlands of Ecuador? (Courtesy of Grace Line) visible in all directions. Many of these are volcanoes. 

No other country in the world has so many volcanic peaks in so small an area as Ecuador has. Some of the volcanoes are active, and smoke can always be seen ascending from their craters. Two of them are more famous than any others. Chimborazo is perhaps the most beautiful, and Cotopaxi is the most famous, for it is said to be the highest active volcano in the world. It has had no eruption now for many years;

but the grumblings and growlings which can be heard in it, and the thin cloud of smoke which can be seen above it, prove that it is not dead. Its last eruption, followed by a severe earthquake, destroyed towns and villages and killed thousands of people. Heavy earthquake shocks are not common in Ecuador, but gentle ones occur very often.

The earliest recorded eruption of Cotopaxi occurred at the time of the arrival of the Spaniards in South America. Quito was then a large city, and Ecuador was an important part of the great Inca Empire. The 

Indians and their rulers had made all their preparations to fight the Spaniards. Like other ignorant peoples, the Indians were superstitious and saw signs and omens in things which they did not understand. They thought that the eruption of Cotopaxi was a sure sign of bad luck for them in the coming struggle. They therefore gave up all resistance, and the country was easily conquered. 

Before the railroad from Guayaquil to Quito was built, all the freight that entered Quito was carried on mule-back from Guayaquil on a ten or twelve days' journey up the steep trail to the capital city. Today the trip by rail occupies less than two days. 

Quito lies in a beautiful valley, with peaks covered with snow above and the hot, tropical land below. The white buildings with their red roofs gleam among the trees, and beyond the city stretches a green, fertile plain. The low houses with their little balconies and projecting roofs, the narrow streets, and the open markets, with Indian women sitting behind their piles of produce, all make us think that we have been

SOUTH AMERICA 

carried back to the seventeenth century. On the other hand, the electric lights, the cars, and the railroad tracks and station make us realize that the twentieth century has come to Quito as it has to other South FIG. 146. Quito is a picturesque city. The climate is springlike all the year round, and flowers are to be seen everywhere. Most of the buildings are Spanish in style. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

American cities, and that years of growth, of increased trade, of higher education, and of closer relationships with the rest of the world lie before it. Some time in the future Quito will be visited by thousands of American tourists, just as beautiful cities and towns in Switzerland and Norway are today. No views could be lovelier, no mountains in any part of the world grander, and no valleys greener than those around this 

South American capital.
