CHAPTER XXV COLOMBIA: THE LAND AND THE PEOPLE 

Colombia is the last country in South America left for us to visit. It was named for Christopher Columbus, who started a short-lived settlement on its shores in 1502. It is about three times as large as California or as large as the states of Utah, Colorado, Arizona, and New Mexico combined.

Colombia was at one time the richest colony of Spain. 

Gold has been mined there since unknown times.

During the Spanish invasion immense quantities were stored in Cartagena.

From this city and other northern ports of South 

America, Spanish ships laden with treasures of gold, silver, and pearls sailed on long voyages to the mother country. The northern shores of South America used to be called the Spanish Main. Sometimes the ships did not reach Spain with their treasures, but were captured by pirates of other nations, who sank the vessels, killed the crews, and divided the booty among themselves. 

The gold and silver came chiefly from Colombia (then called New Granada) and from Peru and Bolivia. On the coast of Colombia there are old forts and prisons and ruins of fortifications which, if they could speak, could tell us many a thrilling tale of those old, wild days.

Besides gold, Colombia contains deposits of coal, iron, and copper, the three most useful minerals in the 349

SOUTH AMERICA 

world. These were but little prized by the Spaniards, and even today have been made little use of. Colombia is more famous for its emeralds, beautiful green gems more valuable than diamonds. Almost the entire world supply of these gems is mined in Colombia. 

Its platinum deposits are also very valuable, and it ranks third among the countries of the world in the production of this mineral, being surpassed by Canada and Russia. About four fifths of the world's supply of platinum is mined in these three countries. 

More important to Colombia than all these minerals are its fertile, well-watered plains. On its coastal lowlands, in the river plains and high valleys, and on the hillsides and mountain slopes practically every product that will grow in tropical and temperate climates can be raised. Valuable crops are grown on many farms and plantations, but there are still large areas of fertile land of which little or no use is made at present. Colombia also has large areas of deep forests from which many useful products might be obtained, and some of the best pasture lands in the world, which could furnish food for many more cattle than now feed on them. 

As in other parts of South America, many changes have come and are coming to this country, and a new Colombia is growing. We are especially interested in its progress; for the United States is Colombia's best customer, and most of the exports of that country are sent to our seaports.

You know that we can learn many things about places from maps. From the regional map in the front of this book you can find out a great deal about the kind of country that Colombia is and some of the

FIG. 147. In this picture you can see part of the wall built around Cartagena by the Spaniards to protect it. The wall is thirty feet high and several yards thick. (Courtesy of National Federation of Coffee Growers of Colombia)

SOUTH AMERICA 

difficulties which have prevented the development of its rich resources. You can also learn about some of its many advantages.

In looking at the map, notice first that Colombia is the only country in South America which has ports on two large bodies of water. What are the names of these waters? Notice, too, that Colombia lies in the torrid zone and that the equator crosses the southern part. 

How does the map show you that, in spite of its location in a warm part of the earth, Colombia has large areas which are cool or cold? Where are these cooler regions?

Notice how much of Colombia is covered by mountains, and then find the long river which flows through the valley between two of the ranges. Colombia has many rivers, but the Magdalena is longer and more important than any other; in fact, it is more important to the people of Colombia than the Mississippi or any other river in our country is to us. A branch of the Magdalena also flows in a long valley between the mountains. Can you find this branch on the map? 

The soil in these river valleys is very fertile. Here and on the coastal plains are many of the farms and plantations of which you will read later. A farmer's home is shown in the picture on page 353. 

The mountains of Colombia, like those of the countries farther south, rise from a high plateau which is eight thousand feet or more above sea level. Some of the peaks are very high and are snow-covered. Think how different the life and work of the people in the low, hot river plains and coastal lowlands must be from the life and work of the people who live on the high, cool plateau and mountain slopes.

COLOMBIA 

353 About how much of Colombia should you say is included in the plains east of the mountains? All this part of the country is little known and sparsely populated. Looking at the map, you see that the streams in the northern part of these eastern plains flow into FIG. 148. This pleasant farm is in the valley of the Cauca River, a branch of the Magdalena. What does the picture tell you about the fertility of the region? (Courtesy of National Federation of Coffee Growers of Colombia) the Orinoco River. This northeastern region, which is an extension of the llanos of Venezuela, is a great pasture land, with thick forests along the rivers. Some cattle feed in these pastures, but thousands more could easily find food here. Perhaps sometime there may be modern slaughterhouses and meat-packing plants here, and frozen and chilled meat will be shipped in large quantities from Colombia to foreign countries as it is today from Argentina and Uruguay.

SOUTH AMERICA 

The rivers in the southern part of eastern Colombia belong to the Amazon system, and their plains are covered with a dense tropical forest like that of the selvas. During the rainy season the rivers overflow their banks and flood much of the land, making travel, except in boats, impossible. The heat, the heavy rainfall, and the insects make this southeastern part of Colombia unhealthful. It has been only partly explored, is entirely undeveloped, and is practically unoccupied.

Besides these southeastern forests in Colombia, there are deep forests containing useful products in other parts of the country. As is often the case in tropical forests, many of the trees here are valuable hardwoods. In a few places, near rivers down which logs can be floated, some cedar and mahogany lumber is being obtained; yet the great lumber resources of the country, as well as most of the other forest products, are as yet scarcely touched. 

Many of the trees and plants yield materials which are useful in making paint and varnish, medicines, oils, tannin extracts, and dyes. Tagua trees grow here, and many tagua nuts are sent to the United States.

Cinchona trees are also found in large numbers. A half century or more ago Colombia led the world in the production of cinchona bark; but today, as you know, the product of trees growing wild in the forests is much less than that from the well-kept plantations in the East Indies. What medicine is made from cinchona bark?

One of the most important forest products at present is rubber. Chicle, from which chewing gum is made,

COLOMBIA 

355 is another of the principal forest products of Colombia. 

Chewing-gum manufacturers in the United States buy every year millions of pounds of chicle. Most of our supply comes from Mexico and British Honduras, but some of it comes from Colombia.

Coconut palm trees grow everywhere in the hot parts of Colombia, and coconuts and bananas are two important foods of Colombian Negroes. Near every little hut are a few coconut trees and banana plants. It is an easy matter for a native to gather some ripe coconuts or a bunch of bananas or plantains. A plantain is a kind of banana. It is never eaten raw, however, but is cooked in different ways and serves the people in place of potatoes.

Do you remember the coffee plantations in Brazil, and the millions of glossy green trees and their cherrylike berries? We can see similar plantations in Colombia, for coffee is the most important product of this country. The industry has increased in recent years, until today the coffee exports make up more than half the value of Colombia's export trade. Colombia ranks next to Brazil in coffee production. On the slopes leading from the rivers up to the high plateau, where the climate is cooler than it is on the lowlands, there are many coffee plantations. Some of these contain thousands of trees and have hundreds of laborers, but much of the coffee in Colombia is raised in a smaller way on farms owned and worked by the planter and his family. In Chapter VII you saw some pictures showing how coffee is raised on a large scale. In Fig. 149 a small grower is gathering coffee berries from a few trees which he has planted near his house.

SOUTH AMERICA 

FIG. 149. There are large coffee plantations in Colombia, but many farmers have a few coffee trees on their land the berries from which yield a small income. (Courtesy of National Federation of Coffee Growers of Colombia)

Other important industries are carried on in the hotter parts of Colombia. On the coastal lowlands and the plains along the rivers we should find cacao plantations much like those which we saw in Brazil and Ecuador. There are cotton fields too, which, with their Negro workers, would remind us of those in our

COLOMBIA 

357 Southern states. The people are beginning to see the importance of using for manufacturing purposes some of the things that they produce, and in recent years cotton mills have been built in several of the larger cities. 

Sugar cane grows well on the lowlands, and on the slopes up to a height of four or five thousand feet. 

Years ago all the sugar produced in the country was used by the people there, but so much cane is now raised that considerable quantities of sugar are exported. There are some large plantations with railroads and cars to carry the cane to the mill, and with modern machinery to prepare the ground, grind the cane, extract the juice, and manufacture the sugar. 

There are also many small farms where Negroes cultivate their small patches of cane and make their coarse brown sugar by hand.

There is an industry carried on around a seaport on the Caribbean coast of Colombia in which you will be much interested, for you probably often eat some of the product grown there. The seaport is Santa Marta, and the product is bananas. Enormous amounts of this fruit are produced on plantations in Central 

America, Mexico, the West Indies, and tropical South

America. A great fruit company in the United States has been the chief agency in starting and maintaining many of these plantations, thus making the banana a common world food. This company owns a large fleet of steamers built for use in tropical waters. Each year these ships bring us millions of bunches of bananas. Some of the fruit comes from company-owned plantations and some from the plantations of independent planters, who ship their bananas on the company's steamers.

SOUTH AMERICA 

You can always have good bananas to eat because there are many large plantations producing them, railroads connecting the plantations with the shipping ports, and refrigerator ships to carry the fruit to market. These three things have been made possible largely by the fruit company mentioned. On hot, unhealthful lowlands this great company has ditched and drained swamp land and cleared away the jungle forests. It has destroyed the breeding places of mosquitoes, provided a pure water supply, and thus greatly reduced sickness. It has constructed bridges, installed telegraph and telephone lines, and built electric-light plants, ice plants, laundries, bakeries, schools, hospitals, churches, and radio stations. Under its direction millions of banana plants have been set out and cared for, railroads built from the plantations to the seaports, and thousands of laborers given steady work, good pay, and comfortable homes. Fig. 150 shows you a small part of a banana plantation. 

On the larger plantations railroad tracks have been laid, and little cars drawn by mules or burros carry the fruit to the railroad line leading to the port. Where the plantations are smaller and the distances shorter, the bunches of bananas are carried in oxcarts, as shown in Fig. 151, or on the backs of animals or men. 

Before a steamer arrives at a banana port, word is sent to each plantation in the vicinity, telling the time of its arrival, the number of bunches each plantation is to furnish, and the destination of the fruit. Bananas bound for far-distant cities must be picked when they are somewhat greener than those destined for nearer places,

FIG. 150. Bananas are harvested when they are green. The laborers use their long knives carefully, so that they will not harm the fruit; for if a single banana is crushed the entire bunch will spoil. Notice that the bananas grow upward on the plants. (Courtesy of United Fruit Company)

SOUTH AMERICA 

There must be no delay between the picking of the fruit and the loading of the ship. At the seaport the loaded cars are run onto the wharf near the ships. 

Laborers place the bunches one by one in the canvas pockets of a conveyor which carries them to the hold 8:358

N°358 FIG. 151. No time is lost after the bananas have been harvested. On this plantation the bunches of fruit are immediately loaded on oxcarts and taken to the railroad ïine close by. (Courtesy of United Fruit Company) of the ship. As the fruit passes from the cars to the loading machines it is carefully inspected, and each bunch that is damaged, or too ripe, or otherwise defective is thrown out. The loading continues day and night until it is finished. Then the machinery is removed, the hatches are closed, and the cooling of the cargo is begun. The temperature is lowered to about 

57° F., the desired point, and kept there throughout the entire voyage. During the winter it is often necessary

COLOMBIA 

361 to warm the compartments where the fruit is stored before the vessel arrives at a cold northern port.

The banana plant is the largest plant that grows which has no woody stem or trunk. What we call its trunk is made up of overlapping leaf stems. When 

FIG. 152. Here you see a cargo of bananas being unloaded at a dock in

New Orleans. Study the picture closely so that you can tell how the work is carried on. (Courtesy of United Fruit Company) fully grown, the leaves are from eight to twelve feet long and two or more feet wide. A banana plant bears only one bunch of fruit. When the fruit is harvested, the plant is cut down, and the next year's crop comes from plants which spring up from the old roots. In time the young shoots which are allowed to mature develop roots of their own.

Over eight million people live in this South American country of which you are reading. When we visit

SOUTH AMERICA 

the capital, Bogotá, we shall find there many people who can trace their descent from the finest families of ancient Spain. These people are cultured, and some of them have attended foreign universities and traveled a great deal. For the most part they make up the governing class, the politicians, the bankers, and the business men. They are usually wealthy or at least well-to-do, and live in attractive, well-furnished houses with modern comforts and conveniences. 

Many Negroes live on the hot coastal lowlands and in the river valleys of Colombia. They are the laborers in the streets, on the wharves, and on the coffee, sugar, cacao, and other plantations. They are usually poor, but good-natured and happy. Their wants are few. Food is cheap and plentiful, often to be had for the picking, and in this tropical climate little clothing is needed at any time.

As in the countries farther south, Indians live in the highlands. They are descendants of the tribes whom the Spanish explorers found here when they invaded the country. Very little that is modern enters into their lives. They work much as their ancestors did, raising a few crops for food and weaving by hand the woolen cloth for their clothes. Some of them work on the highland farms, tend flocks or herds, or serve as carriers on the mountain trails.

The bulk of the population of Colombia is made up of mulattoes and mestizos; that is, mixtures of the white and Negro races and the white and Indian races. 

In the towns and cities these people are the shopkeepers, clerks, and workers in various trades. In the country regions they own little farms or work as over-

COLOMBIA 

363 seers and laborers on large plantations. These mixed races probably make up two thirds or three fourths of the total population.

CHAPTER XXVI  

Now we are ready for our trip through Colombia. 

Where shall we go first? Colombia, you remember, has two front doors opening on the water,- one on the 

Pacific Ocean and the other on the Caribbean Sea.

As we have been sailing along the western shores of South America, it would be natural, perhaps, for us to enter Colombia from that side. There is an important and growing seaport on the Pacific coast by the name of Buenaventura. Here we could take a train and, by a combination of railroad and highway travel, reach the capital, Bogotá, in from two to three days. Find the port and the capital on the map opposite page 336. 

This trip would give us some marvelous views, for the railroads climb from the low coastal plain up over mountain passes to the plateau and then down to the valley of the Magdalena River. We should cross this river by boat, and another railroad would take us again up steep slopes to the plateau on which Bogotá stands, some eight thousand feet above the level of the Pacific port from which we started. On the way we should pass sugar and cacao plantations on the lowlands, coffee plantations on the hillsides, and wheat and vegetable farms on the plateau. Between the settled regions are miles of unpeopled wilderness.

In taking this route, however, we should miss the 365

SOUTH AMERICA 

older, more interesting, and more important cities on the Caribbean shores of Colombia. We should miss also the trip through the Panama Canal. So we will go on through the Canal into the Caribbean Sea. 

Our first stop on the northern shore of Colombia is at Cartagena, one of the oldest and most famous ports on the Caribbean. As we enter its harbor we see on either side gray old forts which the Spaniards built hundreds of years ago. Down by the water are also great moss-grown buildings as old as the forts. Behind these, rising above the low white buildings of the city, are the towers and steeples of ancient churches standing out against a background of hills and cliffs. 

Underground passages connect the town with the forts on the hills and on the shore. It gives one a creepy feeling to walk through one of these dark, damp passages. Voices have a hollow sound, and the echoes make it seem as if the tunnel were still peopled with Spanish soldiers. We can imagine these stern warriors leading their prisoners through the passages, lighted only by flaring torches, to dark dungeons under the forts.

It was in Cartagena, you remember, that the Spaniards used to store much of the gold brought them by their Indian slaves. Here the gold awaited the vessels which would carry it across the ocean to Spain. The chief reason for the building of the forts and strongholds was to protect the treasure stored here. Fig. 147, on page 351, shows you a part of the great wall that once surrounded the city.

Besides the relics left by the Spanish settlers in Cartagena, there are other things in the city which

FIG. 153. Buenaventura is an important South American port. One of its leading exports is coffee. Here you see bags of coffee beans being loaded on an ocean freighter. (Courtesy of National Federation of Coffee 

Growers of Colombia)

SOUTH AMERICA 

they never saw and of which they never even dreamed. 

Among these are the long wharves where ocean vessels dock to take on the products from plantations farther inland; the electric lights which illuminate the city; FIG. 154. How many ways of transporting goods and people can you find in this street in Cartagena? (Courtesy of Grace Line) the railroad which connects Cartagena with the Magdalena River, more than sixty miles away; and the noisy trucks and the automobiles in the streets. 

We shall find it better to begin our journey into the interior of Colombia at Puerto Colombia, which is a little east of Cartagena. Find this port on the map. 

Our steamer draws up beside a long dock which has

been built nearly a mile out into the deep waters of the Caribbean. What is said about this dock under the picture on this page? Ships from several nations tie up here. From some of them float the Stars and Stripes ; for, you know, our country is Colombia's best customer. FIG. 155. This large modern dock at Puerto Colombia is said to be the longest dock in the world. (Courtesy of Grace Line)

Puerto Colombia is neither a large city nor an attractive one. Most of the people who live here are engaged in some work connected with the traffic of the port and of the railroad which runs to Barranquilla, some twenty miles away. Barranquilla, a few miles up the Magdalena River, is the chief port of Colombia. 

The Magdalena River is Colombia's great highway.

Unfortunately it is not so deep at its mouth as it is a little farther on. Sandbars block its mouth, and, in addition, the river brings down from the mountains

SOUTH AMERICA 

great quantities of silt which the currents in the Caribbean are not strong enough to wash away and which have therefore formed a delta with many small streams flowing through it. Until recently the shallow waters prevented ocean steamers from entering the river. They stopped at Puerto Colombia, and passengers and freight were carried by train to Baranquilla, which became an important transfer station for rail and river traffic. 

The lower course of the Magdalena has now been deepened so that ocean steamers can go up the river as far as Barranquilla, but the city continues to be more important as a river port than as a seaport. Its busiest part is along the water front, where the cargoes brought down the river from inland regions are transferred to the train bound for Puerto Colombia. Oil barges tied up at the wharves, and large tanks where petroleum is stored, show the importance of this product. 

The two crops which have helped more than any others in the growth of Barranquilla are cotton and coffee. Cotton is the most important crop raised on the lowlands around the city, and some of it is used in manufacturing there. Coffee, you remember, is the leading crop of Colombia and its most valuable export. Coffeeshipping time is the busiest part of the year in Barranquilla; for much of this product finds its way from the higher lands farther inland, where it is raised, to the ocean and thence to foreign countries by way of the Magdalena River.

Compared with many countries, Colombia is little known. It would be still less known, however, were it not for the Magdalena. For centuries this river has been the only highway leading into the heart of the

country. No railroad runs through its valley. Indeed, there are but few railroads in Colombia. There are several short lines which connect the seaports with towns not far inland, and a few others which extend from the rivers to Bogotá and other cities, but as yet J. 

FIG. 156. Barranquilla is the distributing port for most of the region which the Magdalena River serves as a highway. The large boat at the left carries passengers and freight far up the river. The boat is much like those used on the Mississippi. (Courtesy of Grace Line) there are no continuous routes through the country. 

To reach many interesting places, one must travel either by river or on roads so narrow and trails so steep that one feels safe only on a sure-footed mule. 

The source of the Magdalena is a thousand miles southward from its mouth, nearly under the equator. 

The river is navigable for more than six hundred miles, but the journey is long and tiresome. The climate is hot, and the mosquitoes and other insect pests are

SOUTH AMERICA 

annoying. On both sides of the river the wide valley stretches for miles. Parts of it are low and swampy, and parts are covered with grass where cattle feed. 

Farther upstream are jungles and forests. A few villages like the one shown in Fig. 157 lie on the banks, and the people who live in them come down to the wharf to see the boats come in or to help unload the boxes of cloth, bags of grain and other foods, and iron tools and machinery which the people of a country where little manufacturing is done need to import. 

The women bring articles to sell to the passengers. 

Some of them carry baskets of such fruits as pineapples, bananas, oranges, and coconuts. Others have hats, hammocks, and baskets which they have woven from plant fibers. On the wharves, besides the goods which the south-bound steamer has left, there are bags of coffee, sugar, and cacao beans, and bundles of hides. 

Boats coming down the river will carry these to Barranquilla for export.

Many of the houses in these river towns are little huts made of bamboo daubed over with mud, which hardens quickly in the hot sun. Tied to the wharves or drawn up on the shore are rough boats and dugouts. 

During the rainy season, when much of the river plain is flooded, the people go about in boats, and the cattle are driven to the higher pastures. When the water has lowered again, the whole valley has received a coating of mud. Thus the soil grows richer year by year. 

After the flood subsides, the Magdalena may perhaps plow its way through the softened earth in a new channel, and villages which only a short time before stood on the banks may be some distance away. This shift-

FIG. 157. The people of this little village on the bank of the Magdalena have gathered to await the arrival of a boat from Barranquilla. (Courtesy of National Federation of Coffee Growers of Colombia)

SOUTH AMERICA 

ing of the course of the river makes it difficult for pilots to find the channel. Sometimes it is near one bank and sometimes near the other. Navigation is often hindered, and trips upstream may take two weeks or more, the length of time depending on the condition of the river.

Besides the steamers which run up and down the Magdalena, there are smaller boats which make shorter trips from town to town. These are partly covered with roofs of bamboo and thatch for the protection of freight and passengers against sun and rain. The Negroes push these boats upstream with long poles and haul them over the rapids with ropes. In the old days, before steamers sailed on the Magdalena, all the traffic was carried on in just such boats as these, but now they are used chiefly on the smaller branches of the river and in its upper course, far from the Caribbean Sea. 

Farther south the river becomes narrower, and the forests grow nearer the banks. The trees, shrubs, and vines are massed so thick as to make an impassable wall. Tropical birds of brilliant plumage flit about in the trees, and monkeys chatter in the boughs. Everywhere twining plants and lovely flowers cover the trunks, and long vines, sometimes called monkey ladders, hang down from the branches and take root in the ground. Alligators sun themselves on the banks and wait lazily for their food to find its way into their open mouths.

Most of the time since we left the Caribbean Sea there have been palm trees of many varieties in sight. 

Some are short and some are tall, some are heavy with coconuts, and there are some from which the natives

obtain wax to make matches. There are no other trees in the world which furnish so many millions of people with the necessities of life as do the trees of the palm family. From the palm, people of tropical lands get wood for their houses, leaves for their thatched roofs, food to eat, and refreshing drink.

About two hundred miles up the Magdalena is the mouth of its largest branch, the Cauca River. In the upper part of the valley of this river, where the land is higher, the climate is delightful, never too hot or too cold. The soil is very rich, and the people who live there have large plantations where they raise quantities of cacao, coffee, fruit, sugar, cotton, wheat, corn, and other products of the subtropical and temperate zones. 

More than five hundred miles up the Magdalena, passengers have to leave the boat for a railroad trip around some rapids in the river and then transfer to another steamer to continue their way upstream. If they are bound for Bogotá, they must change again at a town called Girardot, between six and seven hundred miles from the mouth of the river. Here they take a train that runs from the low river valley up to the plateau on which the capital is situated. The entire trip to Bogotá by the river route has taken perhaps two weeks or even more. Just think how it would interfere with business in our country if the trip from Chicago to our capital city of Washington took as long as this!

We could have gone from Barranquilla to Girardot by airplane in fewer hours than the river trip took days. 

Airplanes have been used here since 1921, and the line is said to be one of the safest and best-paying in the world.

SOUTH AMERICA 

When yet some distance away from the capital, we see the mountains by which it is surrounded, and later the city itself comes into view. Bogotá lies in the valley of a mountain river the waters of which we see FIG. 158. This is the airport at Girardot, on the Magdalena River. A hydroplane may be seen in the foreground of the picture. (Photograph from R. I. Nesmith and Associates) later flowing through the gutters of the streets. It is a Spanish city. Its houses are of the Spanish type, the people speak Spanish, and many families are of pure Spanish blood. In some ways the city has changed but little since the old Spanish days. Most of the houses stand close to the narrow streets and form a solid wall on both sides. The walls are broken by heavy doors and a few windows on the first floor. On the second

floor most of the windows have small iron balconies. 

These serve the double purpose of screening the women of the house while they watch the street scenes and protecting the narrow sidewalks below from the sun and rain.

As in most Spanish cities, the houses are built around a patio, often containing a fountain and flowers and trees. In parts of the city a family of the better class lives on the second floor, and the lower floor is occupied by a poorer family or a store.

The bareness of the narrow streets is broken by plazas, many of which are ornamented with gardens, fountains, and statues. The principal plaza is named for Simon Bolivar, and in the center stands a fine statue of this Spanish hero. On one side of the plaza is the capitol, a fine granite building. On other sides are the ancient cathedral and the old palace where the rulers lived who were appointed by the king of Spain in the days when Colombia was a colony of that country. Like several other old buildings, the palace today is occupied by shops and offices. 

Although many parts of Bogotá are still much as they were in colonial days, other sections are very different. When we first left the train which brought us to the city, we noticed the fine railroad station and some large public buildings. Modern homes have been built in an attractive suburb. New streets have been laid out and others widened and paved, and an increasing number of automobiles are being imported to run on them. On the old, narrow streets it is wiser to walk than to ride over the rough cobblestones. 

A number of large cotton and woolen mills have been

SOUTH AMERICA 

built in the city, and here the products of plain and plateau are woven into cloth. There are also mills where some of the wheat raised on the Colombian Plateau is ground into flour. For centuries the people have depended on foreign countries for all manufactured goods. The modern mills, with their noisy machinery, must seem strange to the older inhabitants of the city.

There are many large coffee plantations not far from Bogotá. Perhaps the busiest part of the year in the city is the time when the coffee is ready for shipping. 

It is sent by rail from the capital down to Girardot and thence by the Magdalena River to Barranquilla. It then goes by rail to Puerto Colombia, where it is loaded on ocean ships bound for the United States and other countries.

Bogotá is sometimes called the Athens of South 

America. Do you know what this means? There are many educational institutions in the city, including a university and several colleges, some of which are several hundred years old. The people pride themselves on their education, culture, and fine manners. 

One writer says that the great changes and the many improvements which have come to Bogotá in recent years, as well as the development which has taken place in other parts of the country, have been due chiefly to three things. These are the two mineral resources, platinum and emeralds, and the airplane line up the Magdalena River, which has shortened distances in Colombia and brought the capital city much nearer to the coast and in easier communication with other countries.

FIG. 159. Some of the streets of Bogotá are very modern in appearance. 

The fine building at the left is the Government House, and the large building at the head of the street is a hotel. (Courtesy of National Federation of Coffee Growers of Colombia)

You read on page 350 that Colombia produces nearly all the world's emeralds. Bogotá is the emerald city.

Most of the trade in the gems is carried on there, and most of the emerald mines are less than a hundred miles away, although some of the richest of them can be reached only by a two or three days' journey on muleback over rough mountain trails.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Emeralds, like diamonds, are usually found in the craters of old volcanoes. Using steel bars and shovels, Indian laborers tear down the earth and stone and break up the loosened mass into small pieces. These are washed by water led down through a sluiceway. 

The water is drawn off and the sediment is searched for the precious stones. Emerald-mining is a government monopoly. The mines are heavily guarded by military police, and the miners are constantly watched and searched for hidden gems.

The platinum is found a long distance from Bogotá, in the river valleys near the Pacific coast, but the development of the industry and the high price of platinum have brought much money to the capital city. 

Deposits of platinum are often found with deposits of gold, but for centuries the miners in the gold region of Colombia considered the platinum a waste product of no more value than the earth and rock in which the gold was found. Later, when its value became known, considerable quantities were obtained in the town where much of the waste material had been thrown. Streets were dug up, and property owners searched their fields for platinum. One man even went so far as to tear down his house in order to dig in the earth which had been dumped there before the building was put up. 

It is said that he received enough money for the platinum he found to build a better home and have several thousand dollars left.

DEBATES 

Put the following subjects into the proper form for debating. Divide the class into affirmative and negative sides. After deciding on the subject for the first debate and the side of the question on which you will argue, plan the studying which you will have to do before you can offer any points of value. 

1. The advantages and disadvantages of the position of 

South America. 

2. The comparative advantages of the United States and 

Argentina as wheat-producing countries. 

3. The relative advantages of Europe and South America as the greater future market for our products. 

4. The surface features of South America as a help or a hindrance in the development of the continent.
